# Commonly repeated ideas

Compiled 2026-08-16. Every claim carries the date its quote was true; renting law is amended and the regulator rewrites its guidance, both without announcing it.

184 claims across 29 subjects are established as REPETITIONS: a named publication said it, and the quote proves that it said it. No primary source travelled with the quote.

**Two things this is not.** It is not a list of falsehoods: some of it is probably true. And it is not proof that no primary source exists. These claims were gathered from a bounded set of documents, so a missing source can mean the idea has none, or only that the document carrying it was never read.

**Each idea is being checked on its own**, by searching for the source rather than noting its absence. So far: 23 contradicted by a primary source, 79 backed by one, 15 searched with nothing found, 28 where a real source says something narrower than the retelling. Every entry below carries its result and what was searched; the unchecked ones say so.

Cite an entry by its RESULT. A contradicted idea can be answered flatly, with the quote. An unchecked one is something the field repeats, and nothing more.

## Contradicted by a primary source

The strongest entries here. For each, the regulator or the Act addresses the idea directly and says the opposite.

- **A cooling failure only counts as an urgent repair in Victoria between December and March.**
  - "a failure or breakdown of any cooling appliance or cooling service provided by a residential rental provider, rooming house operator, caravan park owner or caravan owner" - Victorian Government (authorised version, Chief Parliamentary Counsel) https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf
  - Paragraph (ia) of the s 3(1) definition carries no seasonal condition, and neither does CAV's public list, which reads 'a cooling appliance or service provided by the rental provider is not working'. A full-text search of the Act and of the Residential Tenancies Regulations 2021 turns up no December-to-March window anywhere. The likeliest origin of the error is the separate cooling rental minimum standard in Schedule 4 clause 16 of the Regulations, which commences '1 March 2027' - a commencement date, not a season during which cooling failures become urgent. Note that a breach of the minimum standards is itself an urgent repair year-round under paragraph (ib), which is the opposite of a seasonal narrowing.
- **A person living in a Victorian rental property must pay all the utility charges themselves, and those charges include the water rates.**
  - "The rental provider must pay all other charges related to water supply and sewerage or drainage services." - Consumer Affairs Victoria https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/rent-bond-bills-and-condition-reports/paying-for-utilities-phone-and-internet
  - Flatly contradicted. The renter does NOT pay all the charges, and specifically does not pay the water rates. Residential Tenancies Act 1997 s 53(1)(ab) makes the rental provider liable for 'all rates, taxes or charges payable under any Act other than charges payable by the renter under this Division', and s 53(1)(c) for 'all charges arising from a water supply service to separately metered rented premises that are not based on the amount of water supplied to the premises' - i.e. exactly the water rates Soho says the renter pays. The renter's water liability under s 52(c) is confined to volumetric usage plus sewage disposal charges. This claim and researchId 928988e6d0b0 are two mutually exclusive sentences in the same Soho article; the true position is neither of them.
- **A rental applicant in Victoria needs to supply identification adding up to around 100 points, and most agents ask for it.**
  - "A rental provider or their agent can request no more than two identity documents from this list:" - Consumer Affairs Victoria https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/starting-and-changing-rental-agreements/applying-signing-and-moving-in/applying-for-a-property
  - The regulator caps identity documents at two, not at a points total. The same words appear in the prescribed form itself - Schedule 1 Form 3A item 7 of the Residential Tenancies Regulations 2021 reads 'A rental provider or their agent can request no more than two identity documents from the following list—' - and the form is compulsory: s 30AC of the Act says a provider or agent 'must not prepare or authorise the preparation of an application form used to apply to enter into a residential rental agreement in a form that is not in the prescribed form', with a penalty of 60 penalty units (300 for a body corporate). Regulation 15(2) then makes 'any information other than the information set out in Form 3A in Schedule 1' prohibited information under s 30C, and CAV lists 'any questions that are not on the new rental application form' among questions renters cannot be asked. The regime took effect 31 March 2026. A 100-point request is therefore not merely unnecessary in Victoria, it is a request the agent is forbidden to make. The 100-point concept is the Commonwealth financial-sector customer identification procedure (the FTR Act 100 point check used for opening bank accounts) imported wholesale into rental advice; it has never had a basis in Victorian tenancy law, and neither the Act nor the Regulations contains the phrase. Worth noting how this one fails: the Urban Renters page carries a modified date of 8 July 2026 - more than three months after the two-document cap commenced - never mentions Victoria or any jurisdiction anywhere in its text, and cites no source at all. It is generic national-market advice being read as Victorian rules.
- **A Victorian renter may let other people stay in the rented premises short-term and charge them a fee without the rental provider's consent, because a short-stay arrangement of that kind is a licence rather than a sub-lease.**
  - "The tenants had, by means of the AirBnB agreement, effectively and practically passed their right of exclusive occupation, with all its qualities, to their AirBnB guests for the agreed period under the AirBnB agreement." - Victorian Civil and Administrative Tribunal https://www.vcat.vic.gov.au/media/195/download
  - Read in isolation the sentence is a tautology - of course you may do a thing so long as it is not the prohibited thing - but LegalVision offers it as the practical pay-off of the VCAT ruling, i.e. that paying short-stay guests are not sub-renters. That premise is exactly what the Supreme Court reversed, and the backing quote is VCAT's own summary of why: the short duration of the stays did not matter, because the quality of the occupancy 'was not akin to that of a "lodger" or an hotel guest' but 'the possession that would be expected of residential accommodation generally' (VCAT's summary of [46]). The Court also held that the tenants' ability to evict an overstaying guest and their retention of the flat as their principal residence were both irrelevant to exclusive possession - the two facts a renter would most naturally rely on for comfort. CAV's definition of a sub-let describes the same arrangement in terms: 'When a property is sub-let, someone rents part or all of the property from another person who rented it directly from the rental provider'. One live qualification is worth recording rather than flattening: exclusive possession remains the legal test, so a genuinely shared arrangement (a renter staying put while a guest has run of a room but not the premises) can still be a licence. The error is presenting whole-of-premises short-stay letting as safe.
- **An $800,000 Melbourne house can be reconfigured as a five-room rooming house to generate $1,250 to $1,600 per week.**
  - "This dataset documents 345 anonymised residential investment property acquisitions facilitated by PremiumRea, a Melbourne-based independent buyer" - Zenodo / PremiumRea Pty Ltd (dataset curator Joey Don) https://zenodo.org/records/20095886
  - CONTRADICTED BY THE AUTHOR'S OWN DATA. PremiumRea states that the article's statistics are drawn from this dataset, and the dataset is genuinely primary - it is PremiumRea's own settled-transaction record, per-property, released under CC-BY 4.0 with a DOI. It contains 48 rooming house conversions (reno_type = 'rooming'). Not one of them reaches $1,250 per week. The range across all 48 is $1,050 to $1,240, median $1,150 - the maximum sits $10 below the bottom of the claimed band, and zero of 48 rows fall inside $1,250-$1,600. Narrowing to the article's own scenario makes it worse: the 11 rooming conversions with a purchase price between $780,000 and $820,000 return $1,050 to $1,210 per week, median about $1,130, i.e. every one of them lands below the claimed floor. Their gross yields run 6.05% to 7.21%, not the 'highest-yield strategy' figures implied. Across all 345 rows the highest weekly rent of any kind is $2,000 and only 11 rows anywhere exceed $1,250 - none of them rooming conversions. Two further problems with the citation chain: the article's reference [12], REIV 'Specialised Investment Property Yields - Rooming Houses by LGA' (2024), could not be located on REIV's own site, and reference [11] is 'PremiumRea portfolio data' - the same self-published dataset that contradicts the claim. The $1,250-$1,600 band appears to be a forward projection presented as 'real numbers', not a measurement.
- **Because VCAT decided in Swan v Uecker that letting a Victorian rental property to Airbnb guests created only a licence and not a sub-lease, a rental provider cannot rely on the Residential Tenancies Act 1997 provisions that prohibit sub-letting without consent when trying to end the tenancy of a renter who lists the property on Airbnb.**
  - "The AirBnB agreement for occupation of the whole of the apartment was properly to be characterised as a lease between the tenants and the AirBnB guests for the period of occupation agreed between them. It followed that the tenants had entered into a sub-lease in breach of their agreement with the landlord." - Victorian Civil and Administrative Tribunal https://www.vcat.vic.gov.au/media/195/download
  - The retelling rests on a ruling that was reversed five weeks after LegalVision published, and the tribunal itself now teaches the opposite. LegalVision's article is dated 2 May 2016 and describes VCAT's decision of 24 March 2016 in Swan v Uecker (VCAT proceeding R2016/4286). On 10 June 2016 Croft J in the Supreme Court of Victoria granted leave, allowed the appeal and disposed of the matter himself, holding at [75]: 'For the preceding reasons, I am of the opinion that the AirBnB Agreement for occupation of the whole of the Apartment is properly to be characterised as a lease between the Respondents, the tenants, and the AirBnB guests for the period of occupation agreed between them. It follows that their entering into this Agreement is, having regard to their own tenancy of the Apartment, a sub-lease.' The backing quote is VCAT's own summary of that holding, published by the tribunal in 'Useful Supreme Court and VCAT decisions about renting', a compendium whose preamble says 'VCAT is bound to follow decisions of the Supreme Court that are directly on point' - so the tribunal that produced the decision LegalVision relies on now publishes the appellate reversal as the governing law. I could not cite the judgment itself because no host serving it answers an automated fetch: AustLII and CommonLII return 403, the Wayback snapshot 503s and is refused by WebFetch, Jade and CaseMine serve JavaScript shells, and the Supreme Court's own site does not host 2016 judgments. The provisions LegalVision says landlords 'cannot rely on' were renumbered, not repealed, by the Residential Tenancies Amendment Act 2018: s 253 (the section in issue in Swan v Uecker, and the one the VCAT compendium still cites) is now s 91ZV, which lets a rental provider give a notice to vacate where a renter 'has assigned or sub-let or purported to assign or sub-let the whole or any part of the premises without the residential rental provider's consent', and s 81(1) still requires written consent. Ten years on, the LegalVision page carries no correction.
- **In Victoria a prospective renter can still volunteer more than the advertised rent - only the agent asking for it is banned.**
  - "uninvited offer of an amount of rent that is higher than the advertised amount of rent for the rented premises." - Victorian Government (authorised version, Chief Parliamentary Counsel) https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf
  - Section 30F(4) closes exactly the loophole Soho describes: a provider or agent 'must not accept an unsolicited or otherwise uninvited offer' of rent above the advertised amount, penalty 60 penalty units (300 for a body corporate). The subsection was inserted by the Consumer and Planning Legislation Amendment (Housing Statement Reform) Act 2025 (No. 6/2025) s 7, and the Act's own endnote records the commencement as 'ss 3-51 on 25.11.25'. So the claim was true in Victoria for a defined window - 29 March 2021 to 24 November 2025 - and has been false since. Soho's page carries a dateModified of 16 March 2026, four months after the change. Two precision points the reteller misses in both directions: the prohibition binds the provider and the agent, not the renter (a renter who offers more commits no offence, they simply cannot lawfully have it accepted), and the same amending Act inserted s 40(3), which bars accepting an unsolicited offer of more than one month's rent in advance - the other half of the same manoeuvre. Lumping Victoria in with NSW is what carries the error: the two states diverged on 25 November 2025.
- **In Victoria a rental provider must give at least 60 days' notice of a rent increase, that 60 days being a legal minimum rather than a target.**
  - "From 25 November 2025, the minimum notice period for rental providers to issue a rent increase notice will change from 60 to 90 days." - Consumer Affairs Victoria https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/rent-bond-bills-and-condition-reports/rent/rent-increases
  - Contradicted on the number, correct on the structure. The Act now reads 90 days: s 44(1) of the authorised Residential Tenancies Act 1997 (version 113) says a residential rental provider must give a renter at least 90 days notice in the prescribed form of a proposed rent increase, the margin note recording the amendment as No. 6/2025 s. 11 (Consumer and Planning Legislation Amendment (Housing Statement Reform) Act 2025), effective 25 November 2025 (version 111). The pre-reform authorised text (v097, s 44(1)) said 60 days, which is where the industry figure comes from. Collings' page is dated 26 June 2026 - seven months after the change - and still states 60 days, so this is a live error rather than a stale-but-once-true page. The 'minimum, not a target' framing is itself sound: s 44(5) provides that 'A rent increase in contravention of this section is invalid.' The corpus already carries the correction from other retellings but never from a primary source (trace-020 3a5b75c609b9 Cubbi notes the 90-day change from November 2025; trace-020 d26b485a9194 Landlord Wise names s 11 of the 2025 amending Act) - so this is the field's error, not a corpus duplicate.
- **In Victoria a rental provider or agent must give written notice at least 24 hours before a routine inspection.**
  - "for a purpose set out in section 86(1)(d) or (f), if at least 7 days notice has been given to the renter in accordance with section 88." - Victorian Government (Chief Parliamentary Counsel), Residential Tenancies Act 1997, authorised version 113 https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf
  - The number is wrong by a factor of seven. A routine inspection is the s 86(1)(f) ground - 'entry is required to enable inspection of the premises and entry for that purpose has not been made within the last 6 months' - and s 85(b)(iv), quoted above, attaches 7 days notice to that ground, not 24 hours. Twenty-four hours is the s 85(b)(iii) period and it belongs to different grounds entirely: s 86(1)(c) (the provider carrying out a duty under the Act or the agreement), s 86(1)(e) (reasonable grounds to believe the renter has breached their duties) and s 86(1)(g) (inspection for family violence proceedings). Entry must also fall between 8 a.m. and 6 p.m. and not on a public holiday. The 'written' half of the claim is right - s 88 requires a notice of entry to 'be in writing', state why entry is sought, and be given by post or delivered personally between 8 a.m. and 6 p.m. The most likely explanation is staleness, not invention. Twenty-four hours WAS the routine-inspection period under the pre-reform Act; the 7-day period was substituted by the Residential Tenancies Amendment Act 2018 s 74(c), which the Act's own endnotes record as commencing on 29.3.21 (Special Gazette No. 42, 27.1.21). The page is repeating a rule that expired more than five years ago. That matters for renter duties specifically, because the shorter 24-hour notice still exists for s 86(1)(e) - entry on suspicion that the renter has breached their duties - so a provider quoting '24 hours for a routine inspection' is, in effect, using a breach-of-duty notice period for a no-suspicion inspection.
- **In Victoria a residential rental provider must give the renter at least 60 days' written notice before a rent increase takes effect.**
  - "A residential rental provider must give a renter at least 90 days notice in the prescribed form of a proposed rent increase." - Victorian Government - Chief Parliamentary Counsel (authorised version, Residential Tenancies Act 1997) https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf
  - The number is wrong and has been for nine months. Section 44(1) of the authorised Act (v113, p.97) reads 90 days, its margin note recording the amendment as 'S. 44(1) amended by Nos 45/2002 s. 12(1)(a), 45/2018 s. 34(2), 6/2025 s. 11', effective 25 November 2025. Consumer Affairs Victoria says the same in plain words on its rent increases page: 'From 25 November 2025, the minimum notice period for rental providers to issue a rent increase notice will change from 60 to 90 days.' Collings' page is written 'as it stands in 2026' and still says 60 days in five separate places, including a section arguing the 60-day period 'is a minimum, not a target' - so this is a live error, not a stale-but-once-true page. Forge is internally inconsistent in a single sentence: 'Landlords must provide 60 days' written notice using the approved Notice of Rent Increase form CAV says the minimum notice period changed from 60 to 90 days from 25 November 2025'. Cubbi's '60 days now, 90 from November 2025' was true when written and is now simply out of date. Collings' rider that failure invalidates the increase is correct - see the separate finding on s 44(5). Note also that this whole subject is filed as 'rent reduction' but every claim in the corpus for it concerns rent increases; there is no rent-reduction claim to trace.
- **In Victoria, a rental provider or agent normally needs to give only at least 24 hours written notice before entering for a routine (general) inspection.**
  - "for a purpose set out in section 86(1)(d) or (f), if at least 7 days notice has been given to the renter in accordance with section 88." - Victorian Government (Chief Parliamentary Counsel), Residential Tenancies Act 1997 version 113 https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf
  - The primary source says the opposite number. The routine inspection ground is s 86(1)(f) - 'entry is required to enable inspection of the premises and entry for that purpose has not been made within the last 6 months'. The notice period attached to that ground is set by s 85(b)(iv), quoted above: SEVEN days, not 24 hours. Twenty-four hours is the s 85(b)(iii) period, and it attaches to different grounds entirely - s 86(1)(c) (carrying out a duty under the Act or the agreement), (e) (reasonable grounds to believe the renter has breached their duties) and (g) (inspection for family violence proceedings under s 91V(1)). Consumer Affairs Victoria's own notice table says the same thing: 'General inspection (routine inspection)' = 7 days. Tenants Victoria says the same. So does the corpus. On the chain: aussierentlaws.com carries no pinpoint citation at all. Its only references are two bare links, '[1] Residential Tenancies Act 1997 (Vic) - Victorian legislation' and '[2] Consumer Affairs Victoria - renting and inspections', and it tells the reader to 'check the Residential Tenancies Act and Consumer Affairs Victoria guidance for details' - while stating a figure that both of those sources contradict. The most likely explanation is staleness rather than invention: 24 hours WAS the general-inspection notice period under the pre-reform Act, and the 7-day period was introduced by the Residential Tenancies Amendment Act 2018 (No. 45/2018) s 74(c), commencing 29 March 2021. The page is repeating a rule that expired five years ago. The hedge word 'normally' does no work - there is no circumstance in which 24 hours suffices for a routine inspection.
- **In Victoria, a renter or their visitor interfering with a smoke alarm lets the rental provider give the renter a notice to vacate.**
  - "If a renter, resident or their visitor does this, it is a breach of duty and a rental provider or rooming house operator may give the renter or resident a" - Consumer Affairs Victoria https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/repairs-alterations-safety-and-pets/keeping-the-property-safe/smoke-alarms-and-fire-safety
  - The first half is right and the second half names the wrong instrument. Section 63A(1) of the Act provides that 'A renter must not remove, deactivate or otherwise interfere with the operation of a prescribed safety device at rented premises unless it is reasonable in the circumstances to do so', and reg 25 of the Residential Tenancies Regulations 2021 prescribes 'a smoke alarm' first on the list of those devices. Section 63A carries the standard note that it is a duty provision, so a contravention is a breach of duty. But the remedy is a breach of duty notice under s 208, not a notice to vacate. CAV's page says so on exactly this fact pattern - the quoted sentence continues into a link to the 'Notice of breach of duty' form, which is why the quote stops where it does. Getting to a notice to vacate takes more. Section 91ZP allows one without a prior breach of duty notice only where 'on 2 previous occasions the renter has been in breach of the same provision' and a breach of duty notice was given each time; short of that, the provider must serve the breach of duty notice first and wait for non-compliance. The only other route is s 91ZJ 'Danger', which is available where the renter or visitor endangers 'occupiers of neighbouring premises', the provider, the agent, or their contractor or employee - not the renter's own safety, which is what a disabled smoke alarm in a standalone dwelling actually endangers. So a first instance of tampering does not support a notice to vacate, and 1st Call Electrical states the escalated remedy as if it were the immediate one.
- **In Victoria, non-document goods worth under $1,000 may be disposed of after 14 days' notice, while higher-value goods attract longer notice requirements.**
  - "An owner of premises may sell or dispose of stored goods if the former renter or other person who has a lawful right to the stored goods has not reclaimed them within 14 days, unless the owner of premises has agreed or been ordered by the Tribunal to store the goods for longer than 14 days." - Victorian Government (Chief Parliamentary Counsel), Residential Tenancies Act 1997, authorised version 113 https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf
  - The $1,000 threshold does not exist. There is no monetary figure anywhere in Part 9 of the Act, and the single regulation made for Part 9 (reg 93) prescribes categories of object, not values. What the Act actually does is split goods three ways, and value only matters at one extreme. Under s 384(1) an owner may remove and destroy or dispose of goods immediately - no notice, no storage - if they 'are of no monetary value', 'are perishable foodstuffs' or 'are dangerous'. Reg 93 then carves back out of that even for worthless goods: 'labelled containers or labelled urns containing human remains', 'specialised medical devices, equipment and goods including prostheses and prescription medication', and 'medals and trophies'. Everything else - a $50 kettle and a $50,000 piano alike - goes through s 386: notice in the form approved by the Director, then storage for at least 14 days, then disposal or sale under s 391(1). So the second limb is flatly contradicted: higher value does not buy longer notice. The only things that extend the 14 days are the owner's own agreement or a VCAT order, per the closing words of s 391(1) quoted above. The first limb is right about '14 days' but attaches it to a made-up qualifying condition, which is worse than being wrong outright - a reader with a $1,500 item would conclude the 14-day rule does not apply to them when it does.
- **Non-urgent repairs in Victoria fall under section 73A of the Residential Tenancies Act 1997.**
  - "75 Application to Tribunal for non-urgent repairs" - Victorian Government (authorised version, Chief Parliamentary Counsel) https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf
  - There is no section 73A in the Residential Tenancies Act 1997 - a full-text search of the current authorised version (v113) returns no such provision. Section 73 is 'Application to Tribunal for urgent repairs', i.e. the urgent path, not the non-urgent one. The non-urgent route is s 74 (application to the Director to investigate the need for non-urgent repairs) and s 75 (application to the Tribunal), sitting on top of the s 68 duty to maintain the premises in good repair. CAV's own page points readers to ss 72, 74 and 75 for exactly this reason. The invented section number is the tell: a plausible-looking '73A' bridging the real 73 and 74.
- **Rent increases during a fixed-term agreement used to be possible by negotiation before the Victorian rental reforms and are no longer possible at all.**
  - "The rental provider is not allowed to increase the rent during a fixed term agreement unless the rental agreement (lease) has an additional clause (term) allowing for an increase." - Consumer Affairs Victoria https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/rent-bond-bills-and-condition-reports/rent/rent-increases
  - The primary sources contradict this in both directions. Mid-fixed-term increases still exist: current s 44(4) (v113, p.98) permits an increase before the term ends where the agreement 'provides for a rent increase within the fixed term of a specified amount' or 'specifies the method by which a rent increase within the fixed term is to be calculated', which CAV restates in the quoted sentence. And the pre-reform position was not free negotiation either: the authorised version in force to 28 March 2021 (v097, s 44(4)) already said a landlord under a fixed term tenancy agreement must not increase the rent before the term ends unless the agreement provides for a rent increase within the fixed term. So the 2018 reforms tightened what such a clause must specify (amount, or method of calculation); they did not remove a 'flexibility to negotiate mid-lease' that the Act never granted. Collings dates the change to the 2018 reforms, which commenced 29 March 2021.
- **Rental Dispute Resolution Victoria is a specialist dispute service that was established under Victoria's November 2025 rental reforms.**
  - "RDRV launched in June 2025 as part of the response to" - Victorian Civil and Administrative Tribunal https://www.vcat.vic.gov.au/news/rental-dispute-resolution-victoria-rdrv-here
  - Wrong on the date and wrong on the mechanism. VCAT announced RDRV on 22 June 2025, five months before the November 2025 reform package commenced on 25 November 2025, and attributes it to the response to Victoria's Housing Statement released in 2024 - not to the reforms. RDRV's own site says the same: 'RDRV was established in 2025 as part of the response to the Housing Statement released in 2024.' And 'established under the November 2025 reforms' misdescribes what RDRV is: a search of the whole authorised Residential Tenancies Act as at 1 July 2026 for 'Rental Dispute Resolution' returns nothing, because RDRV is an administrative service delivered by VCAT rather than a statutory body created by amendment. The November 2025 changes that CAV actually lists are the ban on no-fault evictions, 90-day notice periods for rent increases and certain notices to vacate, the extended rental bidding ban, minimum standards at the point of advertising, and mandatory annual smoke alarm checks; RDRV appears in that list only as an existing body that will weigh the new excessive-rent factors. The rest of the Landlord Wise passage - that RDRV sits under the VCAT framework, is free, covers bonds, compensation, excessive rent and repairs, and is the first step for most disputes - is accurate, which is what makes the provenance sentence worth flagging rather than dismissing.
- **The one-month-per-year compensation cap applies to all standard-form Victorian rental agreements, not only long-term ones.**
  - "the Tribunal may not award an amount of compensation in excess of a maximum of one month's rent under the agreement for each 12 month period of the unexpired term of the agreement." - Victorian Government - Chief Parliamentary Counsel (authorised version, Residential Tenancies Act 1997) https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf
  - The Act attaches the cap to one kind of agreement and only that kind. Section 211A(4) opens: 'If the Tribunal is determining an application under section 210 in respect of a claim by a residential rental provider for unpaid rent in a residential rental agreement for a fixed term of more than 5 years that has been terminated early...' - the quoted limit then follows. CAV says the same twice: on the lease-breaking page, 'For fixed-term agreements that are longer than 5 years, VCAT cannot order the compensation of more than one month's rent for every 12 months remaining on the agreement', and on the agreements page it lists 'Renters who leave early may have to pay one month's rent for every full year remaining on the agreement up to a maximum of 6 months' under the heading 'Long fixed-term agreements'. Landlord Wise's own page concedes the point in the sentence before ('For agreements with a fixed term of more than 5 years, this per-year cap is also enshrined in the Act itself at s211A(4)') and then extends it to everything - which is the drift worth flagging, because for a normal 12-month lease it makes the cap sound like a ceiling on what VCAT can order when there is none. On a sub-5-year lease the limit on lost-rent compensation is not a formula, it is proof: actual loss, mitigated by prompt re-letting (s 211A(3)(b)).
- **The statutory definition of 'urgent repairs' lists 11 specific categories.**
  - "(ib) a failure to comply with any rental minimum standards; or (ic) a failure or breakdown of any safety-related devices, including a smoke alarm or pool fence; or" - Victorian Government (authorised version, Chief Parliamentary Counsel) https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf
  - The definition runs (a) to (m) plus three inserted paragraphs (ia), (ib) and (ic) - 16 paragraphs, not 11. CAV's plain-English rendering of the same definition lists 15 items. Eleven was roughly the count in the pre-2018-amendment version, before the cooling, minimum-standards and safety-device paragraphs and the pest/mould limb were added, so the figure looks like an unrefreshed count carried forward. The quoted paragraphs (ib) and (ic) are among the ones a count of 11 must be omitting.
- **VCAT typically interprets a 'reasonable time' for a rental provider to respond to an urgent repair as 24-48 hours.**
  - "Rental providers or agents must make sure the repair is done immediately." - Consumer Affairs Victoria https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/repairs-alterations-safety-and-pets/repairs/repairs-in-rental-properties
  - Three things are wrong with this at once. First, there is no 'reasonable time' standard for urgent repairs in the Act - the word used in s 72(1)(a) is 'immediately', and CAV restates it that way; 'reasonable time' belongs to a different duty. Second, the only document VCAT is actually required to consider (Director's Guideline 4, per s 73(3)) contains no 24-48 hour window: it gives 24 hours as the expectation for 'highly urgent' items only, and nothing at 48 hours at all. Third, that same guideline states 'There is no significant case law which provides additional guidance on the interpretation of' immediately - so there is no body of VCAT interpretation for a '24-48 hours' generalisation to be drawn from. OptimaRea's own supporting link for the claim, a VCAT urgent-repairs page, 404s. This is the clearest invented number in the set: 48 hours has no source in the Act, the Regulations, CAV guidance or VCAT material. AUDIT CORRECTION (2026-08-17): this note called 24-48 hours "the clearest invented number", and that overstates it. Guideline 4 DOES give 24 hours for highly urgent repairs, so the figure is not fabricated. What the contradiction actually rests on is the invented VCAT attribution ("no significant case law" supports it) and the presentation of a universal band. Correct the reasoning, keep the status.
- **Victorian electrical safety check records must be kept for five years and a copy must be given to the renter within seven days of each check without the renter asking.**
  - "a record of an electrical safety check must be kept until a record of the next electrical safety check is created" - Victorian Government (authorised version, Chief Parliamentary Counsel) https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-06/21-3sra009-authorised.pdf
  - Both halves are wrong, and both come from the same 13 October 2026 passage as the previous finding. On retention: reg 30(2)(b) of the Residential Tenancies Regulations 2021 ties the retention period to the next check rather than to a number of years, as quoted above, and reg 30(1)(b) says the same for gas records. Since the checks run on a two-year cycle, the practical retention period is about two years, not five. CAV restates the rule the same way: 'Rental providers must keep a record of gas safety checks until a record of the next safety check is created.' On the seven days: the period exists but ConditionHQ has inverted its trigger. Regulation 30(2)(c) requires that a copy of the most recent electrical safety check 'must be provided to the renter within 7 days after receipt by a residential rental provider of a written request from the renter'. It is a response obligation, keyed to a written request. ConditionHQ states the opposite twice over - 'within seven days of the check being completed', and 'This is a proactive obligation - it is not conditional on the renter making a written request.' A rental provider following that advice would be complying with a rule that does not exist while the real one, disclosure on request, is what a renter can actually enforce.
- **Victorian law defines 'urgent repairs' in Schedule 3 of the Residential Tenancies Act 1997.**
  - "Schedule 3—Transitional provisions—Disability and Social Services" - Victorian Government (authorised version, Chief Parliamentary Counsel) https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf
  - The definition of 'urgent repairs' sits in section 3(1) of the Act, in the Preliminary interpretation section - not in a Schedule. Schedule 3 of the Act is 'Transitional provisions—Disability and Social Services Regulation Amendment Act 2023' and has nothing to do with repairs. The Act has only four Schedules (1 and 1A, 2, 3, 4), all transitional/penalty/validation material. CAV's own Director's Guideline 4 states 'Section 3(1) of the Act outlines the definition of urgent repairs'. This is a straightforward citation error by the reteller, not a drafting subtlety.
- **Victorian rental providers must keep smoke alarm safety audit records and disclose the date of the most recent check before a renter signs, and failing to do so can void insurance and attract heavy fines.**
  - "the date of the most recent gas safety check, electrical safety check, and pool barrier compliance check, if required, for the rented premises" - Victorian Government (authorised version, Chief Parliamentary Counsel) https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-06/21-3sra009-authorised.pdf
  - Three assertions, none of which survives contact with the instruments. On disclosure before signing: reg 16(2)(d), quoted above, is the pre-contract disclosure list made for s 30D(e) of the Act, and it names the gas check, the electrical check and the pool barrier check. Smoke alarms are not on it. The smoke alarm test date does get disclosed in Victoria, but through a different document at a different time - item 17 of the prescribed condition report form in Schedule 1, 'Date of last smoke alarm test', which is completed at the start of the tenancy rather than before the renter signs. On record keeping: there is no smoke alarm record-keeping duty at all. Section 68B and reg 30, the only record-keeping provisions in this area, are confined to gas and electrical safety checks, and item 16 of the same condition report form likewise says only that 'The rental provider must keep records of gas and electrical safety checks.' On consequences: s 68AA carries no penalty. It is a duty provision, enforced through a breach of duty notice and then VCAT, so 'heavy fines' overstates it, and insurance is a matter of private contract on which no Victorian regulator or statute says anything. Crown cites nothing for any of this. One aside picked up on the way: old Schedule 3 clause 3(3) used to require the provider to give the renter written information on how each smoke alarm operates and how to test it, and that clause was repealed on 25 November 2025 without being carried into s 68AA - yet CAV's page still describes the duty as current.
- **Where sale proceeds of abandoned goods are unclaimed for six months, the landlord must transfer the remaining money (after costs) to a government trust account or tenancy fund - such as the Residential Tenancies Fund in Queensland - within 30 days.**
  - "the owner of premises must pay into the Residential Tenancies Fund the proceeds of the sale less the amounts in subsection (1)(a) and (b) within 30 days after the end of that 6 month period." - Victorian Government (Chief Parliamentary Counsel), Residential Tenancies Act 1997, authorised version 113 https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf
  - The Residential Tenancies Fund is Victorian, not Queensland, and the six-month / 30-day rule the page describes is the Victorian rule it has just misfiled interstate. Both numbers come from s 392 of this Act: s 392(2) gives the former renter 6 months from the date of sale to request the net proceeds, and s 392(3) - quoted above - requires the owner to pay them into the Residential Tenancies Fund within 30 days after that period ends, on penalty of 30 penalty units. The Fund itself is established by s 491 of the same Act: 'There shall be kept in the Trust Fund under the Financial Management Act 1994 an account to be called the "Residential Tenancies Fund".' The Financial Management Act 1994 is Victorian; s 3(1) defines the Fund as 'the Residential Tenancies Fund established under this Act'; and s 381(3) is the other place Part 9 uses it, letting an owner recover the costs of caring for left-behind personal documents from it. This is the most diagnostic error in the set, because it is not a drifting number - it is a correct Victorian mechanism with a Queensland label stapled on, in an article whose framing device is comparing states. Queensland's rental regulator is the Residential Tenancies Authority, a different body with a different name; I could not open the Queensland regulator's goods-left-behind pages (both 404ed) so I make no positive finding about Queensland law, but nothing is needed for the contradiction: the Fund the page places in Queensland is created by s 491 of the Victorian Act, and the deadlines it quotes are s 392's. Treat every other jurisdictional attribution on that page as suspect.

## utility charges (12)

- **[echoed-claim]** Soho Real Estate asserts that when renting in Victoria the landlord takes care of the water.
  - as of: 2022-12-23
  - quote: "So who pays water rates when renting in Victoria? When renting in Victoria, your landlord takes care of the water."
  - source: https://soho.com.au/articles/who-pays-water-rates-when-renting-in-victoria
  - **A REAL SOURCE SAYS SOMETHING NARROWER** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - A primary source exists and it does not say what Soho says it says. Victorian law splits water, it does not hand the whole of it to the landlord. Residential Tenancies Act 1997 s 52(c) makes the RENTER liable, where the premises are separately metered, for the volumetric cost of water supplied and for sewage disposal charges; s 53(1)(c) makes the rental provider liable only for the water-supply charges that are NOT based on the amount of water supplied (the fixed service charge, which is what 'water rates' usually means), and s 53(1)(d) gives the provider the lot only where the premises are not separately metered. So Soho is right about water rates and wrong about 'the water'. Note also that the same Soho article contradicts itself: this sentence says the landlord takes care of the water, and researchId 3e9df370aaeb on the same page says the renter must pay all the charges including the water rates. The figure does not drift between outlets here; it drifts inside one article.
    - source: "If the property has its own meter, the renter must pay for the water they use, as well as the sewage disposal charge."
      Consumer Affairs Victoria https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/rent-bond-bills-and-condition-reports/paying-for-utilities-phone-and-internet
    - searched: WebSearch: Consumer Affairs Victoria bills and other charges renting who pays water electricity gas separate meter (site:consumer.vic.gov.au)
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/rent-bond-bills-and-condition-reports/paying-for-utilities-phone-and-internet
    - searched: https://www.legislation.vic.gov.au/in-force/acts/residential-tenancies-act-1997 - then version 113 page for the authorised download
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf - read ss 52, 53, 53A, 54 (Division 4, utility charges)
    - searched: https://soho.com.au/articles/who-pays-water-rates-when-renting-in-victoria (redirects 308 to https://soho.ai/... which returns HTTP 403) - could not retrieve the retelling's own citation list
- **[echoed-claim]** Soho Real Estate asserts that a person living in a rental property in Victoria must pay all the charges themselves.
  - as of: 2022-12-23
  - quote: "If you live in a rental property, you must pay all the charges yourself, including the water rates."
  - source: https://soho.com.au/articles/who-pays-water-rates-when-renting-in-victoria
  - **A PRIMARY SOURCE SAYS THE OPPOSITE** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - Flatly contradicted. The renter does NOT pay all the charges, and specifically does not pay the water rates. Residential Tenancies Act 1997 s 53(1)(ab) makes the rental provider liable for 'all rates, taxes or charges payable under any Act other than charges payable by the renter under this Division', and s 53(1)(c) for 'all charges arising from a water supply service to separately metered rented premises that are not based on the amount of water supplied to the premises' - i.e. exactly the water rates Soho says the renter pays. The renter's water liability under s 52(c) is confined to volumetric usage plus sewage disposal charges. This claim and researchId 928988e6d0b0 are two mutually exclusive sentences in the same Soho article; the true position is neither of them.
    - source: "The rental provider must pay all other charges related to water supply and sewerage or drainage services."
      Consumer Affairs Victoria https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/rent-bond-bills-and-condition-reports/paying-for-utilities-phone-and-internet
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/rent-bond-bills-and-condition-reports/paying-for-utilities-phone-and-internet
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf - RTA 1997 s 53(1)(ab), (c), (d), (f); s 52
    - searched: https://soho.com.au/articles/who-pays-water-rates-when-renting-in-victoria (308 -> soho.ai, HTTP 403)

- **[echoed-claim]** Soho Real Estate asserts that landlords and tenants are both responsible for paying their respective bills.
  - as of: 2022-12-23
  - quote: "Landlords and tenants are both responsible for paying their respective bills."
  - source: https://soho.com.au/articles/who-pays-water-rates-when-renting-in-victoria
  - **BACKING FOUND** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - Backed, but only because it is close to a tautology. The substantive backing is the structure of the Act itself: Residential Tenancies Act 1997 s 52 is headed 'Renter's liability for various utility charges' and s 53 'Residential rental provider's liability for various utility charges', so liability genuinely is allocated item by item rather than wholly to one side. Worth flagging that the Soho sentence carries no information - it is true of every Australian jurisdiction and does nothing to resolve the contradiction between the other two sentences in the same article. This is a duplicate of ground the corpus already covers from a primary source, not an unbacked field claim.
    - source: "There are different rules for who pays for the ongoing cost of utilities and services, depending on what they are."
      Consumer Affairs Victoria https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/rent-bond-bills-and-condition-reports/paying-for-utilities-phone-and-internet
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/rent-bond-bills-and-condition-reports/paying-for-utilities-phone-and-internet
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf - s 52 'Renter's liability for various utility charges' and s 53 'Residential rental provider's liability for various utility charges'
- **[echoed-claim]** Soho Real Estate asserts that the landlord must provide water and electricity to each tenant.
  - as of: 2022-12-23
  - quote: "The landlord must provide water and electricity to each tenant."
  - source: https://soho.com.au/articles/who-pays-water-rates-when-renting-in-victoria
  - **NOT YET CHECKED.** Nobody has gone looking for a source for this one, so it says nothing either way.
- **[echoed-claim]** Marshall White asserts that Victorian rental providers are responsible for the initial connection charges for electricity.
  - as of: 2020-02-11
  - quote: "Rental providers are responsible for the initial connection charges for utilities like electricity, water, gas (including bottled gas), and fixed internet and telecommunications connections, including through the NBN."
  - source: https://www.marshallwhite.com.au/article/understanding-utility-responsibilities-in-victorian-rentals
  - **BACKING FOUND** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - Backed on every limb. The energy/water limb comes from Residential Tenancies Act 1997 s 53(1)(a): the rental provider is liable for 'the installation costs and charges in respect of the initial connection to rented premises of any electricity, water, gas, bottled gas or oil supply service'. The telecommunications limb is not in the s 53(1) list itself, so I chased it - Consumer Affairs Victoria states it directly in the quote above, and the Act accommodates it via s 53(1)(h) 'any other prescribed charges'. One boundary Marshall White does not draw: this is the INITIAL installation only. CAV: 'Renters must pay for the ongoing usage costs for phone and internet, unless the rental provider agrees otherwise', and a renter connecting to an existing phone line pays the general connection fee.
    - source: "The rental provider must pay all costs and charges for the initial installation of phone or internet connections. This includes connection to the National Broadband Network (NBN)."
      Consumer Affairs Victoria https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/rent-bond-bills-and-condition-reports/paying-for-utilities-phone-and-internet
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/rent-bond-bills-and-condition-reports/paying-for-utilities-phone-and-internet
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf - RTA 1997 s 53(1)(a) and s 53(1)(h)
    - searched: https://www.marshallwhite.com.au/article/understanding-utility-responsibilities-in-victorian-rentals - checked its own citations: it links tenantsvic.org.au and the generic legislation.vic.gov.au Act landing page (version 104), no section pinpoint

- **[echoed-claim]** Marshall White asserts that where a Victorian rental property does not have a separate meter, the rental provider is responsible for the entire utility costs.
  - as of: 2020-02-11
  - quote: "If the property does not have a separate meter, rental providers are responsible for the entire utility costs, including electricity, gas, and water."
  - source: https://www.marshallwhite.com.au/article/understanding-utility-responsibilities-in-victorian-rentals
  - **BACKING FOUND** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - Backed by the Act as well as by CAV. Residential Tenancies Act 1997 s 53(1)(b) makes the rental provider liable for 'all charges in respect of the supply or use of electricity, gas (except bottled gas) or oil by the renter at rented premises that are not separately metered', and s 53(1)(d) for 'water charges in respect of rented premises which are not separately metered', expressly including sewage disposal charges, all costs and charges related to a water supply service, and the water supplied. The one word doing too much work in Marshall White's sentence is 'entire': bottled gas is carved out. Under s 52(b) the renter remains liable for 'all charges for the use of any gas bottles (including supply or hire of the bottles)' whether or not anything is metered, which CAV states as 'If the property uses bottled gas the renter pays for the supply and hire of gas bottles.'
    - source: "If the property doesn’t have its own meter, the rental provider must pay."
      Consumer Affairs Victoria https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/rent-bond-bills-and-condition-reports/paying-for-utilities-phone-and-internet
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/rent-bond-bills-and-condition-reports/paying-for-utilities-phone-and-internet
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf - RTA 1997 s 52(a), s 52(b), s 53(1)(b), s 53(1)(d)
    - searched: https://www.marshallwhite.com.au/article/understanding-utility-responsibilities-in-victorian-rentals
- **[echoed-claim]** Marshall White asserts that renters are responsible for connecting and disconnecting utilities in their name during their tenancy.
  - as of: 2020-02-11
  - quote: "Renters are responsible for connecting and disconnecting utilities in their name during their tenancy."
  - source: https://www.marshallwhite.com.au/article/understanding-utility-responsibilities-in-victorian-rentals

## urgent repairs (11)

- **[echoed-claim]** Home Upkeep asserts that landlords must act on urgent repairs within 24 hours.
  - as of: 2026-06-07
  - quote: "landlords must maintain rental properties in a reasonable state of repair and act on urgent repairs within 24 hours."
  - source: https://homeupkeep.com.au/tenant-vs-landlord-who-pays-repairs-victoria/
  - **A REAL SOURCE SAYS SOMETHING NARROWER** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - The statutory standard carries no number at all. Section 72(1)(a) of the Residential Tenancies Act 1997 frames the test as the renter having taken reasonable steps to arrange for the provider 'to immediately carry out the repairs', and CAV restates that as 'immediately'. Neither the Act nor the Residential Tenancies Regulations 2021 contains any hour or day figure for urgent repairs. The 24-hour number is not pure invention, but it is conditional and it comes from a guideline rather than the law: the Director's Guideline 4 - Urgent repairs (issued under s 486; VCAT must consider it under s 73(3)) says 'If the situation is highly urgent, the repair should be completed within 24 hours of the renter notifying the RRP or the RRP's agent, or at the very least arrangements must be made to make the fault or damage safe', and in the same passage says 'More latitude would be expected for a fault such as a blocked toilet'. So 24 hours is the top-of-scale benchmark for highly urgent items, not a blanket deadline for every urgent repair, and Home Upkeep states it as a blanket rule. That guideline is published only as a.docx, which is why the verifiable quote here is CAV's HTML page. Home Upkeep's own CAV citation (consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/repairs-alterations-safety/repairs-and-maintenance) is a dead link returning 404, so its chain does not reach a live primary source.
    - source: "Rental providers must make urgent repairs immediately."
      Consumer Affairs Victoria https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/repairs-alterations-safety-and-pets/repairs/repairs-in-rental-properties

- **[echoed-claim]** Home Upkeep asserts that Victorian law defines urgent repairs in Schedule 3 of the Residential Tenancies Act.
  - as of: 2026-06-07
  - quote: "Victorian law defines “urgent repairs” in Schedule 3 of the Residential Tenancies Act."
  - source: https://homeupkeep.com.au/tenant-vs-landlord-who-pays-repairs-victoria/
  - **A PRIMARY SOURCE SAYS THE OPPOSITE** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - The definition of 'urgent repairs' sits in section 3(1) of the Act, in the Preliminary interpretation section - not in a Schedule. Schedule 3 of the Act is 'Transitional provisions—Disability and Social Services Regulation Amendment Act 2023' and has nothing to do with repairs. The Act has only four Schedules (1 and 1A, 2, 3, 4), all transitional/penalty/validation material. CAV's own Director's Guideline 4 states 'Section 3(1) of the Act outlines the definition of urgent repairs'. This is a straightforward citation error by the reteller, not a drafting subtlety.
    - source: "Schedule 3—Transitional provisions—Disability and Social Services"
      Victorian Government (authorised version, Chief Parliamentary Counsel) https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf

- **[echoed-claim]** Home Upkeep asserts that cooling failure counts as urgent from December to March.
  - as of: 2026-06-07
  - quote: "Cooling failure counts as urgent from December to March"
  - source: https://homeupkeep.com.au/tenant-vs-landlord-who-pays-repairs-victoria/
  - **A PRIMARY SOURCE SAYS THE OPPOSITE** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - Paragraph (ia) of the s 3(1) definition carries no seasonal condition, and neither does CAV's public list, which reads 'a cooling appliance or service provided by the rental provider is not working'. A full-text search of the Act and of the Residential Tenancies Regulations 2021 turns up no December-to-March window anywhere. The likeliest origin of the error is the separate cooling rental minimum standard in Schedule 4 clause 16 of the Regulations, which commences '1 March 2027' - a commencement date, not a season during which cooling failures become urgent. Note that a breach of the minimum standards is itself an urgent repair year-round under paragraph (ib), which is the opposite of a seasonal narrowing.
    - source: "a failure or breakdown of any cooling appliance or cooling service provided by a residential rental provider, rooming house operator, caravan park owner or caravan owner"
      Victorian Government (authorised version, Chief Parliamentary Counsel) https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf (s 3(1) def. of urgent repairs, paragraph (ia); searched the whole Act for 'December' and 'cooling')
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-06/21-3sra009-authorised.pdf (searched the Regulations for 'December', 'March', 'cooling')
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/repairs-alterations-safety-and-pets/repairs/repairs-in-rental-properties
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/library/publications/housing-and-accommodation/renting/guideline-4--urgent-repairs.docx (searched for any seasonal qualifier)
- **[echoed-claim]** OptimaRea asserts that the statutory definition of urgent repairs lists 11 specific categories.
  - as of: 2026-08-14
  - quote: "defines "urgent repairs" by listing 11 specific categories. There is no discretion and no judgement call"
  - source: https://optimarea.com.au/blog/rental-property-maintenance-victoria-urgent-non-urgent
  - **A REAL SOURCE SAYS SOMETHING NARROWER** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - RE-STATUSED contradicted -> ambiguous on audit (2026-08-17). Half of this is right and the tracer refuted the whole. Director's Guideline 4 states that the section 3(1) list is closed - every item on it is an urgent repair - which is the sense in which OptimaRea's 'listing specific categories' claim holds. What is wrong is 'no judgement call': the definition is built on evaluative thresholds ('a serious roof leak', 'a dangerous electrical fault', 'a substantial amount of water being wasted', and paragraph (m) delegating to the Regulations). The Guideline evidence the tracer leaned on - that items are not all equally urgent - goes to how fast a provider must respond, which is a different question from whether the definition requires judgement. [right-error-wrong-scope] Evidence: "All the items listed in the definition in section 3(1) of the Act are urgent repairs, however they are not all equally urgent." https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/library/publications/housing-and-accommodation/renting/guideline-4--urgent-repairs.docx ORIGINAL TRACE NOTE, kept because it usually found something real: The definition is built out of evaluative thresholds: 'a serious roof leak', 'serious storm or fire damage', 'a dangerous electrical fault', 'a substantial amount of water being wasted', 'any fault or damage that makes rented premises... unsafe or insecure', 'a serious fault in a lift or staircase', and paragraph (m) 'any damage of a prescribed class', which delegates the boundary to the Regulations outright. Every one of those requires a judgement call. The Director's Guideline 4 makes the point explicitly - it tells VCAT that 'All the items listed in the definition in section 3(1) of the Act are urgent repairs, however they are not all equally urgent', and directs the tribunal to weigh 'The urgency of the repair' and 'The complexity or significance of the repair' on the facts. OptimaRea's 'no discretion and no judgement call' is the opposite of how the Director tells the tribunal to read it.
    - source: "malfunctioning in a way that results or will result in a substantial amount of water being wasted"
      Victorian Government (authorised version, Chief Parliamentary Counsel) https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf (read the whole s 3(1) definition and s 72)
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/library/publications/housing-and-accommodation/renting/guideline-4--urgent-repairs.docx (Director's Guideline 4, 'Guidance on the interpretation of immediately')
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/repairs-alterations-safety-and-pets/repairs/repairs-in-rental-properties
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/library/publications/housing-and-accommodation/renting/guideline-4--urgent-repairs.docx (audit re-check 2026-08-17)

- **[echoed-claim]** OptimaRea asserts that the $2,500 urgent repair limit in Victoria was last increased in the 2021 reforms.
  - as of: 2026-08-14
  - quote: "The $2,500 statutory limit was last increased in the 2021 reforms (up from $1,800 previously)"
  - source: https://optimarea.com.au/blog/rental-property-maintenance-victoria-urgent-non-urgent
  - **BACKING FOUND** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - Regulation 13 of the now-revoked Residential Tenancies Regulations 2019 prescribed $1800 for the same provision, s 72(2)(b). Regulation 4 of the 2021 Regulations revokes the 2019 Regulations, so the $1800 -> $2500 step is a direct substitution on 29 March 2021. The same $1800 figure applied under the 2019 instrument to rooming houses (s 129(2)(b)) and caravans (s 188(2)(b)); those were all lifted to $2500 in 2021 as well. This is one of the few numbers in this subject that the retellers got exactly right.
    - source: "For the purposes of section 72(2)(b) of the Act, the prescribed amount is $1800."
      Victorian Government (authorised version, Chief Parliamentary Counsel) https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/814c2f92-b3a5-3855-a398-099dfde0567e_19-14sra001%20authorised.pdf
    - searched: https://www.legislation.vic.gov.au/repealed-revoked/statutory-rules/residential-tenancies-regulations-2019/001
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/814c2f92-b3a5-3855-a398-099dfde0567e_19-14sra001%20authorised.pdf (reg 13)
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-06/21-3sra009-authorised.pdf (reg 4 Revocation - confirms the 2019 Regulations were the instrument replaced)
    - searched: https://www.legislation.vic.gov.au/repealed-revoked/statutory-rules/residential-tenancies-regulations-2008

- **[echoed-claim]** OptimaRea asserts that a "reasonable time" for a Victorian landlord to respond to an urgent repair is typically interpreted by VCAT as 24-48 hours.
  - as of: 2026-08-14
  - quote: "typically interpreted by VCAT as 24-48 hours for urgent repairs"
  - source: https://optimarea.com.au/blog/rental-property-maintenance-victoria-urgent-non-urgent
  - **A PRIMARY SOURCE SAYS THE OPPOSITE** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - Three things are wrong with this at once. First, there is no 'reasonable time' standard for urgent repairs in the Act - the word used in s 72(1)(a) is 'immediately', and CAV restates it that way; 'reasonable time' belongs to a different duty. Second, the only document VCAT is actually required to consider (Director's Guideline 4, per s 73(3)) contains no 24-48 hour window: it gives 24 hours as the expectation for 'highly urgent' items only, and nothing at 48 hours at all. Third, that same guideline states 'There is no significant case law which provides additional guidance on the interpretation of' immediately - so there is no body of VCAT interpretation for a '24-48 hours' generalisation to be drawn from. OptimaRea's own supporting link for the claim, a VCAT urgent-repairs page, 404s. This is the clearest invented number in the set: 48 hours has no source in the Act, the Regulations, CAV guidance or VCAT material. AUDIT CORRECTION (2026-08-17): this note called 24-48 hours "the clearest invented number", and that overstates it. Guideline 4 DOES give 24 hours for highly urgent repairs, so the figure is not fabricated. What the contradiction actually rests on is the invented VCAT attribution ("no significant case law" supports it) and the presentation of a universal band. Correct the reasoning, keep the status.
    - source: "Rental providers or agents must make sure the repair is done immediately."
      Consumer Affairs Victoria https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/repairs-alterations-safety-and-pets/repairs/repairs-in-rental-properties
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/repairs-alterations-safety-and-pets/repairs/repairs-in-rental-properties
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/library/publications/housing-and-accommodation/renting/guideline-4--urgent-repairs.docx (the guideline VCAT must consider under s 73(3))
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf (ss 68, 72, 73 - searched for 'reasonable time')
    - searched: https://www.vcat.vic.gov.au/case-types/residential-tenancies-list/urgent-repairs (the page OptimaRea itself links for this claim - returns HTTP 404)
    - searched: https://www.vcat.vic.gov.au/cases-and-hearings/case-types/residential-tenancies (HTTP 404)
- **[echoed-claim]** OptimaRea asserts that non-urgent repairs in Victoria fall under section 73A of the Residential Tenancies Act 1997.
  - as of: 2026-08-14
  - quote: "Repairs that do not appear on the urgent list fall under section 73A of the RTA 1997"
  - source: https://optimarea.com.au/blog/rental-property-maintenance-victoria-urgent-non-urgent
  - **A PRIMARY SOURCE SAYS THE OPPOSITE** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - There is no section 73A in the Residential Tenancies Act 1997 - a full-text search of the current authorised version (v113) returns no such provision. Section 73 is 'Application to Tribunal for urgent repairs', i.e. the urgent path, not the non-urgent one. The non-urgent route is s 74 (application to the Director to investigate the need for non-urgent repairs) and s 75 (application to the Tribunal), sitting on top of the s 68 duty to maintain the premises in good repair. CAV's own page points readers to ss 72, 74 and 75 for exactly this reason. The invented section number is the tell: a plausible-looking '73A' bridging the real 73 and 74.
    - source: "75 Application to Tribunal for non-urgent repairs"
      Victorian Government (authorised version, Chief Parliamentary Counsel) https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf (full-text search for '73A'; read ss 72, 73, 74, 75)
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/repairs-alterations-safety-and-pets/repairs/repairs-in-rental-properties (CAV's own 'what the law says' section references)
    - searched: https://www.legislation.vic.gov.au/in-force/acts/residential-tenancies-act-1997/113
- **[echoed-claim]** Crown Real Estate asserts that if urgent repairs are not addressed immediately, Victorian renters can spend up to $2,500 to fix the issue themselves.
  - as of: 2026-07-12
  - quote: "If these aren’t addressed immediately, renters can spend up to $2,500 to fix the issue themselves and must be reimbursed within seven days."
  - source: https://www.crownrea.com.au/landlord-rights-and-responsibilities-victoria-the-2026-rental-providers-guide/
  - **BACKING FOUND** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - Section 72(2)(c) of the Act requires the provider to 'reimburse the renter for the reasonable cost' of repairs within 7 days after receiving written notice of the repairs and their cost; s 72(2)(a) requires the renter to give that written notice within 7 days of the repairs. The seven-day clock therefore runs from receipt of the renter's written notice, not from the date of the repair - a distinction Crown's wording elides but does not get wrong. This is the one numeric deadline in the whole urgent-repairs subject that is genuinely statutory.
    - source: "Rental providers have 7 days to pay from the date they receive written notice."
      Consumer Affairs Victoria https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/repairs-alterations-safety-and-pets/repairs/repairs-in-rental-properties
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf (s 72(2)(a) and s 72(2)(c))
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/repairs-alterations-safety-and-pets/repairs/repairs-in-rental-properties
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/library/publications/housing-and-accommodation/renting/guideline-4--urgent-repairs.docx

## modifications (10)

- **[echoed-claim]** Some modifications can be made by renters without the rental provider's consent.
  - as of: 2026-08-14
  - quote: "You can make some changes or modifications to your rental property. Some you can make without the consent of the rental provider (landlord), while others need their approval – but this cannot be unreasonably refused."
  - source: https://tenantsvic.org.au/explore-topics/during-your-tenancy/modifications/
  - **BACKING FOUND** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - Backed, and the primary source is more specific than the retelling. RTA 1997 s 64(1) says a renter may make 'prescribed modifications' without consent, and Residential Tenancies Regulations 2021 reg 26 sets the closed list: window film, wireless doorbell, curtains, adhesive child locks, pressure-mounted gates, letterbox lock (any premises), plus picture hooks/screws, wall anchors, LED globes, water-efficient shower head, blind or cord anchors, non-hardwired security lights/alarms/cameras and hardware-mounted child gates (only where the premises is not a 'registered place', i.e. not on the Victorian Heritage Register). It is a fixed list, not a general licence. Commenced 29 March 2021 (reg 3).
    - source: "A renter can install any of the following items without permission"
      Consumer Affairs Victoria https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/repairs-alterations-safety-and-pets/renters-making-changes-to-the-property
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/repairs-alterations-safety-and-pets/renters-making-changes-to-the-property (Consumer Affairs Victoria, last updated 7 December 2025)
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2021-04/21-003sra%20authorised.PDF (Residential Tenancies Regulations 2021 S.R. 3/2021, authorised version - read regs 3, 26, 27, 28)
    - searched: Residential Tenancies Act 1997 (Vic) authorised version incorporating amendments as at 1 July 2026, s 64 Modifications to rented premises (read s 64(1), (1A), (1B), (1C), (1D), (1E), (2), (3))
    - searched: site:consumer.vic.gov.au renters making changes to the property modifications without permission
    - searched: https://tenantsvic.org.au/explore-topics/during-your-tenancy/modifications/ (the cited retelling - Tenants Victoria, an advocacy body, not the primary source)

    - Backed, and the primary source is more specific than the retelling. RTA 1997 s 64(1) says a renter may make 'prescribed modifications' without consent, and Residential Tenancies Regulations 2021 reg 26 sets the closed list: window film, wireless doorbell, curtains, adhesive child locks, pressure-mounted gates, letterbox lock (any premises), plus picture hooks/screws, wall anchors, LED globes, water-efficient shower head, blind or cord anchors, non-hardwired security lights/alarms/cameras and hardware-mounted child gates (only where the premises is not a 'registered place', i.e. not on the Victorian Heritage Register). It is a fixed list, not a general licence. Commenced 29 March 2021 (reg 3).
    - source: "A renter can install any of the following items without permission"
      Consumer Affairs Victoria https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/repairs-alterations-safety-and-pets/renters-making-changes-to-the-property
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/repairs-alterations-safety-and-pets/renters-making-changes-to-the-property (Consumer Affairs Victoria, last updated 7 December 2025)
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2021-04/21-003sra%20authorised.PDF (Residential Tenancies Regulations 2021 S.R. 3/2021, authorised version - read regs 3, 26, 27, 28)

    - searched: site:consumer.vic.gov.au renters making changes to the property modifications without permission
    - searched: https://tenantsvic.org.au/explore-topics/during-your-tenancy/modifications/ (the cited retelling - Tenants Victoria, an advocacy body, not the primary source)
- **[echoed-claim]** RACV asserts that modifications to a rental property can be made by Victorian renters without permission from March 2021.
  - as of: 2026-08-14
  - quote: "in March 2021 outlined that renters can now make certain changes to the rental property without permission – as long as the property can be returned to its original state when they leave. That means that any home decor updates must either be reversed before the renter vacates the property, or the renter must pay the landlord the cost of doing so."
  - source: https://www.racv.com.au/royalauto/property/renting/rental-tenant-laws-victoria.html
  - **A REAL SOURCE SAYS SOMETHING NARROWER** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - Half right, and the half that is wrong matters. The restore-or-pay duty is real and almost verbatim: RTA s 64(2) requires a renter who has installed fixtures or altered the premises (with or without consent) to restore them, fair wear and tear excepted, or pay the reasonable cost of restoration. But 'as long as the property can be returned to its original state' is not the test for which modifications may be made without permission - that is the closed prescribed list in reg 26. Reversibility appears elsewhere: as a ground on which a provider may refuse consent (s 64(1C)(b)(iv), (c)). And the restoration duty has express exceptions under s 64(3) / CAV: where the provider agrees, where the agreement says otherwise, or where a modification bond has been paid (a modification bond cannot be required if it would be under $500, or where the changes are NDIS-funded on condition that no restoration is required).
    - source: "restore the property to the condition it was in immediately before the changes were made, allowing for fair wear"
      Consumer Affairs Victoria https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/repairs-alterations-safety-and-pets/renters-making-changes-to-the-property
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/repairs-alterations-safety-and-pets/renters-making-changes-to-the-property (Consumer Affairs Victoria, last updated 7 December 2025)
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2021-04/21-003sra%20authorised.PDF (Residential Tenancies Regulations 2021 S.R. 3/2021, authorised version - read regs 3, 26, 27, 28)

- **[echoed-claim]** RACV asserts that the law allows renters to install picture hooks provided it is not a heritage-listed property and they do not penetrate exposed brick or concrete walls.
  - as of: 2026-08-14
  - quote: "The law allows renters to install picture hooks, provided it's not a heritage-listed property and does not penetrate exposed brick or concrete walls."
  - source: https://www.racv.com.au/royalauto/property/renting/rental-tenant-laws-victoria.html
  - **BACKING FOUND** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - Accurate. Reg 26(a)(i) permits 'installation of picture hooks or screws for wall mounts, shelves or brackets on surfaces other than exposed brick or concrete walls' in premises that are not a 'registered place' - i.e. not on the Victorian Heritage Register, which is what RACV means by heritage-listed. The complementary rule RACV omits: on exposed brick or concrete the renter must ask, but under reg 28(a) consent cannot be unreasonably refused. CAV adds a practical warning about asbestos-containing materials.
    - source: "picture hooks or screws for wall mounts, shelves or brackets on all surfaces except exposed brick or concrete walls"
      Consumer Affairs Victoria https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/repairs-alterations-safety-and-pets/renters-making-changes-to-the-property

- **[echoed-claim]** RACV asserts that a Victorian renter can install a security device without the landlord's permission as long as it is not hard wired, can be removed easily and does not affect neighbours' privacy.
  - as of: 2026-08-14
  - quote: "without the landlord’s permission, as long as the device is not hard wired, can be removed easily and doesn’t affect neighbours’ privacy. You’ll need prior permission if the security device needs to be fitted by a"
  - source: https://www.racv.com.au/royalauto/property/renting/rental-tenant-laws-victoria.html
  - **BACKING FOUND** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - Backed, with all three conditions intact. Reg 26(a)(vi) permits installation of security lights, alarm systems or security cameras that (A) do not impact on the privacy of neighbours (the regulation's own example: a renter must not install a camera or light that directly faces a neighbouring premises), (B) can be easily removed, and (C) are not hardwired. RACV's follow-on point is also right: a security system that must be fitted by a suitably qualified person falls under reg 28(e), which requires consent - but consent that cannot be unreasonably refused, provided an invoice naming the installer is given when consent is requested. On the CAV page this quote sits under the 'Changes that can be made without permission' heading, in the sub-list that also requires the property not to be on the Victorian Heritage Register.
    - source: "removable safety devices such as alarm systems or security cameras, as long as they"
