- **[echoed-claim]** RACV asserts that the landlord cannot unreasonably refuse disability-related modifications.
  - as of: 2026-08-14
  - quote: "The landlord can’t unreasonably refuse if you want to install disability-related modifications, such as grab rails and hand rails determined necessary by an occupational therapist or other practitioner."
  - source: https://www.racv.com.au/royalauto/property/renting/rental-tenant-laws-victoria.html
  - **BACKING FOUND** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - Backed by RTA s 64(1B)(c): consent must not be unreasonably refused for modifications that are reasonable alterations within the meaning of s 55 of the Equal Opportunity Act 2010 and are assessed and determined to be required by an accredited occupational therapist or a prescribed practitioner (reg 27: a registered health practitioner under the Health Practitioner Regulation National Law). Stronger than RACV states: s 64(1D) excludes disability-related modifications from the s 64(1C) grounds on which a provider may otherwise refuse. Renters still need written agreement (and the owners corporation's, if any) before the work is done, and NDIS-funded modifications can be exempt from the restoration/modification-bond rules.
    - source: "Rental providers can not refuse disability-related modifications without good reason."
      Consumer Affairs Victoria https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/repairs-alterations-safety-and-pets/renters-making-changes-to-the-property
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/repairs-alterations-safety-and-pets/renters-making-changes-to-the-property (Consumer Affairs Victoria, last updated 7 December 2025)
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2021-04/21-003sra%20authorised.PDF (Residential Tenancies Regulations 2021 S.R. 3/2021, authorised version - read regs 3, 26, 27, 28)
    - searched: Residential Tenancies Act 1997 (Vic) authorised version incorporating amendments as at 1 July 2026, s 64 Modifications to rented premises (read s 64(1), (1A), (1B), (1C), (1D), (1E), (2), (3))
    - searched: Residential Tenancies Act 1997 (Vic) s 64(1B)(c) reasonable alterations Equal Opportunity Act 2010 s 55, accredited occupational therapist or prescribed practitioner
    - searched: Residential Tenancies Regulations 2021 reg 27 Practitioners (prescribed practitioner = registered health practitioner under the Health Practitioner Regulation National Law)
    - searched: Consumer Affairs Victoria 'Changes for renters with disability' section

- **[echoed-claim]** The modifications' further requirement takes effect on 1 July 2027.
  - as of: 2026-08-14
  - quote: "A further requirement takes effect on 1 July 2027, when draughtproofing becomes mandatory at the start of any new lease or month-to-month conversion. Rental providers will need to have sealed external doors, windows and unsealed wall vents to prevent unnecessary heat loss or gain."
  - source: https://www.kayburton.com.au/victorias-evolving-minimum-standards-for-rental-properties-what-you-need-to-know/
  - **BACKING FOUND** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - Backed, date and trigger both correct: CAV states the requirement applies 'From 1 July 2027' and 'At the start of a new rental agreement, or conversion to a month-to-month agreement', under the Residential Tenancies Amendment (Minimum Energy Efficiency Standards) Regulations 2025. Kay & Burton omits two material qualifiers: where the property has gas appliances a licensed plumber must complete a gas safety check less than six months old before the work, and draughtproofing must not proceed at all if un-flued or open-flued gas appliances (or a gas cooktop without a rangehood) are present; heritage and owners-corporation restrictions are also exemptions. Their surrounding timeline is otherwise consistent with CAV (1 December 2025 corded window coverings, 1 March 2027 ceiling insulation / 4-star shower heads / efficient cooling and end-of-life replacement rules, 1 July 2030 cooling for all rentals).
    - source: "rental providers must draughtproof all external doors, windows and unsealed wall vents"
      Consumer Affairs Victoria https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/resources-and-tools/legislation/public-consultations-and-reviews/new-minimum-energy-efficiency-standards
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/repairs-alterations-safety-and-pets/renters-making-changes-to-the-property (Consumer Affairs Victoria, last updated 7 December 2025)
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2021-04/21-003sra%20authorised.PDF (Residential Tenancies Regulations 2021 S.R. 3/2021, authorised version - read regs 3, 26, 27, 28)
    - searched: Residential Tenancies Act 1997 (Vic) authorised version incorporating amendments as at 1 July 2026, s 64 Modifications to rented premises (read s 64(1), (1A), (1B), (1C), (1D), (1E), (2), (3))
    - searched: Victoria rental minimum standards draughtproofing "1 July 2027" regulations consumer.vic.gov.au
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/resources-and-tools/legislation/public-consultations-and-reviews/new-minimum-energy-efficiency-standards (CAV, last updated 2 August 2026)
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/repairs-alterations-safety-and-pets/minimum-standards/minimum-standards-for-rental-properties (only points forward to the 1 March 2027 tranche)
    - searched: https://www.energy.vic.gov.au/households/save-with-all-electric-home/electric-and-efficiency-standards-for-buildings/energy-efficiency-for-rental-properties-in-victoria (403 to a scripted fetch)

## abandoned goods (9)

- **[echoed-claim]** In Victoria, non-document abandoned goods under $1,000 may be disposed of after 14 days' notice.
  - as of: 2026-03-07
  - quote: "For example, in Victoria, non-document goods under $1,000 may be disposed of after 14 days’ notice, while higher-value goods have longer notice requirements."
  - source: https://dirt2tidy.com.au/blog/how-tenant-belongings-left-behind-rental/
  - **A PRIMARY SOURCE SAYS THE OPPOSITE** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - The $1,000 threshold does not exist. There is no monetary figure anywhere in Part 9 of the Act, and the single regulation made for Part 9 (reg 93) prescribes categories of object, not values. What the Act actually does is split goods three ways, and value only matters at one extreme. Under s 384(1) an owner may remove and destroy or dispose of goods immediately - no notice, no storage - if they 'are of no monetary value', 'are perishable foodstuffs' or 'are dangerous'. Reg 93 then carves back out of that even for worthless goods: 'labelled containers or labelled urns containing human remains', 'specialised medical devices, equipment and goods including prostheses and prescription medication', and 'medals and trophies'. Everything else - a $50 kettle and a $50,000 piano alike - goes through s 386: notice in the form approved by the Director, then storage for at least 14 days, then disposal or sale under s 391(1). So the second limb is flatly contradicted: higher value does not buy longer notice. The only things that extend the 14 days are the owner's own agreement or a VCAT order, per the closing words of s 391(1) quoted above. The first limb is right about '14 days' but attaches it to a made-up qualifying condition, which is worse than being wrong outright - a reader with a $1,500 item would conclude the 14-day rule does not apply to them when it does.
    - source: "An owner of premises may sell or dispose of stored goods if the former renter or other person who has a lawful right to the stored goods has not reclaimed them within 14 days, unless the owner of premises has agreed or been ordered by the Tribunal to store the goods for longer than 14 days."
      Victorian Government (Chief Parliamentary Counsel), Residential Tenancies Act 1997, authorised version 113 https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf - Part 9 (Goods left behind by renters, residents and site tenants) read in full: ss 379-394, i.e. Division 1 definitions, Division 2 personal documents (ss 380-382), Division 3 goods (ss 383, 384, 386-388, 391, 392, 394)
    - searched: grep of the whole authorised Act text for any monetary threshold in Part 9 ($1,000, 1 000, prescribed amount) - no monetary figure appears anywhere in Part 9
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2021-04/21-003sra%20authorised.PDF - Residential Tenancies Regulations 2021, reg 93 (Goods left behind), the only regulation made for Part 9
    - searched: https://dirt2tidy.com.au/blog/how-tenant-belongings-left-behind-rental/ - the retelling itself

    - searched: https://dirt2tidy.com.au/blog/how-tenant-belongings-left-behind-rental/ - the retelling itself
- **[echoed-claim]** Dirt2Tidy states that Victorian landlords must store personal documents for up to 90 days.
  - as of: 2026-03-07
  - quote: "For example, Victorian landlords must store personal documents for up to 90 days, while in Queensland, the period is just seven days."
  - source: https://dirt2tidy.com.au/blog/how-tenant-belongings-left-behind-rental/
  - **BACKING FOUND** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - Backed on the number, with one wording quibble that runs the safe way for renters and the unsafe way for a landlord relying on the page. s 380(a) is not a ceiling of 'up to 90 days' - it is a fixed obligation to take reasonable care of the documents FOR 90 days. A landlord who reads 'up to' as licence to bin documents at day 30 has broken the duty. The disposal power only arrives at the end of that period, under s 381(1), and s 380(b)-(c) additionally forbid destroying or disposing of the documents except in accordance with Part 9 and require reasonable steps to notify the former renter when and where they can be collected. s 382 lets the former renter reclaim them at any time before disposal on paying the reasonable notification, removal and care costs, and refusing to hand them over then carries 150 penalty units for a natural person, 750 for a body corporate. Documents are a genuinely separate track: s 383 says 'This Division does not apply to personal documents', so the 14-day goods regime never touches them. The Queensland half of the same sentence ('the period is just seven days') is outside Victoria and outside this trace; the Queensland regulator's pages 404ed, so I make no finding either way on it.
    - source: "must take reasonable care of the personal documents for a period of 90 days"
      Victorian Government (Chief Parliamentary Counsel), Residential Tenancies Act 1997, authorised version 113 https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf - Part 9 Division 2 (Personal documents left behind), ss 380, 381, 382, and s 383 which excludes documents from the goods Division
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2021-04/21-003sra%20authorised.PDF - checked the Regulations for any variation of the 90-day period; there is none
    - searched: https://www.rta.qld.gov.au/ending-a-tenancy/goods-and-documents-left-behind - HTTP 404 (the claim's Queensland comparison could not be checked against the Queensland regulator)
    - searched: https://www.rta.qld.gov.au/during-a-tenancy/ending-a-tenancy/goods-left-behind - HTTP 404
    - searched: https://dirt2tidy.com.au/blog/how-tenant-belongings-left-behind-rental/ - the retelling itself
- **[echoed-claim]** Dirt2Tidy states that under the Residential Tenancies Act 1997 a property is considered abandoned when the tenant has permanently vacated and no longer treats it as their primary residence.
  - as of: 2026-03-07
  - quote: "Under the Residential Tenancies Act 1997, a property is considered abandoned when the tenant has permanently vacated and no longer treats it as their primary residence."
  - source: https://dirt2tidy.com.au/blog/how-tenant-belongings-left-behind-rental/
  - **A REAL SOURCE SAYS SOMETHING NARROWER** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - The Act is named, but it does not contain this definition. For rented premises the Act says only that 'A residential rental agreement terminates if the renter abandons the rented premises' (s 91F) and then hands the question to VCAT: under s 91ZG a rental provider who believes the premises are abandoned applies to VCAT, the application must be heard within 5 business days, and VCAT may declare the premises abandoned on a specified day - the words quoted above. 'Abandon' is left undefined in s 3(1) and in the Part 9 definitions in s 379. Where the Act does define abandonment, it uses a different test from the one being repeated. s 206AO(1), for caravan park sites, turns on intention rather than residence: a resident abandons the site 'if the resident leaves it without any intention of returning' and either without first giving notice of intention to vacate or without the owner's agreement. s 206AO(2) then gives two indicators of no intention to return, the first being that 'the resident has not occupied the site or caravan for a period of at least 14 days and has not paid any rent or hiring charges for that period'. 'Permanently vacated and no longer treats it as their primary residence' is therefore not the statutory test in Victoria, and the difference is practical rather than semantic. A renter can stop treating premises as their primary residence and still not have abandoned them - they may intend to return, or may simply be away - and the Act's answer in that case is that the provider cannot self-declare; it must go to VCAT. Reading the blog's definition as law invites exactly the self-help the s 91ZG procedure exists to prevent.
    - source: "the Tribunal by order may declare that the rented premises were abandoned by the renter on a day specified by the Tribunal"
      Victorian Government (Chief Parliamentary Counsel), Residential Tenancies Act 1997, authorised version 113 https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf - searched every occurrence of 'abandon' in the authorised Act: ss 91F, 91ZG, 91ZH (rented premises), ss 142O, 142Z (rooming houses), ss 206AE, 206AO, 206AP (caravan parks and sites), s 390A (Part 4A dwellings)
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf - checked the s 3(1) definitions and the Part 9 s 379 definitions for a definition of 'abandon' or 'abandoned'; there is none
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting - CAV's renting index lists 'renters abandoning the property' under 'Moving out, giving notice and evictions'
    - searched: https://dirt2tidy.com.au/blog/how-tenant-belongings-left-behind-rental/ - the retelling itself, which attributes the definition to the Act by name
- **[echoed-claim]** Haven Agents states that rental providers must give renters notice of goods left behind.
  - as of: 2021-03-29
  - quote: "Rental providers must give renters notice of goods left behind and store these in a safe place for at least 14 days after giving the notice."
  - source: https://www.havenagents.com.au/residential-tenancies-act-changes-2021/
  - **BACKING FOUND** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - Backed, and correct on the detail that most retellings get wrong: the 14 days run from the day the NOTICE was given, not from the day the renter moved out. This is s 386(1)(b), and it is one of the 29 March 2021 changes - the section was substituted by the Residential Tenancies Amendment Act 2018 s 268. Two small corrections to the phrasing. The notice is not free-form: s 386(1)(a) requires 'reasonable steps to give a notice, in the form approved by the Director, to the former renter that the goods have been left behind', and s 386(2) requires it to state the former renter's rights and obligations. And storing 'in a safe place' is a permission, not the duty - s 386(3) says the owner 'may remove the goods from the premises and store them at a safe place'. The duty is to store for at least 14 days; where is the owner's choice. Also note s 386(1) only applies to goods other than those disposable immediately under s 384, and that an owner who has to store goods can charge an occupation fee under s 388, capped at the daily rent and at 14 days' rent in total unless VCAT orders otherwise.
    - source: "store the goods for a period of at least 14 days, beginning on the day on which the owner of premises gave the notice to the former renter."
      Victorian Government (Chief Parliamentary Counsel), Residential Tenancies Act 1997, authorised version 113 https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf - s 386 (What must an owner of premises do about goods left behind?), subsections (1), (2) and (3), and s 387
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf - s 379 definition of 'stored goods', which is keyed to storage 'in accordance with section 386'
    - searched: https://www.havenagents.com.au/residential-tenancies-act-changes-2021/ - the retelling itself
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2021-04/21-003sra%20authorised.PDF - checked the Regulations for a prescribed notice form; s 386(1)(a) uses a form approved by the Director rather than a prescribed one

  - **BACKING FOUND** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - Backed, and correct on the detail that most retellings get wrong: the 14 days run from the day the NOTICE was given, not from the day the renter moved out. This is s 386(1)(b), and it is one of the 29 March 2021 changes - the section was substituted by the Residential Tenancies Amendment Act 2018 s 268. Two small corrections to the phrasing. The notice is not free-form: s 386(1)(a) requires 'reasonable steps to give a notice, in the form approved by the Director, to the former renter that the goods have been left behind', and s 386(2) requires it to state the former renter's rights and obligations. And storing 'in a safe place' is a permission, not the duty - s 386(3) says the owner 'may remove the goods from the premises and store them at a safe place'.       Victorian Government (Chief Parliamentary Counsel), Residential Tenancies Act 1997, authorised version 113 https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf - s 386 (What must an owner of premises do about goods left behind?), subsections (1), (2) and (3), and s 387

- **[echoed-claim]** Dirt2Tidy states that landlords cannot dispose of or handle abandoned goods until the lease is officially over and the tenant has fully vacated.
  - as of: 2026-03-07
  - quote: "Landlords cannot dispose of or handle any leftover property until the lease is officially over and the tenant has fully vacated."
  - source: https://dirt2tidy.com.au/blog/how-tenant-belongings-left-behind-rental/
  - **A REAL SOURCE SAYS SOMETHING NARROWER** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - Half right, and the half that is wrong is the sweeping half. Part 9 is indeed keyed to the tenancy having ended - it speaks throughout of a 'former renter', and the s 379 definition of 'stored goods' refers to premises 'in respect of which a residential rental agreement has been terminated'. To that extent the claim holds. But 'cannot dispose of or handle ANY leftover property' is contradicted by the Act's own first move. s 384(1), quoted above, continues: the owner may remove and destroy or dispose of goods if '(a) they are of no monetary value; or (b) they are perishable foodstuffs; or (c) they are dangerous' - with no notice period and no storage obligation, subject only to the reg 93 exclusions (human remains, specialised medical devices and prescription medication, medals and trophies). And 'handle' is wrong on its own terms: s 386(3) expressly permits the owner to remove goods from the premises and store them elsewhere, which is the normal thing to do. The phrase 'the lease is officially over' also misleads about the commonest case. Where the renter simply goes, nobody signs anything: s 91F terminates the agreement by operation of law on abandonment, and s 91ZG lets VCAT declare the premises abandoned from a specified day, with the hearing required within 5 business days. Waiting for an 'official' end can mean waiting for a document that will never exist.
    - source: "If goods have been left behind, the owner of premises may remove and destroy or dispose of the goods if"
      Victorian Government (Chief Parliamentary Counsel), Residential Tenancies Act 1997, authorised version 113 https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf - s 384 (Disposal of certain goods left behind), s 386(3), s 391, and the s 379 definitions which key Part 9 to a 'former renter' and to an agreement that 'has been terminated'
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf - ss 91F and 91ZG, on when the agreement ends where the renter has gone without notice
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2021-04/21-003sra%20authorised.PDF - reg 93, the goods that may not be destroyed even when worthless
    - searched: https://dirt2tidy.com.au/blog/how-tenant-belongings-left-behind-rental/ - the retelling itself
- **[echoed-claim]** Dirt2Tidy states that if proceeds from the sale of abandoned goods are unclaimed for six months, landlords must transfer the remaining money to a government trust account or tenancy fund within 30 days.
  - as of: 2026-03-07
  - quote: "If they don’t claim within six months, landlords are responsible for transferring any remaining sale proceeds (after costs) to a government trust account or tenancy fund—such as the Residential Tenancies Fund in Queensland—within 30 days."
  - source: https://dirt2tidy.com.au/blog/how-tenant-belongings-left-behind-rental/
  - **A PRIMARY SOURCE SAYS THE OPPOSITE** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - The Residential Tenancies Fund is Victorian, not Queensland, and the six-month / 30-day rule the page describes is the Victorian rule it has just misfiled interstate. Both numbers come from s 392 of this Act: s 392(2) gives the former renter 6 months from the date of sale to request the net proceeds, and s 392(3) - quoted above - requires the owner to pay them into the Residential Tenancies Fund within 30 days after that period ends, on penalty of 30 penalty units. The Fund itself is established by s 491 of the same Act: 'There shall be kept in the Trust Fund under the Financial Management Act 1994 an account to be called the "Residential Tenancies Fund".' The Financial Management Act 1994 is Victorian; s 3(1) defines the Fund as 'the Residential Tenancies Fund established under this Act'; and s 381(3) is the other place Part 9 uses it, letting an owner recover the costs of caring for left-behind personal documents from it. This is the most diagnostic error in the set, because it is not a drifting number - it is a correct Victorian mechanism with a Queensland label stapled on, in an article whose framing device is comparing states. Queensland's rental regulator is the Residential Tenancies Authority, a different body with a different name; I could not open the Queensland regulator's goods-left-behind pages (both 404ed) so I make no positive finding about Queensland law, but nothing is needed for the contradiction: the Fund the page places in Queensland is created by s 491 of the Victorian Act, and the deadlines it quotes are s 392's. Treat every other jurisdictional attribution on that page as suspect.
    - source: "the owner of premises must pay into the Residential Tenancies Fund the proceeds of the sale less the amounts in subsection (1)(a) and (b) within 30 days after the end of that 6 month period."
      Victorian Government (Chief Parliamentary Counsel), Residential Tenancies Act 1997, authorised version 113 https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf - s 392 (Renter may request proceeds of sale of goods), subsections (1), (2) and (3)
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf - s 491 (Establishment of Residential Tenancies Fund), ss 492-495, and the s 3(1) definition of 'Residential Tenancies Fund'
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf - s 381(3), the other Part 9 provision that draws on the Fund
    - searched: https://www.rta.qld.gov.au/ending-a-tenancy/goods-and-documents-left-behind - HTTP 404
    - searched: https://www.rta.qld.gov.au/during-a-tenancy/ending-a-tenancy/goods-left-behind - HTTP 404
    - searched: https://dirt2tidy.com.au/blog/how-tenant-belongings-left-behind-rental/ - the retelling itself

- **[echoed-claim]** Best Carpet Steam Cleaning Melbourne states that property managers across Melbourne now use high-resolution digital inspection tools that catch even the smallest details.
  - as of: 2026-08-14
  - quote: "Property managers across Melbourne now utilize high-resolution digital inspection tools that catch even the smallest details."
  - source: https://www.carpetsteammelbourne.com.au/end-of-lease-cleaning-checklist-victoria-2026-the-ultimate-bond-back-guide/
  - **SEARCHED, NOTHING FOUND** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - Unbacked and unfalsifiable as written: no named vendor, no resolution, no share of agencies, no date. The trail ends at cleaning-company copy on one side and at software vendors' own product pages on the other; nobody measures Melbourne property managers. The only primary material on the subject cuts the other way - Consumer Affairs Victoria's condition report guidance still treats photography as optional and format-neutral ('Photos can be either hard copies or digital ones.', https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/rent-bond-bills-and-condition-reports/condition-reports, last updated 24 March 2025), and neither the Act nor the prescribed condition report template requires any digital tool. CAV's own description of the process is procedural, not technological: 'A rental provider, property manager or agent will do a final inspection at the end of the rental agreement.' (https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/rent-bond-bills-and-condition-reports/bond/bond-claims-and-refunds). The closest quantified figure anywhere (Buildium's 67% of property managers using a digital inspection tool) is a US vendor survey and cannot support a claim about Melbourne.
    - searched: https://www.carpetsteammelbourne.com.au/end-of-lease-cleaning-checklist-victoria-2026-the-ultimate-bond-back-guide/ (the retelling; cites Consumer Affairs Victoria, Tenants Victoria, VCAT, RTBA and Wikipedia generally, but attaches no source at all to the inspection-tools sentence)
    - searched: web search: "high-resolution digital inspection tools" property managers Melbourne - returns only vendor marketing (Inspection Express, Property Inspection Manager, zInspector, Inspect365, Little Hinges, MRI Inspect), no measurement
    - searched: web search: Melbourne property managers adoption survey digital inspection software REIV statistics 2025 2026
    - searched: web search: REIV Real Estate Institute of Victoria property manager digital inspection technology adoption survey Melbourne - REIV publishes market insights and VicForms, no inspection-technology survey found
    - searched: web search: "digital inspection" OR "inspection app" adoption rate Victorian property managers rental condition report statistics government - nearest thing is Buildium's US-centric State of Property Management (67% of property managers use some digital inspection tool), which is a vendor survey and not Melbourne or Victoria specific
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/rent-bond-bills-and-condition-reports/condition-reports (Consumer Affairs Victoria condition report requirements)
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/rent-bond-bills-and-condition-reports/bond/bond-claims-and-refunds (Consumer Affairs Victoria final inspections)
    - searched: checked the authorised Residential Tenancies Act 1997 (v113) and CAV's prescribed condition report template for any requirement or measurement of digital/photographic inspection tooling
- **[echoed-claim]** Best Carpet Steam Cleaning Melbourne states that following its end of lease cleaning checklist is the most effective way to meet the legal 'reasonably clean' standard defined by Consumer Affairs Victoria.
  - as of: 2026-08-14
  - quote: "Following a professional end of lease cleaning checklist victoria 2026 is the most effective way to ensure you meet the legal “reasonably clean” standard defined by Consumer Affairs Victoria."
  - source: https://www.carpetsteammelbourne.com.au/end-of-lease-cleaning-checklist-victoria-2026-the-ultimate-bond-back-guide/
  - **A REAL SOURCE SAYS SOMETHING NARROWER** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - The standard is real but the attribution is wrong-ended. Parliament imposes it, not CAV: s 63 of the Residential Tenancies Act 1997 ('Renter must keep and leave rented premises reasonably clean') requires a renter to keep the premises in a reasonably clean condition and, at the end of the agreement, to leave them reasonably clean and in the same condition as when they entered into possession, fair wear and tear excepted; s 411AB(1)(c) lets a provider claim bond for the reasonable cost of cleaning 'if the premises were not left reasonably clean by the renter, having regard to the condition of the premises at the commencement of the residential rental agreement'. What CAV actually contributes is interpretation, under a power the Act gives it: s 486(fa) empowers the Director to issue guidelines, and the Director's Guideline 2 - Cleanliness (linked from CAV's own condition reports page) says VCAT 'must have regard to this guideline when determining an application under sections 211B and 452 of the Act regarding the cleanliness of the rented premises', and that cleanliness 'should be measured according to average standards in the community. It does not mean spotless or pristine'. So a renter-facing article is half right - there is an authoritative CAV document on what reasonably clean means - but the legal standard is statutory, the benchmark is the incoming condition report rather than any CAV checklist, and nothing in either instrument defines the standard by reference to a 'final inspection'. Worth noting against the source article's framing: reg 12 of the Residential Tenancies Regulations 2021 bars a professional-cleaning requirement at the end of a tenancy unless the premises were professionally cleaned immediately before it started or professional cleaning is needed to restore them to that condition.
    - source: "Renters and residents must keep the property in a reasonably clean condition."

## renter duties (9)

- **[echoed-claim]** Harcourts states that the purpose of routine inspections is not to check renter duties, such as whether tenants have made their beds.
  - as of: 2026-08-14
  - quote: "The purpose of Routine Inspections is not to check if the tenant's have made their beds or if all the dishes are put away, but to ensure that the property is maintained"
  - source: https://harcourts.net/au/office/trends/resources/news-and-media/routine-inspections-photos-landlord-attendance
  - **A REAL SOURCE SAYS SOMETHING NARROWER** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - A source exists but it does not draw the line Harcourts draws. The Act says nothing at all about the purpose or scope of a routine inspection beyond the bare ground in s 86(1)(f) - 'entry is required to enable inspection of the premises' - so the beds-and-dishes framing is an agency's customer-service gloss, not law. And the underlying premise is only half right. Cleanliness is a standing renter duty under s 63(1), quoted above, not merely an end-of-tenancy one, so an inspector is entitled to look at it. What protects the renter is the standard, not the subject matter. Director's Guideline 2, which VCAT must have regard to under s 486, sets it: 'Cleanliness should be measured according to average standards in the community. It does not mean spotless or pristine nor does it mean terribly messy; the standard should sit somewhere in the middle and will depend on the nature, age, and circumstances of the rented premises.' On that standard an unmade bed and dishes in the sink are indeed nothing; the Guideline's actual examples of falling short are cobwebs, dusty walls, dirty kitchen appliances, overgrown garden beds, vermin, hoarding and unregistered vehicles. There is also a structural reason to be careful with the claim: a general inspection under s 86(1)(f) is available once every 6 months on 7 days notice, whereas s 86(1)(e) allows entry on 24 hours notice where the provider 'has reasonable grounds to believe that the renter has failed to comply with the renter's duties'. Housekeeping is therefore not outside the inspection regime - it is the trigger for the other, faster entry ground.
    - source: "A renter must keep the rented premises in a reasonably clean condition except to the extent that the residential rental provider is responsible under this Act for keeping the premises in that condition."
      Victorian Government (Chief Parliamentary Counsel), Residential Tenancies Act 1997, authorised version 113 https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf - s 86(1)(e) and (f) (grounds for entry), s 63 (renter must keep and leave premises reasonably clean), s 63A, s 64
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/library/publications/housing-and-accommodation/renting/guideline-2--cleanliness.docx - Director's Guideline 2 (Cleanliness), issued under s 486, which VCAT must have regard to
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/rent-bond-bills-and-condition-reports/condition-reports - CAV's 'Cleaning responsibilities' section
    - searched: https://harcourts.net/au/office/trends/resources/news-and-media/routine-inspections-photos-landlord-attendance - the retelling itself

- **[echoed-claim]** Haven Agents states that new rental laws came into effect on 29 March 2021.
  - as of: 2021-03-29
  - quote: "New rental laws came into effect on 29 March 2021."
  - source: https://www.havenagents.com.au/residential-tenancies-act-changes-2021/
  - **BACKING FOUND** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - Backed twice over. The Regulations state their own commencement date in reg 3, quoted above, and the Act's endnotes record the matching commencement of the substantive reform - the Residential Tenancies Amendment Act 2018 (No. 45/2018) - as 'ss 4-5(3)(d), 5(3)(f)-(5), 6-34(5)(b), 35-53(3), 53(5)-122, 125-234, 236-322, 329-342(12), 342(15)-367, 380-386, Sch. 1 on 29.3.21: Special Gazette (No. 42) 27.1.21 p. 1'. Two caveats worth carrying. First, 29 March 2021 was not the original date: the reform package was passed in September 2018 and was to start on 1 July 2020, and the date slipped through the COVID-19 Omnibus (Emergency Measures) Act 2020 and its extension before being fixed by gazettal in January 2021. A handful of provisions commenced earlier (3 April 2019, 19 June 2019, 10 March 2021) and a few were never proclaimed at all, so 'the new rental laws started on 29 March 2021' is true of the package, not of every section. Second, the date is not the end of the story - CAV's own site now flags a further set of rental law changes effective 25 November 2025, so a 2021-dated agency explainer is no longer a safe statement of current law.
    - source: "These Regulations come into operation on 29 March 2021."
      Victorian Government (Chief Parliamentary Counsel), Residential Tenancies Regulations 2021, S.R. No. 3/2021 https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2021-04/21-003sra%20authorised.PDF
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2021-04/21-003sra%20authorised.PDF - Residential Tenancies Regulations 2021, reg 3 (Commencement)
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf - endnote table of amendments, commencement entry for the Residential Tenancies Amendment Act 2018 (No. 45/2018)
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/rent-bond-bills-and-condition-reports/condition-reports - CAV: 'If you sign a rental agreement from 29 March 2021, there are extra requirements for professional cleaning'
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/library/publications/housing-and-accommodation/renting/guideline-2--cleanliness.docx - Director's Guideline 2 uses the same date
    - searched: https://www.havenagents.com.au/residential-tenancies-act-changes-2021/ - the retelling itself
- **[echoed-claim]** Haven Agents states that a term in a rental agreement can only require professional cleaning if it is needed to return the property to the condition it was in at the start of the rental agreement, taking into account fair wear and tear.
  - as of: 2021-03-29
  - quote: "A term in a rental agreement can only require professional cleaning if it is needed to return the property to the condition it was in at the start of the rental agreement, taking into account fair wear and tear."
  - source: https://www.havenagents.com.au/residential-tenancies-act-changes-2021/
  - **BACKING FOUND** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - Backed, and unusually cleanly - the retelling is close to a paraphrase of reg 12(1)(b). Two things it leaves out are worth having. First, reg 12 has a second gateway the agency page omits: reg 12(1)(a) also permits a professional-cleaning requirement where professional cleaning 'was carried out to the rented premises immediately before the start of the tenancy and the renter was advised that professional cleaning... had been carried out'. Director's Guideline 2 reads 'immediately' strictly - cleaning done 'well in advance (for example, months or even years prior)' does not enable the requirement. Second, and this is the part the brief asked about: Tenants Victoria's line that a lease clause requiring professional cleaning does not bind the renter DOES have a primary source, and it is stronger than a policy position. s 27B(1)(d) makes it a prohibited term to include 'a term that requires all or part of the rented premises to be professionally cleaned at the end of the tenancy, unless that term is contained in the standard form', with s 27B(1)(e) doing the same for a term making the renter pay for it. s 26A(2) then supplies the consequence: 'If a residential rental agreement contains a prohibited term, that term is void and unenforceable.' Including one is itself an offence carrying 25 penalty units. So a bespoke professional-cleaning clause typed into a lease is not merely unfair, it is void; the only professional-cleaning obligation that can bite is the prescribed reg 12 term in the standard form, and even that only in the two circumstances reg 12(1)(a) and (b) allow. On carpets specifically, Director's Guideline 2 is explicit that steam cleaning sits above the statutory duty: 'A requirement to have the rented premises professionally cleaned, or cleaned to a professional standard, seeks to impose a requirement for cleanliness over and above what is required under the Act. This will often include all the examples listed above, as well as the steam cleaning of carpets.' It gives wine or urine stains in carpet as an example of when reg 12(1)(b) is satisfied - i.e. steam cleaning is owed on evidence of a specific detriment, not as a routine end-of-lease ritual.
    - source: "professional cleaning or cleaning to a professional standard is required to restore the rented premises to the same condition they were in immediately before the start of the tenancy, having regard to the condition report and taking into account fair wear and tear."
      Victorian Government (Chief Parliamentary Counsel), Residential Tenancies Regulations 2021, S.R. No. 3/2021 https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2021-04/21-003sra%20authorised.PDF
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2021-04/21-003sra%20authorised.PDF - Residential Tenancies Regulations 2021, reg 12 (Professional cleaning), the prescribed term for s 27C(1)(a) and (b)
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf - ss 26A, 27, 27B(1)(d) and (e), 27C
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/library/publications/housing-and-accommodation/renting/guideline-2--cleanliness.docx - Director's Guideline 2 (Cleanliness), s 486 guideline, sections 4.2 and 5
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/rent-bond-bills-and-condition-reports/condition-reports - CAV's 'Cleaning responsibilities' section
    - searched: https://www.havenagents.com.au/residential-tenancies-act-changes-2021/ - the retelling itself

- **[echoed-claim]** A renter must return the home to the state it was in when they first moved in, excluding fair wear and tear.
  - as of: 2026-06-03
  - quote: "You must return the home to the state it was in when you first moved in, excluding fair wear and tear."
  - quote: "hiring Best Carpet Steam Cleaning Melbourne ensures you meet every strict requirement without the stress"
  - source: https://www.carpetsteammelbourne.com.au/end-of-lease-cleaning-checklist-victoria-2026-the-ultimate-bond-back-guide/
  - **BACKING FOUND** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - Backed - this is s 63(2)(b), and CAV restates it in the same terms. But the retelling drops two qualifiers that do real work, and it drops them in the direction of the cleaning service that published it. The first is 'as far as practicable', which governs both limbs of s 63(2). The second is that s 63(2) has two limbs, not one: the renter must leave the premises '(a) reasonably clean; and (b) in the same condition as when the renter entered into possession'. Limb (b) is a condition test measured against the condition report, not a cleanliness standard, and limb (a) is expressly the 'reasonably clean' standard - which Director's Guideline 2 says 'does not mean spotless or pristine'. Neither limb imports a professional or steam-cleaning standard; the Guideline says such a requirement 'seeks to impose a requirement for cleanliness over and above what is required under the Act', and it can only be imposed at all through the prescribed reg 12 term. That is the load-bearing point here, because the page pairs the accurate legal statement with 'hiring Best Carpet Steam Cleaning Melbourne ensures you meet every strict requirement without the stress'. The Act's requirement is not strict in that sense, and meeting s 63(2) does not require hiring anyone. The page's own carpet paragraph gets closer to the law than its sales pitch does - it correctly identifies the reg 12(1)(a) gateway - but adds a written-notice element ('you were informed of this in writing') that reg 12(1)(a) does not contain; the regulation says the renter must have been 'advised', without prescribing the medium.
    - source: "in the same condition as when the renter entered into possession of the premises, taking into account fair wear and tear to the premises."
      Victorian Government (Chief Parliamentary Counsel), Residential Tenancies Act 1997, authorised version 113 https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf - s 63 (Renter must keep and leave rented premises reasonably clean), subsections (1) and (2)
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/rent-bond-bills-and-condition-reports/condition-reports - CAV states the same two-limb rule
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/library/publications/housing-and-accommodation/renting/guideline-2--cleanliness.docx - Director's Guideline 2 on 'reasonably clean' and on fair wear and tear
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2021-04/21-003sra%20authorised.PDF - reg 12, to check whether the duty imports a professional-cleaning standard
    - searched: https://www.carpetsteammelbourne.com.au/end-of-lease-cleaning-checklist-victoria-2026-the-ultimate-bond-back-guide/ - the retelling itself
- **[echoed-claim]** Best Carpet Steam Cleaning Melbourne states that cleaning issues account for nearly 70% of bond disputes in Victoria.
  - as of: 2026-06-03
  - quote: "Cleaning issues account for nearly 70% of bond disputes in Victoria."
  - quote: "hiring Best Carpet Steam Cleaning Melbourne ensures you meet every strict requirement without the stress"
  - source: https://www.carpetsteammelbourne.com.au/end-of-lease-cleaning-checklist-victoria-2026-the-ultimate-bond-back-guide/
  - **SEARCHED, NOTHING FOUND** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - No primary source, and the number carries three of the four unbacked-idea signals at once. It drifts within a single page: the same article says 'nearly 70%' in one place and 'up to 70% of the 53,000 bond disputes heard in Victoria recently' in another - 'nearly' and 'up to' are opposite hedges around the same figure, and 'recently' is doing the work a date should do. The denominator does not correspond to anything a Victorian body publishes. Roughly 50,000 is the order of magnitude of the whole VCAT Residential Tenancies List caseload, which is dominated by possession and rent-arrears applications, not bond disputes. Presenting the full list caseload as 'bond disputes' and then attributing 70% of it to cleaning would be a category error even if the 70% existed. And no regulator publishes the breakdown the claim needs. Bond claims are administered by the RTBA and contested claims are decided by VCAT; neither publishes bond outcomes coded by reason (cleaning / damage / arrears), so there is no dataset from which a 'share of bond disputes caused by cleaning' could have been computed. The chain here does not end in another blog - it ends nowhere at all. Weigh it accordingly: the publisher sells end-of-lease carpet steam cleaning, and the statistic's only function on the page is to make that purchase feel obligatory.
    - searched: https://www.carpetsteammelbourne.com.au/end-of-lease-cleaning-checklist-victoria-2026-the-ultimate-bond-back-guide/ - opened the page to find its citation. It carries no footnote, no link and no named source for the figure. Its only outbound links are Wikipedia's 'Leasehold estate' article and bare homepage links to Consumer Affairs Victoria and Tenants Victoria.
    - searched: Bing: "of bond disputes" cleaning Victoria rental statistic (en-AU) - returned dictionary entries, no source
    - searched: Bing: cleaning most common cause bond disputes Victoria RTBA statistics percentage (en-AU) - returned only commercial cleaning companies, no regulator data
    - searched: Bing: "bond disputes" Victoria cleaning "70%" RTBA statistics - no matching source
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/resources-and-tools - CAV's resources index; no research/statistics section that publishes bond outcomes
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/resources-and-tools/research - HTTP 404
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/resources-and-tools/research/rental-report - HTTP 404
    - searched: https://www.vcat.vic.gov.au/ - no link to statistics or a caseload breakdown from the homepage
    - searched: https://www.vcat.vic.gov.au/about-vcat/annual-reports - HTTP 404
    - searched: https://www.vcat.vic.gov.au/about-vcat/reports-and-publications - HTTP 404
    - searched: https://rentalbonds.vic.gov.au/ - RTBA site, TLS certificate chain error, not reachable

## rent bidding (8)

- **[echoed-claim]** Soho Real Estate states that tenants in Victoria can still offer more than the advertised rent voluntarily.
  - as of: 2026-03-16
  - quote: "It’s illegal for agents to ask for higher offers in some states like NSW and Victoria, but tenants can still offer more voluntarily."
  - source: https://www.soho.ai/articles/rental-bidding-what-is-it-and-what-do-you-need-to-know
  - **A PRIMARY SOURCE SAYS THE OPPOSITE** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - Section 30F(4) closes exactly the loophole Soho describes: a provider or agent 'must not accept an unsolicited or otherwise uninvited offer' of rent above the advertised amount, penalty 60 penalty units (300 for a body corporate). The subsection was inserted by the Consumer and Planning Legislation Amendment (Housing Statement Reform) Act 2025 (No. 6/2025) s 7, and the Act's own endnote records the commencement as 'ss 3-51 on 25.11.25'. So the claim was true in Victoria for a defined window - 29 March 2021 to 24 November 2025 - and has been false since. Soho's page carries a dateModified of 16 March 2026, four months after the change. Two precision points the reteller misses in both directions: the prohibition binds the provider and the agent, not the renter (a renter who offers more commits no offence, they simply cannot lawfully have it accepted), and the same amending Act inserted s 40(3), which bars accepting an unsolicited offer of more than one month's rent in advance - the other half of the same manoeuvre. Lumping Victoria in with NSW is what carries the error: the two states diverged on 25 November 2025.
    - source: "uninvited offer of an amount of rent that is higher than the advertised amount of rent for the rented premises."
      Victorian Government (authorised version, Chief Parliamentary Counsel) https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf (Part 2 Division 1C 'Rental auctions prohibited', s 30F(3) and s 30F(4); Endnote 3 Table of Amendments for the commencement of Act No. 6/2025)
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/new-changes-to-the-rental-laws (CAV's dated table of reforms)
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/starting-and-changing-rental-agreements/applying-signing-and-moving-in/applying-for-a-property (CAV 'Pricing rules for advertised rental properties')
    - searched: https://www.legislation.vic.gov.au/in-force/acts/residential-tenancies-act-1997/113
    - searched: https://www.soho.ai/articles/rental-bidding-what-is-it-and-what-do-you-need-to-know (the retelling: audited for citations - it links to no Victorian government source at all; schema dateModified 2026-03-16)
- **[echoed-claim]** RentBetter states that rent bidding is prohibited in Victoria under new rental laws that came into effect in 2021.
  - as of: 2026-08-11
  - quote: "New rental laws came into effect in 2021 and rent bidding is prohibited in Victoria."
  - source: https://rentbetter.com.au/article/understanding-rent-bidding
  - **A REAL SOURCE SAYS SOMETHING NARROWER** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - This is the conflation that runs through almost every industry page on the subject: there are two dates, not one. What commenced on 29 March 2021 was Part 2 Division 1C 'Rental auctions prohibited' (ss 30F and 30G, inserted by the Residential Tenancies Amendment Act 2018 No. 45/2018 s 21 - the Act's endnote lists 'ss 6-34(5)(b)... on 29.3.21'). That gave two prohibitions: advertise or offer only at a fixed amount (s 30F(1)), and do not 'solicit or otherwise invite' a higher offer (s 30F(3)). Accepting an offer the renter made unprompted stayed lawful. The accepting limb, s 30F(4), arrived with Act No. 6/2025 s 7 and commenced 25 November 2025 - which is why CAV files it under 'Changes now in effect' against the date 25 November 2025 and heads the row 'Ban on all types of rental bidding', noting it 'builds on the existing law'. So 'rent bidding is prohibited in Victoria' is true today; attributing that state of affairs to the 2021 laws is not. Ambiguous rather than contradicted because half the sentence is right, and the half that is wrong is a date, not a rule. RentBetter's supporting link points at CAV's now-deleted 2021 rental law changes page, so the citation chain for the 2021 framing is dead as well as out of date.
    - source: "Agents and rental providers will be banned from accepting offers made by a renter to pay rent higher than the advertised amount"
      Consumer Affairs Victoria https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/new-changes-to-the-rental-laws
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf (Division 1C inserted by Act No. 45/2018 s 21; Endnote 3 commencement table for both No. 45/2018 and No. 6/2025)
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/new-changes-to-the-rental-laws
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/starting-and-changing-rental-agreements/applying-signing-and-moving-in/applying-for-a-property
    - searched: https://rentbetter.com.au/article/understanding-rent-bidding (the retelling; its only government citation is https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/2021-victorian-rental-laws-changes, which now returns HTTP 404)
    - searched: https://www.legislation.vic.gov.au/in-force/acts/residential-tenancies-act-1997
- **[echoed-claim]** The Real Estate Institute of Victoria stated that all Victorian residential rental properties must be advertised at a fixed price.
  - as of: 2024-02-05
  - quote: "The REIV also notes that all Victorian residential rental properties must be advertised at a fixed price. It is unlawful to advertise rental properties with a price range or initiate rental bidding."
  - source: https://reiv.com.au/our-industry/news/reiv-issues-reminder-to-members-on-rental-price-advertising-and-the-rent-review-process
  - **BACKING FOUND** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - Both limbs check out. The price-range prohibition follows from s 30F(1) (rent must be advertised or offered 'as a fixed amount'), and CAV states it directly in its list of unlawful conduct: 'advertise a property with a price range'. The initiating limb is s 30F(3), quoted here, which has been in force since 29 March 2021 and sits under the Division heading 'Division 1C—Rental auctions prohibited'. Note the word REIV chose - 'initiate' - is the accurate one for the 2021 law and, unlike most of the pages in this set, does not overclaim into accepting; the accepting prohibition came separately in s 30F(4) on 25 November 2025.
    - source: "must not solicit or otherwise invite an offer of an amount of rent that is higher than the advertised amount of rent for the rented premises."
      Victorian Government (authorised version, Chief Parliamentary Counsel) https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf

- **[echoed-claim]** McNeill Real Estate told rental providers that owners and agents cannot accept rent above the advertised price.
  - as of: 2025-11-13
  - quote: "Owners and agents cannot accept rent above the advertised price"
  - source: https://www.mcneillrealestate.com.au/post/Victorian-Rental-Rule-Changes-from-25-November-2025
  - **BACKING FOUND** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - Correct as at the date it was written for, and the date matters: s 30F(4) commenced 25 November 2025, so McNeill's statement is right from that day and would have been wrong the week before. CAV's page now collapses all three verbs into one line - 'ask for, invite or accept offers of, rent higher than the advertised price' - which is why later retellings can no longer see the seam between the 2021 and 2025 rules. The claim is a rare case of an agency page getting the accepting limb right; it just backs it with a Herald Sun link rather than the Act, so the chain would have run into a newspaper if it had not happened to be accurate.
    - source: "ask for, invite or accept offers of, rent higher than the advertised price"
      Consumer Affairs Victoria https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/starting-and-changing-rental-agreements/applying-signing-and-moving-in/applying-for-a-property
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/starting-and-changing-rental-agreements/applying-signing-and-moving-in/applying-for-a-property
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf (s 30F(4), inserted by No. 6/2025 s 7; Endnote 3 commencement 'ss 3-51 on 25.11.25')
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/new-changes-to-the-rental-laws (row dated 25 November 2025)
    - searched: https://www.mcneillrealestate.com.au/post/Victorian-Rental-Rule-Changes-from-25-November-2025 (the retelling, posted 13 Nov 2025 - it sources this point to the Herald Sun, a newspaper, not to the Act or CAV)
- **[echoed-claim]** Forge Property advises renters not to offer rent above the advertised price.
  - as of: 2026-02-11
  - quote: "Do not offer rent above the advertised price or propose side deals"
  - source: https://www.forgeproperty.com.au/en-au/media/how-can-you-actually-secure-a-rental-in-melbournes-2026-market
  - **BACKING FOUND** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - 'Side deals' is vague phrasing, but the two manoeuvres it covers in this market both have statutory answers. Offering extra rent in advance: s 40(1) bars a provider from soliciting or inviting more than one month's rent in advance, and s 40(3) - inserted by the same Act No. 6/2025 (s 8(2)) that produced s 30F(4), commencing 25 November 2025 - bars accepting an unsolicited offer of it; s 41 tightens the limit to two weeks for weekly agreements. Offering more rent: s 30F(3) and (4). CAV's page states both prohibitions in the same list. As with the previous finding, the offences bind the provider and agent rather than the applicant, so the advice is practical rather than protective of the renter from prosecution: a side deal cannot be lawfully accepted, so proposing one buys nothing and marks the applicant as someone inviting the agent to commit an offence.
    - source: "ask for, invite or accept more than one month's rent in advance"
      Consumer Affairs Victoria https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/starting-and-changing-rental-agreements/applying-signing-and-moving-in/applying-for-a-property
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/starting-and-changing-rental-agreements/applying-signing-and-moving-in/applying-for-a-property (CAV's list of unlawful pricing conduct, including rent in advance)
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf (ss 40(1), 40(3) and 41 - limits on rent in advance; s 30F(3)-(4))
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/new-changes-to-the-rental-laws (the 25 November 2025 row covers advance rent as well as higher offers)
    - searched: https://www.forgeproperty.com.au/en-au/media/how-can-you-actually-secure-a-rental-in-melbournes-2026-market

## subletting (8)

- **[echoed-claim]** LegalVision asserts that landlords cannot rely on the Residential Tenancies Act provisions prohibiting subletting without consent when trying to evict tenants who list their properties on Airbnb.
  - as of: 2016-05-02
  - quote: "The VCAT decision means that landlords should be careful about trying to evict tenants who list their properties on Airbnb, on the basis that they do not have permission to sub-lease. As the matter currently stands, you cannot rely on the provisions in the RTA which prohibit subletting without consent."
  - source: https://legalvision.com.au/vcat-considers-airbnb-a-licence-not-a-sub-lease/
  - **A PRIMARY SOURCE SAYS THE OPPOSITE** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - The retelling rests on a ruling that was reversed five weeks after LegalVision published, and the tribunal itself now teaches the opposite. LegalVision's article is dated 2 May 2016 and describes VCAT's decision of 24 March 2016 in Swan v Uecker (VCAT proceeding R2016/4286). On 10 June 2016 Croft J in the Supreme Court of Victoria granted leave, allowed the appeal and disposed of the matter himself, holding at [75]: 'For the preceding reasons, I am of the opinion that the AirBnB Agreement for occupation of the whole of the Apartment is properly to be characterised as a lease between the Respondents, the tenants, and the AirBnB guests for the period of occupation agreed between them. It follows that their entering into this Agreement is, having regard to their own tenancy of the Apartment, a sub-lease.' The backing quote is VCAT's own summary of that holding, published by the tribunal in 'Useful Supreme Court and VCAT decisions about renting', a compendium whose preamble says 'VCAT is bound to follow decisions of the Supreme Court that are directly on point' - so the tribunal that produced the decision LegalVision relies on now publishes the appellate reversal as the governing law. I could not cite the judgment itself because no host serving it answers an automated fetch: AustLII and CommonLII return 403, the Wayback snapshot 503s and is refused by WebFetch, Jade and CaseMine serve JavaScript shells, and the Supreme Court's own site does not host 2016 judgments. The provisions LegalVision says landlords 'cannot rely on' were renumbered, not repealed, by the Residential Tenancies Amendment Act 2018: s 253 (the section in issue in Swan v Uecker, and the one the VCAT compendium still cites) is now s 91ZV, which lets a rental provider give a notice to vacate where a renter 'has assigned or sub-let or purported to assign or sub-let the whole or any part of the premises without the residential rental provider's consent', and s 81(1) still requires written consent. Ten years on, the LegalVision page carries no correction.
    - source: "The AirBnB agreement for occupation of the whole of the apartment was properly to be characterised as a lease between the tenants and the AirBnB guests for the period of occupation agreed between them. It followed that the tenants had entered into a sub-lease in breach of their agreement with the landlord."
      Victorian Civil and Administrative Tribunal https://www.vcat.vic.gov.au/media/195/download
    - searched: https://legalvision.com.au/vcat-considers-airbnb-a-licence-not-a-sub-lease/ (the retelling; fetched 2026-08-15, HTTP 200, schema.org datePublished 2016-05-02T09:36:57+00:00 and no dateModified - the sentence 'you cannot rely on the provisions in the RTA which prohibit subletting without consent' is still on the live page)
    - searched: https://www.austlii.edu.au/cgi-bin/viewdoc/au/cases/vic/VSC/2016/313.html (Swan v Uecker [2016] VSC 313) - HTTP 403 Cloudflare to curl AND to WebFetch, so it could not be used as backing.url
    - searched: https://www8.austlii.edu.au/cgi-bin/viewdoc/au/cases/vic/VSC/2016/313.html, https://classic.austlii.edu.au/au/cases/vic/VSC/2016/313.html, https://www.austlii.edu.au/au/cases/vic/VSC/2016/313.html, http://www.austlii.edu.au/cgi-bin/sinodisp/au/cases/vic/VSC/2016/313.html - all HTTP 403
    - searched: http://www.commonlii.org/au/cases/vic/VSC/2016/313.html and https://www.commonlii.org/au/cases/vic/VSC/2016/313.html (AustLII sister site) - HTTP 403
    - searched: https://web.archive.org/web/20251116040611/http://www6.austlii.edu.au/cgi-bin/viewdoc/au/cases/vic/VSC/2016/313.html - a snapshot exists (confirmed via http://archive.org/wayback/available) but web.archive.org returns HTTP 503 to curl from here and WebFetch refuses the host outright
    - searched: https://jade.io/search/?query=Swan%20v%20Uecker and https://jade.io/j/?a=outline&id=470979 - HTTP 200 but a ~9KB JavaScript shell with no judgment text in the served HTML; https://www.casemine.com/search/au?q=%22Swan+v+Uecker%22 likewise renders results client-side
    - searched: https://www.supremecourt.vic.gov.au/search?query=Swan%20v%20Uecker (client-side search, serves 'Enter in some keywords above'); /law-and-practice/judgments-and-sentences, /legal-resources/about-decisions, /legal-resources/judgment-summaries and /search/node?keys=uecker all HTTP 404; judgments.vic.gov.au does not resolve
    - searched: https://www.vcat.vic.gov.au/sitemap.xml - enumerated, which surfaced https://www.vcat.vic.gov.au/documents/useful-supreme-court-and-vcat-decisions-about-renting and its PDF at https://www.vcat.vic.gov.au/media/195/download (88 pages, downloaded; the tribunal's own case-summary compendium, which carries Swan v Uecker at pp 35-36 under the heading '14 day notice to vacate - assigning or subletting without consent'). Quote checked for contiguity under both pdftotext and pdftotext -layout
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/sitemap.xml then https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/starting-and-changing-rental-agreements/different-rental-agreements/subletting (CAV, last updated 7 December 2025): 'If someone sub-lets without permission, the rental provider can end the rental agreement. They can ask any of the people living at the property to leave within 14 days.'
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf (authorised RTA 1997 version 113, as at 1 July 2026 - downloaded, md5 verified against the live file, ss 81 and 91ZV read in both the plain and -layout pdftotext extractions)
- **[echoed-claim]** LegalVision asserts, regarding subletting, that Victorian tenants are able to let others stay on the premises short-term and charge a fee, as long as it is not a sub-lease.
  - as of: 2016-05-02
  - quote: "The VCAT decision has been considered good news for tenants wanting to earn some extra cash through Airbnb. Tenants are able to let others stay on the premises short-term and charge a fee, as long as it is not a sub-lease."
  - source: https://legalvision.com.au/vcat-considers-airbnb-a-licence-not-a-sub-lease/
  - **A PRIMARY SOURCE SAYS THE OPPOSITE** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - Read in isolation the sentence is a tautology - of course you may do a thing so long as it is not the prohibited thing - but LegalVision offers it as the practical pay-off of the VCAT ruling, i.e. that paying short-stay guests are not sub-renters. That premise is exactly what the Supreme Court reversed, and the backing quote is VCAT's own summary of why: the short duration of the stays did not matter, because the quality of the occupancy 'was not akin to that of a "lodger" or an hotel guest' but 'the possession that would be expected of residential accommodation generally' (VCAT's summary of [46]). The Court also held that the tenants' ability to evict an overstaying guest and their retention of the flat as their principal residence were both irrelevant to exclusive possession - the two facts a renter would most naturally rely on for comfort. CAV's definition of a sub-let describes the same arrangement in terms: 'When a property is sub-let, someone rents part or all of the property from another person who rented it directly from the rental provider'. One live qualification is worth recording rather than flattening: exclusive possession remains the legal test, so a genuinely shared arrangement (a renter staying put while a guest has run of a room but not the premises) can still be a licence. The error is presenting whole-of-premises short-stay letting as safe.
    - source: "The tenants had, by means of the AirBnB agreement, effectively and practically passed their right of exclusive occupation, with all its qualities, to their AirBnB guests for the agreed period under the AirBnB agreement."
      Victorian Civil and Administrative Tribunal https://www.vcat.vic.gov.au/media/195/download
    - searched: https://legalvision.com.au/vcat-considers-airbnb-a-licence-not-a-sub-lease/ (the retelling, still live, published 2016-05-02)
    - searched: https://www.vcat.vic.gov.au/documents/useful-supreme-court-and-vcat-decisions-about-renting and the PDF it links, https://www.vcat.vic.gov.au/media/195/download (VCAT's case-summary compendium; the Swan v Uecker entry sets out the Court's reasoning on exclusive possession at paragraphs 40-46 of the judgment)
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/starting-and-changing-rental-agreements/different-rental-agreements/subletting (CAV's definition of a sub-let: 'When a property is sub-let, someone rents part or all of the property from another person who rented it directly from the rental provider'; last updated 7 December 2025)
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/starting-and-changing-rental-agreements/people-moving-in-and-out/ending-a-sublet (checked for any short-stay carve-out - there is none)
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/sitemap.xml - searched every CAV URL for 'short', 'airbnb' and 'sublet'. The only short-stay pages are owners-corporations pages (/housing/owners-corporations/rules/making-rules-to-ban-short-stay-accommodation and .../short-stay-accommodation-laws-to-prevent-unruly-parties); CAV publishes nothing telling renters they may take paying guests
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf - grepped the authorised Act for every occurrence of 'sub-let' (ss 81, 82, 91G, 91ZV, 207E, 207ZD and the Part 4A equivalents). There is no exception for short stays, for fee-charging, or for arrangements the parties label a licence
    - searched: Swan v Uecker [2016] VSC 313 itself sought at austlii.edu.au, www8.austlii.edu.au, classic.austlii.edu.au and commonlii.org (all HTTP 403), web.archive.org (HTTP 503 to curl, host refused by WebFetch), jade.io and casemine.com (JavaScript shells), supremecourt.vic.gov.au (no 2016 judgments hosted)
- **[echoed-claim]** Property Managers Melbourne asserted that VCAT ruled the rooms used as an Airbnb were a licensing issue.
  - as of: 2026-08-14
  - quote: "This case was heard at the Supreme Court of Victoria after the Victorian Civil and Administrative Tribunal (VCAT) had ruled in favour of the tenants. VCAT had decided that the rooms used as an Airbnb were a licensing issue and not a sublease. This ruling was concluded because the Airbnb guests did not have exclusive use of the property."
  - source: https://www.propertymanagersmelb.com.au/can-tenants-use-your-property-as-an-airbnb/
  - **BACKING FOUND** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - Accurate as a description of what VCAT decided, and the tribunal's own compendium says it in the same terms. Croft J records the same at [28] of the Supreme Court judgment: 'as a result of the finding that the AirBnB guests did not have exclusive possession of the Apartment, the Tribunal concluded that the nature of the legal relationship between the Respondents and those guests was a licence to occupy, rather than a lease.' Two wrinkles the retelling introduces. It says 'the rooms used as an Airbnb', but the arrangement was for the whole of a two-bedroom St Kilda apartment - which is precisely why exclusive possession was made out on appeal, and 'rooms' would leave a reader with the wrong test. And, more importantly, the ruling being described has had no legal force since 10 June 2016: the Supreme Court held that VCAT's finding was unsupported by evidence and that VCAT had taken irrelevant matters into account. The page does mention the appeal in the same paragraph, so this is a matter of emphasis rather than a false statement - but a landlord skim-reading it would take away the tribunal's reasoning as the law when the appellate reversal is the law.
    - source: "VCAT dismissed the application, finding the AirBnB guests did not have exclusive possession of the rented premises and that the tenants had granted licences only to the AirBnB guests, not leases."
      Victorian Civil and Administrative Tribunal https://www.vcat.vic.gov.au/media/195/download
    - searched: https://www.propertymanagersmelb.com.au/can-tenants-use-your-property-as-an-airbnb/ (the retelling)
    - searched: https://www.vcat.vic.gov.au/sitemap.xml, then https://www.vcat.vic.gov.au/documents/useful-supreme-court-and-vcat-decisions-about-renting, then the PDF at https://www.vcat.vic.gov.au/media/195/download - VCAT's own account of what the tribunal decided at first instance and why. Quote verified contiguous under both pdftotext and pdftotext -layout

Not reachable

- **[echoed-claim]** Property Managers Melbourne asserted that VCAT ruled the rooms used as an Airbnb were not a sublease.
  - as of: 2026-08-14
  - quote: "This case was heard at the Supreme Court of Victoria after the Victorian Civil and Administrative Tribunal (VCAT) had ruled in favour of the tenants. VCAT had decided that the rooms used as an Airbnb were a licensing issue and not a sublease. This ruling was concluded because the Airbnb guests did not have exclusive use of the property."
  - source: https://www.propertymanagersmelb.com.au/can-tenants-use-your-property-as-an-airbnb/
  - **BACKING FOUND** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - Accurate as a description of what VCAT decided, and the tribunal's own compendium says it in the same terms. Croft J records the same at [28] of the Supreme Court judgment: 'as a result of the finding that the AirBnB guests did not have exclusive possession of the Apartment, the Tribunal concluded that the nature of the legal relationship between the Respondents and those guests was a licence to occupy, rather than a lease.' Two wrinkles the retelling introduces. It says 'the rooms used as an Airbnb', but the arrangement was for the whole of a two-bedroom St Kilda apartment - which is precisely why exclusive possession was made out on appeal, and 'rooms' would leave a reader with the wrong test. And, more importantly, the ruling being described has had no legal force since 10 June 2016: the Supreme Court held that VCAT's finding was unsupported by evidence and that VCAT had taken irrelevant matters into account. The page does mention the appeal in the same paragraph, so this is a matter of emphasis rather than a false statement - but a landlord skim-reading it would take away the tribunal's reasoning as the law when the appellate reversal is the law.
    - source: "VCAT dismissed the application, finding the AirBnB guests did not have exclusive possession of the rented premises and that the tenants had granted licences only to the AirBnB guests, not leases."
      Victorian Civil and Administrative Tribunal https://www.vcat.vic.gov.au/media/195/download
    - searched: https://www.propertymanagersmelb.com.au/can-tenants-use-your-property-as-an-airbnb/ (the retelling)
    - searched: https://www.vcat.vic.gov.au/sitemap.xml, then https://www.vcat.vic.gov.au/documents/useful-supreme-court-and-vcat-decisions-about-renting, then the PDF at https://www.vcat.vic.gov.au/media/195/download - VCAT's own account of what the tribunal decided at first instance and why. Quote verified contiguous under both pdftotext and pdftotext -layout
