- **[echoed-claim]** Nelson Alexander asserts that the law surrounding sub-leasing in Victoria is clear.
  - as of: 2026-07-01
  - quote: "At the moment, the law surrounding sub-leasing is clear."
  - source: https://www.nelsonalexander.com.au/news/landlords-can-victorian-tenants-use-property-airbnb/
  - **BACKING FOUND** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - The consent rule itself is clear and primary-sourced. s 81(1) of the authorised Residential Tenancies Act 1997 (version 113) provides that a renter 'must not assign or sub-let the whole or any part of the rented premises without the residential rental provider's written consent', s 81(2) that consent must not be unreasonably withheld, and s 81(3) that a sub-letting without consent is invalid unless the Tribunal has determined consent is not required. CAV restates all three. Two caveats. The wording Nelson Alexander quotes as CAV's ('a tenant must get written permission from the landlord before sub-letting') is the pre-2021 text and is no longer published by CAV - 'tenant' and 'landlord' were replaced by 'renter' and 'rental provider' by the Residential Tenancies Amendment Act 2018 from 29 March 2021 - so the quotation marks sit around words the regulator does not currently use. And 'the law surrounding sub-leasing is clear' is asserted in an article whose entire subject is the one question on which it demonstrably was not clear: whether a short-stay letting is a sub-lease at all, on which VCAT and the Supreme Court reached opposite answers within eleven weeks in 2016. The page concedes as much two paragraphs later ('the law was yet to be adjusted to accommodate AirBnB arrangements'). Backed on the rule, self-contradicting on the framing.
    - source: "Renters must ask for written permission from the rental provider to sub-let."
      Consumer Affairs Victoria https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/starting-and-changing-rental-agreements/different-rental-agreements/subletting
    - searched: https://www.nelsonalexander.com.au/news/landlords-can-victorian-tenants-use-property-airbnb/ (the retelling; it attributes the rule to Consumer Affairs Victoria and quotes 'a tenant must get written permission from the landlord before sub-letting')
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/starting-and-changing-rental-agreements/different-rental-agreements/subletting (CAV, last updated 7 December 2025 - the current wording of the same rule)
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/sitemap.xml (enumerated to confirm this is the page the rule now lives on and that the older 'tenant/landlord' wording Nelson Alexander puts in quotation marks is no longer anywhere on the CAV site)
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf - s 81 'Assignment and sub-letting by a renter' in the authorised Act (version 113, as at 1 July 2026), read in both the plain and -layout pdftotext extractions
    - searched: https://www.vcat.vic.gov.au/media/195/download (VCAT case-summary compendium - checked whether the tribunal treats the sub-letting rule as settled; it states the s 253/91ZV notice-to-vacate ground without qualification)

## condition report (7)

- **[echoed-claim]** Landlord Wise asserted that for a self-managing landlord in Victoria, the condition report is the single most important document they will rely on when a tenancy ends.
  - as of: 2026-08-10
  - quote: "If you’re a self-managing landlord in Victoria, the condition report is the single most important document you’ll rely on when your tenancy ends. It’s the foundation of every bond claim, every cleaning dispute, and every damage argument at VCAT."
  - source: https://landlordwise.com.au/guides/condition-report-vic/
  - **SEARCHED, NOTHING FOUND** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - This is a superlative, not a proposition a regulator can confirm or deny, and no primary source ranks documents by importance. The closest primary statement is Consumer Affairs Victoria's own hedge - condition reports 'can be important evidence' - and CAV in the same breath tells both parties to take photos, which is the opposite of a single-document story. The Act gives the report a specific and limited job (s 36: evidence of state of repair and general condition at the start), not a general evidentiary primacy. While tracing this I settled the section confusion flagged in the brief. The Act carries four parallel condition-report sections, one per tenure type, and they are not alternatives for the same tenancy: s 35 sits in 'Part 2 - Residential tenancies - residential rental agreements'; s 97 is the rooming-house version ('a rooming house operator must give the resident or proposed resident 2 copies of a condition report'); s 148 is the caravan-park version ('a caravan park owner or a caravan owner'); s 206O is the Part 4A site version ('a site owner must give the site tenant'). For an ordinary Victorian rental the governing section is s 35, full stop. The reason so many pages name all four (or pick the wrong one) is almost certainly CAV's own page, which ends with 'Condition reports are covered by these sections of the Residential Tenancies Act 1997: Section 35, Section 97, Section 148, Section 206O' without saying which applies to which tenure. CAV's prescribed template settles it from the other direction: the form is headed 'Residential Tenancies Act 1997 Section 35(1B)' and 'Residential Tenancies Regulations 2021 Regulation 18'.
    - searched: WebSearch: Residential Tenancies Regulations 2021 Victoria condition report prescribed form regulation legislation.vic.gov.au
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf - read ss 35, 35A and 36 in full, and ss 97, 98, 148, 206O, 206P for comparison. Nothing in the Act ranks the condition report against any other document.
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting - navigated to find the CAV condition report page
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/rent-bond-bills-and-condition-reports - section index
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/rent-bond-bills-and-condition-reports/condition-reports - CAV's own page; its strongest statement is 'Condition reports can be important evidence if there's a dispute about bond claims, or who should pay for cleaning or fixing damage'
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/starting-a-rental-agreement-lease/condition-reports - HTTP 404 (guessed path)
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/starting-and-changing-rental-agreements/condition-reports - HTTP 404 (guessed path)
    - searched: https://landlordwise.com.au/guides/condition-report-vic/ - HTTP 403 to a fetcher; the retelling itself could not be re-opened to inspect its citations
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/library/forms/housing-and-accommodation/renting/condition-report-word.docx - CAV's prescribed template
- **[echoed-claim]** Landlord Wise asserts that a Victorian condition report cannot be completed using a generic template.
  - as of: 2026-08-10
  - quote: "You cannot use a generic template, your own spreadsheet, or an app that doesn’t follow the prescribed format."
  - source: https://landlordwise.com.au/guides/condition-report-vic/
  - **A REAL SOURCE SAYS SOMETHING NARROWER** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - RE-STATUSED contradicted -> ambiguous on audit (2026-08-17). The legal test in reg 18 is a content test, so the tracer is right that a spreadsheet or app carrying Form 4's information satisfies s 35(1B) - but Consumer Affairs Victoria's own live condition-reports page instructs the opposite in plain words, and the source sentence's trailing qualifier ('that doesn't follow the prescribed format') reads naturally as governing all three items. Publishing 'contradicted' would brand a source wrong for repeating the regulator's own instruction. The real defect to name: Landlord Wise hardens CAV's plain-English 'use the template' into a prohibition on the medium, which reg 18 does not impose. [right-error-wrong-scope] Evidence: "The report must be created using the Condition report template (Word, 187KB)." https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/rent-bond-bills-and-condition-reports/condition-reports ORIGINAL TRACE NOTE, kept because it usually found something real: The regulation that defines 'prescribed form' makes it an information test, not a document test: a report is in the prescribed form 'if it contains the information in Form 4'. On that wording a spreadsheet, a bespoke template or a third-party app all satisfy s 35(1B) provided they carry Form 4's information - which is exactly what every commercial inspection app in this market is built to do. So two of the three things Landlord Wise says you cannot use, you can. The third limb is true but circular: an app 'that doesn't follow the prescribed format' is by definition non-compliant. Splitting the sentence this way matters because the false half is the half that sells software. The likely origin of the error is CAV's page, which says the report 'must be created using the Condition report template' - a plain-English instruction to consumers, not the legal test. The legal test is reg 18, and it is looser than CAV's phrasing. Nothing in s 35 or reg 18 restricts the medium; s 35(1A) goes the other way and deems an electronic copy to satisfy the two-copy duty.
    - source: "For the purposes of section 35(1B) of the Act, a condition report is in the prescribed form if it contains the information in Form 4 in Schedule 1."
      Victorian Government (Chief Parliamentary Counsel), Residential Tenancies Regulations 2021, S.R. No. 3/2021 https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2021-04/21-003sra%20authorised.PDF
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2021-04/21-003sra%20authorised.PDF - reg 18, the provision that defines what 'prescribed form' means for s 35(1B)
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf - s 35(1B) itself imposes no requirement as to medium; s 35(1A) expressly contemplates an electronic copy
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/library/forms/housing-and-accommodation/renting/condition-report-word.docx - the CAV template is a Word document, i.e. an editable container for the Form 4 content, not a controlled artefact
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/rent-bond-bills-and-condition-reports/condition-reports - CAV says 'The report must be created using the Condition report template (Word, 187KB)', which is the wording the retelling has hardened into a prohibition
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/rent-bond-bills-and-condition-reports/condition-reports (audit re-check 2026-08-17)

- **[echoed-claim]** A signed Victorian condition report matters more than photos alone.
  - as of: 2026-08-10
  - quote: "This is why the condition report matters more than photos alone. Photos are helpful supporting evidence, but a signed condition report is legally conclusive."
  - source: https://landlordwise.com.au/guides/condition-report-vic/
  - **A REAL SOURCE SAYS SOMETHING NARROWER** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - There is a real provision behind this, but it does not say what the retelling says. Four qualifications sit inside and beside s 36(1), all of them dropped by 'legally conclusive': 1. It is conclusive 'for the purposes of this Act' only - not at large.

2. It is conclusive only as to 'the state of repair or general condition' on the day specified in the report. It proves the starting condition, not who caused any later change, not the cost of repair, and not whether something is fair wear and tear.
3. It is conclusive only if signed by or on behalf of BOTH parties. The single most common real-world case - the renter never returns their copy within the 5 business days s 35(2) allows - falls outside s 36(1) entirely.
4. s 36(2) carves out three classes of statement: a condition 'that could not reasonably have been discovered on a reasonable inspection of the premises'; any statement the renter has recorded a written disagreement with; and any statement the rental provider disagreed with in writing where the renter completed the report first. The comparative half of the claim - that the report therefore matters more than photos - has no primary source at all, and both CAV and the prescribed form push the other way. Form 4 itself instructs 'Take photos that show the condition of items, fixtures and fittings', and CAV says 'It is a good idea for both the renter and the rental provider to take photos of the property at the start of the rental agreement'. Director's Guideline 2, which VCAT must have regard to under s 486, describes them working together rather than in a hierarchy: 'VCAT relies on the condition report and any photos or video taken to determine the condition of the premises and whether they were ‘reasonably clean’ at the start of the tenancy.' Photos are also how a party discharges the s 36(2)(a) point about defects not reasonably discoverable on inspection - so in the cases where the report is NOT conclusive, photos are what is left.
   - source: "A statement in a condition report under section 35 is conclusive evidence, for the purposes of this Act, of the state of repair or general condition of the rented premises on the day specified in the report if the condition report is signed by or on behalf of the residential rental provider and the renter."
     Victorian Government (Chief Parliamentary Counsel), Residential Tenancies Act 1997, authorised version 113 https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf
   - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf - s 36 in full, including the s 36(2) exceptions and s 36(1A)
   - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/rent-bond-bills-and-condition-reports/condition-reports - CAV's framing is 'Condition reports can be important evidence', and it advises photos in addition
   - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/library/publications/housing-and-accommodation/renting/guideline-2--cleanliness.docx - Director's Guideline 2 (Cleanliness), issued under s 486, on how VCAT weighs the report against photos
   - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2021-04/21-003sra%20authorised.PDF - Form 4 tells the parties to take photos and attach them

- **[echoed-claim]** A signed Victorian condition report is legally conclusive.
  - as of: 2026-08-10
  - quote: "This is why the condition report matters more than photos alone. Photos are helpful supporting evidence, but a signed condition report is legally conclusive."
  - source: https://landlordwise.com.au/guides/condition-report-vic/
  - **A REAL SOURCE SAYS SOMETHING NARROWER** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - There is a real provision behind this, but it does not say what the retelling says. Four qualifications sit inside and beside s 36(1), all of them dropped by 'legally conclusive': 1. It is conclusive 'for the purposes of this Act' only - not at large.

2. It is conclusive only as to 'the state of repair or general condition' on the day specified in the report. It proves the starting condition, not who caused any later change, not the cost of repair, and not whether something is fair wear and tear.
3. It is conclusive only if signed by or on behalf of BOTH parties. The single most common real-world case - the renter never returns their copy within the 5 business days s 35(2) allows - falls outside s 36(1) entirely.
4. s 36(2) carves out three classes of statement: a condition 'that could not reasonably have been discovered on a reasonable inspection of the premises'; any statement the renter has recorded a written disagreement with; and any statement the rental provider disagreed with in writing where the renter completed the report first. The comparative half of the claim - that the report therefore matters more than photos - has no primary source at all, and both CAV and the prescribed form push the other way. Form 4 itself instructs 'Take photos that show the condition of items, fixtures and fittings', and CAV says 'It is a good idea for both the renter and the rental provider to take photos of the property at the start of the rental agreement'. Director's Guideline 2, which VCAT must have regard to under s 486, describes them working together rather than in a hierarchy: 'VCAT relies on the condition report and any photos or video taken to determine the condition of the premises and whether they were ‘reasonably clean’ at the start of the tenancy.' Photos are also how a party discharges the s 36(2)(a) point about defects not reasonably discoverable on inspection - so in the cases where the report is NOT conclusive, photos are what is left.
   - source: "A statement in a condition report under section 35 is conclusive evidence, for the purposes of this Act, of the state of repair or general condition of the rented premises on the day specified in the report if the condition report is signed by or on behalf of the residential rental provider and the renter."
     Victorian Government (Chief Parliamentary Counsel), Residential Tenancies Act 1997, authorised version 113 https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf
   - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf - s 36 in full, including the s 36(2) exceptions and s 36(1A)
   - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/rent-bond-bills-and-condition-reports/condition-reports - CAV's framing is 'Condition reports can be important evidence', and it advises photos in addition
   - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/library/publications/housing-and-accommodation/renting/guideline-2--cleanliness.docx - Director's Guideline 2 (Cleanliness), issued under s 486, on how VCAT weighs the report against photos
   - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2021-04/21-003sra%20authorised.PDF - Form 4 tells the parties to take photos and attach them

- **[echoed-claim]** Landlord Wise asserts that failing to provide a Victorian condition report carries a penalty of up to 25 penalty units.
  - as of: 2026-08-10
  - quote: "This isn’t optional. Failing to provide one carries a penalty of up to 25 penalty units, and more importantly, it undermines your ability to recover costs at the end of the tenancy."
  - source: https://landlordwise.com.au/guides/condition-report-vic/
  - **BACKING FOUND** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - Correct, and correctly hedged as a maximum - 25 penalty units is the penalty printed immediately under s 35(1), the duty to give the renter 2 copies of a signed condition report before they enter into occupation. The quote above is the penalty line together with the opening of s 35(1A) that follows it, because the penalty line is set on its own line in the authorised PDF. One thing to keep straight: this is the s 35 figure, for a residential rental agreement. Do not carry it across to the other tenure types - the same duty in a rooming house (s 97), caravan park (s 148) or on a Part 4A site (s 206O) is its own offence with its own penalty. And a penalty unit is a moving value set annually under the Monetary Units Act 2004, so any page that converts 25 penalty units into a dollar figure is stating something that goes stale each 1 July.
    - source: "Penalty: 25 penalty units. (1A) For the purposes of subsection (1), a residential rental provider is deemed to give 2 copies of a condition report to the renter"
      Victorian Government (Chief Parliamentary Counsel), Residential Tenancies Act 1997, authorised version 113 https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf - the penalty attached to s 35(1)
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/rent-bond-bills-and-condition-reports/condition-reports - CAV's page states the duty but no penalty, so it cannot back the figure
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf - compared the parallel penalties: s 97(1) (rooming houses), s 148(1) (caravan parks) and s 206O(1) (Part 4A sites)

## fixed term agreement (7)

- **[echoed-claim]** The standard form rental agreement at clause 16.1 limits a claim for rent foregone to no more than one month's rent for each year remaining on the agreement.
  - as of: 2026-04-06
  - quote: "The standard form rental agreement (clause 16.1) limits the amount of rent foregone you can claim to no more than one month’s rent for each year remaining on the agreement."
  - source: https://landlordwise.com.au/guides/break-lease-vic/
  - **A REAL SOURCE SAYS SOMETHING NARROWER** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - The clause is real and says exactly this - but it is not in the agreement almost anyone signs. Clause 16 ('Ending the agreement early') exists only in Form 2 of Schedule 1, the prescribed form for a residential rental agreement of MORE THAN 5 years. Regulation 10 sets the mapping: 10(1) prescribes Form 1 for fixed terms of not more than 5 years (s 26(1) of the Act), 10(2) prescribes Form 2 for fixed terms of more than 5 years, and 10(3) prescribes Form 1 again for periodic agreements. Form 1 has no 'ending the agreement early' clause at all and no per-year cap; its clause 16 is a safety-check clause. So 'the standard form rental agreement (clause 16.1)' names the wrong instrument: on a normal 6- or 12-month lease there is no clause 16.1 to point at. The rest of the clause is worth knowing and is dropped from the retelling: the amount of rent foregone 'must reflect the actual losses suffered by the rental provider' (16.1), the provider 'must take all reasonable steps to re-let the premises' (16.2), and no rent foregone is payable at all where the renter left under s 91ZD, 91ZE or 91ZF (16.3).
    - source: "That amount must be no more than one month's rent for each year left in the agreement."
      Victorian Government - Chief Parliamentary Counsel (authorised version, Residential Tenancies Regulations 2021) https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-06/21-3sra009-authorised.pdf
    - searched: https://www.legislation.vic.gov.au/in-force/statutory-rules/residential-tenancies-regulations-2021 (version list is JS-rendered; probed version pages 001-011 for PDFs, 009 is the latest with a PDF)
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-06/21-3sra009-authorised.pdf (authorised Residential Tenancies Regulations 2021 v009, as at 30 June 2026; Schedule 1 Forms 1 and 2 read in full)
    - searched: grep of the authorised Regulations for "one month's rent for each year" - exactly one hit in the whole instrument, inside Form 2
    - searched: reg 10 of the same Regulations (which form is prescribed for which agreement)
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf (Residential Tenancies Act 1997 v113, ss 26, 211A)
    - searched: https://landlordwise.com.au/guides/break-lease-vic/ (the retelling, re-fetched 15 August 2026)
- **[echoed-claim]** Landlord Wise states the one-month-per-year compensation cap applies to all standard-form agreements, not just long-term ones.
  - as of: 2026-04-06
  - quote: "This cap applies to all standard-form agreements — not just long-term ones."
  - source: https://landlordwise.com.au/guides/break-lease-vic/
  - **A PRIMARY SOURCE SAYS THE OPPOSITE** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - The Act attaches the cap to one kind of agreement and only that kind. Section 211A(4) opens: 'If the Tribunal is determining an application under section 210 in respect of a claim by a residential rental provider for unpaid rent in a residential rental agreement for a fixed term of more than 5 years that has been terminated early...' - the quoted limit then follows. CAV says the same twice: on the lease-breaking page, 'For fixed-term agreements that are longer than 5 years, VCAT cannot order the compensation of more than one month's rent for every 12 months remaining on the agreement', and on the agreements page it lists 'Renters who leave early may have to pay one month's rent for every full year remaining on the agreement up to a maximum of 6 months' under the heading 'Long fixed-term agreements'. Landlord Wise's own page concedes the point in the sentence before ('For agreements with a fixed term of more than 5 years, this per-year cap is also enshrined in the Act itself at s211A(4)') and then extends it to everything - which is the drift worth flagging, because for a normal 12-month lease it makes the cap sound like a ceiling on what VCAT can order when there is none. On a sub-5-year lease the limit on lost-rent compensation is not a formula, it is proof: actual loss, mitigated by prompt re-letting (s 211A(3)(b)).
    - source: "the Tribunal may not award an amount of compensation in excess of a maximum of one month's rent under the agreement for each 12 month period of the unexpired term of the agreement."
      Victorian Government - Chief Parliamentary Counsel (authorised version, Residential Tenancies Act 1997) https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf (s 211A(4), p.512-513 of the authorised Act v113)
    - searched: grep of the authorised Act for 'in excess of a maximum of one month' and for 'fixed term of more than 5 years'
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-06/21-3sra009-authorised.pdf (Form 1 in Schedule 1 contains no equivalent of clause 16; the phrase appears once, in Form 2)
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/moving-out-giving-notice-and-evictions/breaking-a-rental-agreement (CAV, section 'Costs for lost rent'; last updated 7 December 2025)
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/starting-and-changing-rental-agreements/different-rental-agreements/residential-rental-agreements (CAV lists the cap as one of the 'different laws for long-term agreements'; last updated 24 November 2025)
    - searched: https://landlordwise.com.au/guides/break-lease-vic/ (the retelling - which cites s 211A(4) itself for 5+ year agreements and then generalises past it)
- **[echoed-claim]** Landlord Wise states a self-managing rental provider who does not use an agent has no re-letting fee to recover.
  - as of: 2026-04-06
  - quote: "If you self-manage your property and do not use an agent, there is no re-letting fee to recover."
  - source: https://landlordwise.com.au/guides/break-lease-vic/
  - **BACKING FOUND** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - Backed, and unusually cleanly - the quoted line is an item in CAV's list of 'Costs a renter doesn't have to pay'. CAV states the positive case in the same section: 'A renter who has broken the agreement may be asked to pay the re-letting fee if the rental provider uses an agent.' The Act supports the same result from the other direction: s 211A(3)(a) requires the Tribunal to determine 'advertising costs and reletting fees (if any) incurred by the residential rental provider... on a basis that is proportionate to the actual cost of securing the renter' - a self-manager incurs none, so there is nothing to pro-rate. Landlord Wise's added gloss ('You cannot invent a fee to charge the renter for your own time') is not stated anywhere primary, but follows from the same 'incurred' requirement.
    - source: "re-letting fees if the property is rented directly with the owner and not through a real estate agent."
      Consumer Affairs Victoria https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/moving-out-giving-notice-and-evictions/breaking-a-rental-agreement
    - searched: web search (allowed_domains consumer.vic.gov.au): 'consumer.vic.gov.au lease breaking renter ends rental agreement early costs advertising re-letting fee'
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/moving-out-giving-notice-and-evictions/breaking-a-rental-agreement (sections 'Costs for re-letting fees' and 'Costs a renter doesn't have to pay'; last updated 7 December 2025)
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf (s 211A(3)(a) - costs must be ones 'incurred' by the provider)
    - searched: https://tenantsvic.org.au/explore-topics/ending-your-tenancy/ending-or-breaking-your-lease/private-rental/ (Tenants Victoria agrees the fee must be evidenced by the agent's invoice)
    - searched: https://landlordwise.com.au/guides/break-lease-vic/ (the retelling)
- **[echoed-claim]** Landlord Wise states that a rental provider cannot charge the renter a penalty for breaking the lease.
  - as of: 2026-04-06
  - quote: "You cannot charge the renter a penalty for breaking the lease."
  - source: https://landlordwise.com.au/guides/break-lease-vic/
  - **BACKING FOUND** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - Backed by the regulator and, underneath it, by an outright prohibition in the Act that the retelling never mentions. Section 27B(1)(c) makes it a prohibited term to include in a residential rental agreement 'a term that provides that if the renter contravenes the residential rental agreement, the renter is liable to pay' (i) all or part of the remaining rent, (ii) increased rent, (iii) a penalty, or (iv) liquidated damages. Section 26A(1) makes preparing an agreement containing such a term an offence carrying 25 penalty units, and s 26A(2) provides that 'If a residential rental agreement contains a prohibited term, that term is void and unenforceable.' So the position is stronger than 'you cannot charge it': a penalty clause is void on its face, and the provider's remedy is confined to a compensation application under s 210 assessed on actual, mitigated loss (s 211A(3)).
    - source: "Renters do not have to pay a penalty for breaking the agreement, but they do have to cover certain costs so the rental provider doesn’t lose money when the agreement is broken."
      Consumer Affairs Victoria https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/moving-out-giving-notice-and-evictions/breaking-a-rental-agreement
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/moving-out-giving-notice-and-evictions/breaking-a-rental-agreement (section 'Costs for breaking an agreement (lease)'; last updated 7 December 2025)
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf (ss 26A, 27, 27B, 28, 210, 211, 211A)
    - searched: grep of the authorised Act for 'liquidated damages' and 'is liable to pay' - s 27B(1)(c) is the prohibition
    - searched: https://tenantsvic.org.au/explore-topics/ending-your-tenancy/ending-or-breaking-your-lease/private-rental/ (advocacy restatement, last updated June 2026)
    - searched: https://landlordwise.com.au/guides/break-lease-vic/ (the retelling)
- **[echoed-claim]** Landlord Wise states that a fixed early-termination fee is unenforceable where the agreement gives no explanation of how the figure was calculated.
  - as of: 2026-04-06
  - quote: "If the agreement says “the renter will pay $2,000 for breaking the lease” with no explanation of how that figure was calculated, the clause is unenforceable."
  - source: https://landlordwise.com.au/guides/break-lease-vic/
  - **A REAL SOURCE SAYS SOMETHING NARROWER** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - A source exists, and the conclusion survives, but the test does not. Nothing primary says an unexplained flat fee is unenforceable while an explained one would be enforceable. What the Act says is blunter and cuts the other way: under s 27B(1)(c) a term making the renter liable on contravention to pay remaining rent, increased rent, 'a penalty' or 'liquidated damages' is a prohibited term whatever arithmetic is shown for it, and s 26A(2) makes a prohibited term 'void and unenforceable'. A '$2,000 for breaking the lease' clause with a beautifully documented derivation is still a liquidated damages clause. The nearest thing to the retelling's test is CAV's quoted sentence and s 28, under which a renter may ask the Tribunal to declare a term invalid as harsh or unconscionable - both of which put the explanation in issue without making it decisive. The practical outcome Landlord Wise describes is right; the reason it gives would mislead a provider into thinking a well-documented flat fee is safe.
    - source: "A rental agreement may include fees for breaking the agreement. If there are fees listed, the agreement must indicate the reason for the fees."
      Consumer Affairs Victoria https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/moving-out-giving-notice-and-evictions/breaking-a-rental-agreement
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf (ss 26A(1)-(2), 27(1), 27A(2), 27B(1)(c), 28)
    - searched: grep of the authorised Act for 'unenforceable', 'penalty', 'liquidated damages', 'harsh or unconscionable'
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-06/21-3sra009-authorised.pdf (reg 11, the prescribed prohibited terms made under s 27B(1)(g) - no 'unexplained fee' category)
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/moving-out-giving-notice-and-evictions/breaking-a-rental-agreement (CAV on fees listed in the agreement; last updated 7 December 2025)
    - searched: https://landlordwise.com.au/guides/break-lease-vic/ (the retelling, in two places: the guide body and the FAQ)
- **[echoed-claim]** Landlord Wise states a no-fault notice to vacate is of no effect if it specifies a termination date earlier than the end of the fixed term.
  - as of: 2026-04-06
  - quote: "For no-fault reasons (sale, renovation, demolition, business use, family member, public purposes), a notice is of no effect if it specifies a termination date earlier than the end of the fixed term."
  - source: https://landlordwise.com.au/guides/notice-to-vacate-vic/
  - **BACKING FOUND** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - Backed, and the reason list matches the sections exactly. Section 91ZZI(1) provides that a notice given under s 91ZX, 91ZY, 91ZZ, 91ZZA, 91ZZB, 91ZZC or 91ZZE in respect of a fixed term residential rental agreement is of no effect, then gives two limbs, the quoted one being limb (b). Mapping the sections to Landlord Wise's plain-English list: 91ZX Repairs (renovation), 91ZY Demolition, 91ZZ Premises to be used for business, 91ZZA Premises to be occupied by the rental provider or a family member, 91ZZB Premises to be sold, 91ZZC Premises required for public purposes. Two things the retelling omits: s 91ZZE (renter no longer meets eligibility criteria, i.e. social housing) is in the same list, and limb (a) carves out agreements that themselves contain a break-by-notice provision, where the test is instead compliance with that provision. Note the list is current only because of the November 2025 reforms - s 28 of Act No. 6/2025 struck '91ZZD, 91ZZDA' out of s 91ZZI(1) when the end-of-fixed-term and periodic no-reason notices were repealed.
    - source: "in any other case, if it specifies a termination date that is earlier than the end of the term of the residential rental agreement."
      Victorian Government - Chief Parliamentary Counsel (authorised version, Residential Tenancies Act 1997) https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf (s 91ZZI 'Notice to have no effect in certain circumstances', p.215-216; headings of ss 91ZX-91ZZE read to map the reasons)
    - searched: grep of the authorised Act for 'of no effect' (16 hits) and for the section list in s 91ZZI(1)
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2025-03/25-006aa-authorised.pdf (s 28 of Act No. 6/2025, which rewrote the s 91ZZI section list)
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/moving-out-giving-notice-and-evictions/notice-to-vacate/giving-notice-to-a-renter (CAV notice to vacate page)
    - searched: https://landlordwise.com.au/guides/notice-to-vacate-vic/ (the retelling, re-fetched 15 August 2026)
- **[echoed-claim]** Collings Real Estate states that for fixed-term agreements under 5 years rent increases are allowed only if the amount or method of calculation is specified in the lease.
  - as of: 2026-06-26
  - quote: "Fixed-term agreements under 5 years: increases only if the amount or method of calculation is specified in the lease"
  - source: https://www.collings.com.au/rent-increases-in-victoria-rules-and-limits/
  - **BACKING FOUND** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - True as stated, but the '5 years' qualifier is Collings', not the Act's. Section 44(4) provides that a residential rental provider under a fixed term residential rental agreement must not increase the rent before the term ends unless the agreement '(a) provides for a rent increase within the fixed term of a specified amount and the increase is not more than that amount; or (b) specifies the method by which a rent increase within the fixed term is to be calculated and the increase is not more than an amount calculated using the specified method'. That applies to every fixed term agreement, long or short. Collings' companion line - 'Fixed-term agreements of 5 years or more: one increase per 12-month period is permitted' - reads as though the clause requirement drops away above 5 years, which it does not; what changes is that Form 2, the prescribed long-term form, already carries a rent adjustment clause (clause 15, with four options: CPI, Statewide Rent Index, fixed percentage, fixed amount), so the condition is normally satisfied on the form itself. CAV states the long-term rule as 'rent must not be increased more than once every 12 months, and only if the agreement says that the rent can be increased.' Also note Collings' own cut-off is misdrawn: the statutory boundary is 'more than 5 years' (s 26(1A)), so an exactly-5-year agreement sits on the short side.
    - source: "The rental provider is not allowed to increase the rent during a fixed term agreement unless the rental agreement (lease) has an additional clause (term) allowing for an increase."
      Consumer Affairs Victoria https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/rent-bond-bills-and-condition-reports/rent/rent-increases
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf (s 44(4), p.98 of the authorised Act v113)
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/rent-bond-bills-and-condition-reports/rent/rent-increases (sections 'Rules for increasing rent' and 'How often rent can increase'; last updated 26 February 2026)
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-06/21-3sra009-authorised.pdf (Form 2 clause 15 'Rent adjustments' - the four prescribed rent increase options for long-term agreements)
    - searched: https://www.collings.com.au/rent-increases-in-victoria-rules-and-limits/ (the retelling, re-fetched 15 August 2026)

## gas and electrical safety checks (7)

- **[echoed-claim]** LongView states that gas safety checks are required every two years for Victorian rental properties.
  - as of: 2026-01-21
  - quote: "Gas safety checks are required every two years for Victorian rental properties, but this is where many rental providers unintentionally fall out of compliance."
  - source: https://blog.longview.com.au/gas-and-electrical-safety-checks-in-victoria-who-can-do-them-and-how-often
  - **A REAL SOURCE SAYS SOMETHING NARROWER** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - The two-year period is real; the word 'Victorian rental properties' is where this comes apart. Schedule 3 clause 2(2) of the Residential Tenancies Regulations 2021 reads 'The residential rental provider must ensure that a gas safety check of all gas installations and fittings in the rented premises is conducted every 2 years by a licensed or registered gasfitter'. But Schedule 3 is not a free-standing duty. Regulation 13 says 'For the purposes of section 27C(2) of the Act, the prescribed terms are in Schedule 3', s 27C(2) says an agreement in the standard form 'may include' such a term, and s 68A(1) makes the duty bite only 'if that agreement contains a term prescribed under section 27C(2)'. It is a contractual term the Act then enforces, so it reaches only agreements made on the post-29-March-2021 standard form. Two Victorian regulators state the consequence differently and both statements are on their current pages. Consumer Affairs Victoria, quoted above and updated 3 May 2026, is explicit that pre-29-March-2021 agreements are outside it and that such providers are merely 'strongly encouraged'. Energy Safe Victoria's consumer page (reviewed 28 May 2026) says without qualification: 'These include both gas and electrical safety checks. These checks must be done every 2 years.' The tie-breaker is that ESV's own trade guidance agrees with CAV rather than with its consumer page - the Gasfitters toolkit says the duty runs 'For all new agreements after 29 March 2021' or where a fixed term agreement of more than five years rolls over into a periodic tenancy after that date. So LongView's unqualified sentence tracks ESV's simplified consumer page, not the instrument. Marked ambiguous rather than backed because the cadence is right and the population is not.
    - source: "It is not legislated that rental providers who entered into an agreement before 29 March 2021 conduct gas safety checks."
      Consumer Affairs Victoria https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/repairs-alterations-safety-and-pets/gas-electrical-and-water-safety-standards/rental-providers-gas-and-electrical-safety
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-06/21-3sra009-authorised.pdf (Schedule 3 clause 2 'Gas safety activities'; reg 13; reg 30)

- **[echoed-claim]** LongView states that under Victorian rental laws rental providers must ensure electrical safety checks are completed at least once every two years.
  - as of: 2026-01-21
  - quote: "Under Victorian rental laws, rental providers must ensure that electrical safety checks are completed at least once every two years for rental properties."
  - source: https://blog.longview.com.au/gas-and-electrical-safety-checks-in-victoria-who-can-do-them-and-how-often
  - **A REAL SOURCE SAYS SOMETHING NARROWER** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - Same shape as the gas claim and the same gap. Schedule 3 clause 1(1) of the Residential Tenancies Regulations 2021 requires that an 'electrical safety check of all electrical installations, appliances and fittings provided by a residential rental provider in the rented premises is conducted every 2 years by a licensed or registered electrician', and clause 1(2) adds that if none has been done in the last 2 years when the renter takes occupation, one must be arranged as soon as practicable. But Schedule 3 exists only as a prescribed agreement term under s 27C(2), enforced through s 68A(1), so the duty attaches to the agreement, not to the building. CAV's sentence, quoted above, states the population correctly and is the closest primary statement to LongView's claim; CAV adds one nuance LongView omits, that the duty also picks up 'rental agreements where a fixed term agreement rolls over into a periodic rental agreement after 29 March 2021'. Where LongView says 'rental providers must ensure that electrical safety checks are completed at least once every two years for rental properties' full stop, a rental provider on a pre-2021 agreement is not in fact under that obligation - CAV says only that they are 'encouraged'. Ambiguous rather than backed for that reason. Note also that the electrical check is defined by reference to AS/NZS 3019, so 'checked by a licensed electrician' is a floor, not the whole content of the obligation.
    - source: "Rental providers who enter into a new agreement after 29 March 2021, must have all electrical installations and fittings checked by a licensed electrician at least once every 2 years."
      Consumer Affairs Victoria https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/repairs-alterations-safety-and-pets/gas-electrical-and-water-safety-standards/rental-providers-gas-and-electrical-safety
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-06/21-3sra009-authorised.pdf (Schedule 3 clause 1 'Electrical safety activities'; reg 5 definition of electrical safety check; reg 13)
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf (ss 27C(2), 68A(1), 68B)
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/repairs-alterations-safety-and-pets/gas-electrical-and-water-safety-standards/rental-providers-gas-and-electrical-safety
    - searched: https://www.energysafe.vic.gov.au/community-safety/energy-safety-guides/home-safety/tenants-and-renting-laws
    - searched: https://www.energysafe.vic.gov.au/industry-guidance/electrical/electricians-toolkit/residential-tenancy
    - searched: https://blog.longview.com.au/gas-and-electrical-safety-checks-in-victoria-who-can-do-them-and-how-often

- **[echoed-claim]** ConditionHQ states that Victoria is the only Australian state with mandatory periodic gas and electrical safety checks every 2 years.
  - as of: 2026-08-14
  - quote: "Victoria is the only Australian state with mandatory periodic gas and electrical safety checks — every 2 years, under the Residential Tenancies Regulations 2021."
  - source: https://conditionhq.app/blog/gas-electrical-safety-compliance-rental-property-australia
  - **SEARCHED, NOTHING FOUND** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - The claim spans eight jurisdictions and ConditionHQ supports it with nothing at all - the article cites no regulator, no statute and no other source for any state, and its only outbound link is to its own app in the App Store. I could confirm the Victorian half from the Regulations and could confirm from the Queensland RTA that Queensland's requirement is safety switches on power point circuits with three-monthly button testing only recommended, not a mandated periodic licensed check, which is consistent with what ConditionHQ says about Queensland. I could not reach primary sources for NSW, SA, WA, Tasmania, NT or the ACT within this trace: the NSW Fair Trading page renders client-side and produced no readable text, the SA regulator returns 403, and the nsw.gov.au equivalent 404s. So the comparative is unproven rather than disproven. Two things count against it independently of the interstate question. First, the Victorian limb it rests on is overstated in exactly the way finding 1 and 2 describe: the two-year checks are a prescribed term of agreements made on or after 29 March 2021, not a blanket duty on all Victorian rental properties, so 'Victoria has mandatory periodic checks' is already a simplification. Second, this same article is the source of the fabricated 13 October 2026 change traced below, which is reason to treat its unsourced assertions as unreliable generally.
    - searched: https://conditionhq.app/blog/gas-electrical-safety-compliance-rental-property-australia (read in full for a citation supporting the comparative claim - the page carries exactly one outbound link, to its own App Store listing, and cites no regulator or statute for any jurisdiction)
    - searched: https://www.rta.qld.gov.au/during-a-tenancy/maintenance/electrical-safety (Queensland Residential Tenancies Authority - safety switches on all power point circuits, testing by button 'every three months' only as an Electrical Safety Office recommendation, no mandated periodic licensed check)
    - searched: https://www.rta.qld.gov.au/sitemap.xml (enumerated RTA pages for any periodic gas or electrical check obligation)
    - searched: https://www.fairtrading.nsw.gov.au/housing-and-property/renting/during-a-tenancy/repairs-maintenance-and-damage (NSW Fair Trading - page renders client-side and yielded no readable statement of a periodic check duty)
    - searched: https://www.nsw.gov.au/housing-and-construction/rules/repairs-maintenance-rented-homes (HTTP 404)
    - searched: https://www.cbs.sa.gov.au/housing/renting-and-letting/during-tenancy/repairs-and-maintenance (South Australia Consumer and Business Services - HTTP 403, blocked)
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-06/21-3sra009-authorised.pdf (confirmed the Victorian half of the comparison)
- **[echoed-claim]** A significant change to the two-year electrical safety check took effect on 13 October 2026.
  - as of: 2026-08-14
  - quote: "A significant change took effect on 13 October 2026: the mandatory two-year electrical safety check requirement in Victoria now applies to all residential rental agreements, including long-standing tenancies that predate the requirement's introduction under the Residential Tenancies Regulations 2021."
  - source: https://conditionhq.app/blog/gas-electrical-safety-compliance-rental-property-australia
  - **A PRIMARY SOURCE SAYS THE OPPOSITE** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - Both halves are wrong, and both come from the same 13 October 2026 passage as the previous finding. On retention: reg 30(2)(b) of the Residential Tenancies Regulations 2021 ties the retention period to the next check rather than to a number of years, as quoted above, and reg 30(1)(b) says the same for gas records. Since the checks run on a two-year cycle, the practical retention period is about two years, not five. CAV restates the rule the same way: 'Rental providers must keep a record of gas safety checks until a record of the next safety check is created.' On the seven days: the period exists but ConditionHQ has inverted its trigger. Regulation 30(2)(c) requires that a copy of the most recent electrical safety check 'must be provided to the renter within 7 days after receipt by a residential rental provider of a written request from the renter'. It is a response obligation, keyed to a written request. ConditionHQ states the opposite twice over - 'within seven days of the check being completed', and 'This is a proactive obligation - it is not conditional on the renter making a written request.' A rental provider following that advice would be complying with a rule that does not exist while the real one, disclosure on request, is what a renter can actually enforce.
    - source: "a record of an electrical safety check must be kept until a record of the next electrical safety check is created"
      Victorian Government (authorised version, Chief Parliamentary Counsel) https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-06/21-3sra009-authorised.pdf
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-06/21-3sra009-authorised.pdf (reg 30 'Requirements for gas and electrical safety check record keeping', both subregulations in full; searched for 'five years' and '5 years')
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf (s 68B, the empowering provision)
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/repairs-alterations-safety-and-pets/gas-electrical-and-water-safety-standards/rental-providers-gas-and-electrical-safety (CAV's 'Record keeping' sections for both gas and electrical)
    - searched: https://conditionhq.app/blog/gas-electrical-safety-compliance-rental-property-australia

- **[echoed-claim]** 1st Call Electrical states that the recently changed Residential Tenancy Regulations made it mandatory for landlords to conduct gas and electric safety checks at least every 2 years.
  - as of: 2026-08-14
  - quote: "The recently changed Residential Tenancy Regulations have made it mandatory for landlords to conduct regular gas and electric safety checks on their rental properties. Gas and electric safety checks must be done at least every 2 years and smoke alarms must be tested annually."
  - source: https://www.firstcallelectrical.com.au/electrical-blog/new-smoke-alarm-laws-victoria
  - **A REAL SOURCE SAYS SOMETHING NARROWER** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - A third retelling of the same two-year cadence, with the same missing qualifier, plus one part that has gone out of date in a way that matters. The two-year figure is correct for agreements containing the prescribed Schedule 3 term; the conditional in s 68A(1), quoted above, is precisely the limit that 1st Call Electrical's flat 'mandatory for landlords' drops. The smoke alarm half was accurate when written but is now attributed to the wrong instrument: annual smoke alarm testing sat in Schedule 3 clause 3 of the Regulations as another prescribed agreement term until S.R. No. 123/2025 reg 19 repealed it on 25 November 2025, on the same day that s 68AA of the Act took over, requiring alarms to be 'tested at least once every 12 months in accordance with any instructions by the manufacturer of the smoke alarm' and applying regardless of when the agreement was entered into. So as of today the annual smoke alarm duty is in the Act and universal, while the two-year gas and electrical duties remain in the Regulations and are not - the opposite of the single undifferentiated rule the article describes. Also worth recording: 'recently changed' points at the 29 March 2021 commencement, five years ago.
    - source: "must undertake any safety-related repairs and maintenance activities set out in the residential rental agreement if that agreement contains a term prescribed under section 27C(2)"
      Victorian Government (authorised version, Chief Parliamentary Counsel) https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-06/21-3sra009-authorised.pdf (Schedule 3 clauses 1 and 2; reg 13; the repealed smoke alarm clause 3 and its marginal note 'Sch. 3 amended by S.R. No. 123/2025 reg. 19')
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf (ss 27C(2), 68A, 68AA)
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/repairs-alterations-safety-and-pets/gas-electrical-and-water-safety-standards/rental-providers-gas-and-electrical-safety
    - searched: https://www.energysafe.vic.gov.au/community-safety/energy-safety-guides/home-safety/tenants-and-renting-laws
    - searched: https://www.firstcallelectrical.com.au/electrical-blog/new-smoke-alarm-laws-victoria (checked the retelling for outbound citations)

## rent increase (7)

- **[echoed-claim]** Collings Real Estate states that the 60-day period for notice of a rent increase is a minimum, not a target.
  - as of: 2026-06-26
  - quote: "The 60-day period is a minimum, not a target. Landlords who issue notice fewer than 60 days before the intended start date cannot simply push the start date back informally."
  - source: https://www.collings.com.au/rent-increases-in-victoria-rules-and-limits/
  - **A PRIMARY SOURCE SAYS THE OPPOSITE** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - Contradicted on the number, correct on the structure. The Act now reads 90 days: s 44(1) of the authorised Residential Tenancies Act 1997 (version 113) says a residential rental provider must give a renter at least 90 days notice in the prescribed form of a proposed rent increase, the margin note recording the amendment as No. 6/2025 s. 11 (Consumer and Planning Legislation Amendment (Housing Statement Reform) Act 2025), effective 25 November 2025 (version 111). The pre-reform authorised text (v097, s 44(1)) said 60 days, which is where the industry figure comes from. Collings' page is dated 26 June 2026 - seven months after the change - and still states 60 days, so this is a live error rather than a stale-but-once-true page. The 'minimum, not a target' framing is itself sound: s 44(5) provides that 'A rent increase in contravention of this section is invalid.' The corpus already carries the correction from other retellings but never from a primary source (trace-020 3a5b75c609b9 Cubbi notes the 90-day change from November 2025; trace-020 d26b485a9194 Landlord Wise names s 11 of the 2025 amending Act) - so this is the field's error, not a corpus duplicate.
    - source: "From 25 November 2025, the minimum notice period for rental providers to issue a rent increase notice will change from 60 to 90 days."
      Consumer Affairs Victoria https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/rent-bond-bills-and-condition-reports/rent/rent-increases
    - searched: web search: Residential Tenancies Act 1997 Victoria section 44 rent increase 90 days notice legislation.vic.gov.au
    - searched: https://www.legislation.vic.gov.au/in-force/acts/residential-tenancies-act-1997 (version list; current in-force version 113, effective 1 July 2026)
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf (authorised consolidated Act, s 44 at p.97-98; s 45-47 at p.99-101)
    - searched: https://www.collings.com.au/rent-increases-in-victoria-rules-and-limits/ (the retelling; page dated 26 June 2026, still says 60 days)
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/rent-bond-bills-and-condition-reports/rent/rent-increases
    - searched: web search: consumer.vic.gov.au rent increases 90 days notice renting Victoria
    - searched: checked s 44(1) in both the current authorised Act (v113) and the pre-reform authorised version v097 to date the change
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2021-03/97-109aa097%20authorised.pdf (s 44 as it stood to 28 March 2021: 60 days)
- **[echoed-claim]** Collings Real Estate states that the 12-month limit on rent increases applies regardless of whether the property is on a fixed-term or periodic agreement.
  - as of: 2026-06-26
  - quote: "This 12-month limit applies regardless of whether the property is on a fixed-term or periodic (month-to-month) agreement."
  - source: https://www.collings.com.au/rent-increases-in-victoria-rules-and-limits/
  - **BACKING FOUND** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - Backed by the Act itself, not just by the regulator's restatement: s 44(4A) of the authorised Residential Tenancies Act 1997 (v113, p.98) provides that a residential rental provider must not increase the rent payable under a residential rental agreement at intervals of less than 12 months - the subsection is agreement-type neutral, which is what makes the 'fixed-term or periodic' framing correct. Two caveats the retellings drop: for agreements that started (or became periodic) before 19 June 2019 the interval is 6 months, per CAV's own page ('for periodic rental agreements that started before 19 June 2019, the rent can't be increased more than once every 6 months'); and during a fixed term an increase is only possible at all if the agreement contains a conforming clause (s 44(4)). Forge's version ('rent can only increase once every 12 months') is the same idea stated without those caveats.
    - source: "In most cases, rent cannot be increased more than once every 12 months, even if the rental agreement specifies a rent increase before that time."
      Consumer Affairs Victoria https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/rent-bond-bills-and-condition-reports/rent/rent-increases
    - searched: web search: Residential Tenancies Act 1997 Victoria section 44 rent increase 90 days notice legislation.vic.gov.au
    - searched: https://www.legislation.vic.gov.au/in-force/acts/residential-tenancies-act-1997 (version list; current in-force version 113, effective 1 July 2026)
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf (authorised consolidated Act, s 44 at p.97-98; s 45-47 at p.99-101)
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/rent-bond-bills-and-condition-reports/rent/rent-increases (sections 'How often rent can increase', covering fixed term, long-term and periodic agreements)
    - searched: grep of the authorised Act text for s 44(4A)
    - searched: https://www.collings.com.au/rent-increases-in-victoria-rules-and-limits/
    - searched: https://www.forgeproperty.com.au/en-au/media/what-are-the-rules-for-rent-increases-and-form-3a-in-victoria-in-2026 (the second retelling; page dated 20 February 2026)
- **[echoed-claim]** Collings Real Estate states that landlords on fixed-term agreements could once sometimes negotiate rent increases mid-lease.
  - as of: 2026-06-26
  - quote: "Prior to those reforms, landlords on fixed-term agreements could sometimes negotiate rent increases mid-lease. That flexibility no longer exists for residential tenancies."
  - source: https://www.collings.com.au/rent-increases-in-victoria-rules-and-limits/
  - **A PRIMARY SOURCE SAYS THE OPPOSITE** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - The primary sources contradict this in both directions. Mid-fixed-term increases still exist: current s 44(4) (v113, p.98) permits an increase before the term ends where the agreement 'provides for a rent increase within the fixed term of a specified amount' or 'specifies the method by which a rent increase within the fixed term is to be calculated', which CAV restates in the quoted sentence. And the pre-reform position was not free negotiation either: the authorised version in force to 28 March 2021 (v097, s 44(4)) already said a landlord under a fixed term tenancy agreement must not increase the rent before the term ends unless the agreement provides for a rent increase within the fixed term. So the 2018 reforms tightened what such a clause must specify (amount, or method of calculation); they did not remove a 'flexibility to negotiate mid-lease' that the Act never granted. Collings dates the change to the 2018 reforms, which commenced 29 March 2021.
    - source: "The rental provider is not allowed to increase the rent during a fixed term agreement unless the rental agreement (lease) has an additional clause (term) allowing for an increase."
      Consumer Affairs Victoria https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/rent-bond-bills-and-condition-reports/rent/rent-increases
    - searched: web search: Residential Tenancies Act 1997 Victoria section 44 rent increase 90 days notice legislation.vic.gov.au
    - searched: https://www.legislation.vic.gov.au/in-force/acts/residential-tenancies-act-1997 (version list; current in-force version 113, effective 1 July 2026)
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf (authorised consolidated Act, s 44 at p.97-98; s 45-47 at p.99-101)
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2021-03/97-109aa097%20authorised.pdf (pre-reform s 44(4), in force to 28 March 2021)
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/rent-bond-bills-and-condition-reports/rent/rent-increases (section 'Rules for increasing rent')
    - searched: https://www.collings.com.au/rent-increases-in-victoria-rules-and-limits/
    - searched: compared s 44(4) text before and after the Residential Tenancies Amendment Act 2018 (No. 45/2018 s 34(4), commenced 29 March 2021)
- **[echoed-claim]** Collings Real Estate states that the flexibility for landlords on fixed-term agreements to negotiate rent increases mid-lease no longer exists for residential tenancies.
  - as of: 2026-06-26
  - quote: "Prior to those reforms, landlords on fixed-term agreements could sometimes negotiate rent increases mid-lease. That flexibility no longer exists for residential tenancies."
  - source: https://www.collings.com.au/rent-increases-in-victoria-rules-and-limits/
  - **A PRIMARY SOURCE SAYS THE OPPOSITE** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - The primary sources contradict this in both directions. Mid-fixed-term increases still exist: current s 44(4) (v113, p.98) permits an increase before the term ends where the agreement 'provides for a rent increase within the fixed term of a specified amount' or 'specifies the method by which a rent increase within the fixed term is to be calculated', which CAV restates in the quoted sentence. And the pre-reform position was not free negotiation either: the authorised version in force to 28 March 2021 (v097, s 44(4)) already said a landlord under a fixed term tenancy agreement must not increase the rent before the term ends unless the agreement provides for a rent increase within the fixed term. So the 2018 reforms tightened what such a clause must specify (amount, or method of calculation); they did not remove a 'flexibility to negotiate mid-lease' that the Act never granted. Collings dates the change to the 2018 reforms, which commenced 29 March 2021.
    - source: "The rental provider is not allowed to increase the rent during a fixed term agreement unless the rental agreement (lease) has an additional clause (term) allowing for an increase."
      Consumer Affairs Victoria https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/rent-bond-bills-and-condition-reports/rent/rent-increases

- **[echoed-claim]** Collings Real Estate states there is no statutory cap on the percentage by which rent can be increased in Victoria.
  - as of: 2026-06-26
  - quote: "There is no statutory cap on the percentage by which rent can be increased in Victoria."
  - source: https://www.collings.com.au/rent-increases-in-victoria-rules-and-limits/
  - **NOT YET CHECKED.** Nobody has gone looking for a source for this one, so it says nothing either way.
- **[echoed-claim]** Forge Real Estate states that in Victoria rent can only increase once every 12 months under the Residential Tenancies Act 1997.
  - as of: 2026-02-20
  - quote: "In Victoria, rent can only increase once every 12 months and must not be excessive under the Residential Tenancies Act 1997."
  - source: https://www.forgeproperty.com.au/en-au/media/what-are-the-rules-for-rent-increases-and-form-3a-in-victoria-in-2026
  - **A REAL SOURCE SAYS SOMETHING NARROWER** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - A source exists but it does not impose the duty the retelling states. Nothing in the Act says rent 'must not be excessive' and charging an excessive rent is not an offence; excessive rent is a ground of complaint, not a prohibition. The machinery is s 45 (renter may apply to the Director to investigate and report, in writing within 30 days after the notice of the rent increase is given), s 46 (application to the Tribunal for an order declaring the rent or proposed rent excessive), and s 47(3), under which the Tribunal 'must make an order declaring the rent or proposed rent excessive if it is satisfied that the rent or proposed rent is more than that which should reasonably be paid' having regard to comparable premises, state of repair and other listed matters. If no one applies within the window, an above-market rent simply stands, and where VCAT does cap it, CAV says the maximum is usually set for 12 months for private rentals. Forge's own page is internally inconsistent on the neighbouring fact: dated 20 February 2026, it states 60 days' notice and then notes CAV's 60-to-90-day change of 25 November 2025.
    - source: "Consumer Affairs Victoria and RDRV will look at the list of factors set out in the law and regulations when we assess whether a rent increase is excessive."
      Consumer Affairs Victoria https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/rent-bond-bills-and-condition-reports/rent/challenging-rent-increases-or-high-rent
    - searched: web search: Residential Tenancies Act 1997 Victoria section 44 rent increase 90 days notice legislation.vic.gov.au
    - searched: https://www.legislation.vic.gov.au/in-force/acts/residential-tenancies-act-1997 (version list; current in-force version 113, effective 1 July 2026)
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf (authorised consolidated Act, s 44 at p.97-98; s 45-47 at p.99-101)
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/rent-bond-bills-and-condition-reports/rent/challenging-rent-increases-or-high-rent
    - searched: grep of the authorised Act for 'excessive' (ss 45, 46, 47 for rental agreements; ss 102-103 rooming houses; ss 153-154 caravan parks)
    - searched: https://www.forgeproperty.com.au/en-au/media/what-are-the-rules-for-rent-increases-and-form-3a-in-victoria-in-2026
    - searched: web search: excessive rent disputes Rental Dispute Resolution Victoria rdrv.vic.gov.au

## rent reduction (7)

- **[echoed-claim]** Collings Real Estate states that a landlord must give a renter at least 60 days written notice before a rent increase takes effect.
  - as of: 2026-06-26
  - quote: "A landlord must give a renter at least 60 days written notice before a rent increase takes effect. This is one of the most important procedural requirements, and failing to meet it invalidates the increase entirely."
  - source: https://www.collings.com.au/rent-increases-in-victoria-rules-and-limits/
  - **A PRIMARY SOURCE SAYS THE OPPOSITE** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - The number is wrong and has been for nine months. Section 44(1) of the authorised Act (v113, p.97) reads 90 days, its margin note recording the amendment as 'S. 44(1) amended by Nos 45/2002 s. 12(1)(a), 45/2018 s. 34(2), 6/2025 s. 11', effective 25 November 2025. Consumer Affairs Victoria says the same in plain words on its rent increases page: 'From 25 November 2025, the minimum notice period for rental providers to issue a rent increase notice will change from 60 to 90 days.' Collings' page is written 'as it stands in 2026' and still says 60 days in five separate places, including a section arguing the 60-day period 'is a minimum, not a target' - so this is a live error, not a stale-but-once-true page. Forge is internally inconsistent in a single sentence: 'Landlords must provide 60 days' written notice using the approved Notice of Rent Increase form CAV says the minimum notice period changed from 60 to 90 days from 25 November 2025'. Cubbi's '60 days now, 90 from November 2025' was true when written and is now simply out of date. Collings' rider that failure invalidates the increase is correct - see the separate finding on s 44(5). Note also that this whole subject is filed as 'rent reduction' but every claim in the corpus for it concerns rent increases; there is no rent-reduction claim to trace.
    - source: "A residential rental provider must give a renter at least 90 days notice in the prescribed form of a proposed rent increase."
      Victorian Government - Chief Parliamentary Counsel (authorised version, Residential Tenancies Act 1997) https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf
    - searched: https://www.legislation.vic.gov.au/in-force/acts/residential-tenancies-act-1997/114 (returns a page but no PDF - version 113 is still the current in-force version)
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf (authorised Residential Tenancies Act 1997 v113, as at 1 July 2026; s 44 read in full at pp.97-98)
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/rent-bond-bills-and-condition-reports/rent/rent-increases (fetched 15 August 2026; page last updated 26 February 2026)
    - searched: https://www.collings.com.au/rent-increases-in-victoria-rules-and-limits/ (the retelling, re-fetched 15 August 2026 - it still says '60-day period is a minimum, not a target')
    - searched: https://www.forgeproperty.com.au/en-au/media/what-are-the-rules-for-rent-increases-and-form-3a-in-victoria-in-2026 (retelling dated 20 February 2026)
    - searched: https://www.cubbi.com.au/blog/when-can-i-increase-the-rent-in-victoria (retelling; written before the change commenced)
    - searched: grep of the authorised Act text for 'at least 90 days notice in the prescribed form' and for the s 44 margin notes
- **[echoed-claim]** Collings Real Estate states that there is no statutory cap on the percentage by which rent can be increased in Victoria.
  - as of: 2026-06-26
  - quote: "There is no statutory cap on the percentage by which rent can be increased in Victoria."
  - source: https://www.collings.com.au/rent-increases-in-victoria-rules-and-limits/
  - **BACKING FOUND** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - Correct, and correct for the right reason: there is no capping provision anywhere in the Act. The only constraint on amount is retrospective and application-driven - under s 45 a renter may ask the Director to investigate a proposed rent within 30 days of the notice, under s 46 apply to the Tribunal, and under s 47(3) the Tribunal must declare the rent excessive if satisfied it is more than should reasonably be paid. Collings' own rider ('increases significantly above market can be overturned at VCAT') describes that machinery accurately. Two dating caveats, since this area moves: from 31 March 2026 both CAV and the Tribunal must also take into account 'any prescribed matters' when assessing excessive rent (ss 12-13 of Act No. 6/2025), and excessive-rent disputes now route first through Rental Dispute Resolution Victoria rather than straight to VCAT. What is not capped is the increase; what is constrained is the resulting rent, after someone complains.
    - source: "The law doesn’t say exactly how much a rental provider can put the rent up by."
      Consumer Affairs Victoria https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/rent-bond-bills-and-condition-reports/rent/rent-increases
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/rent-bond-bills-and-condition-reports/rent/rent-increases (section 'How much rent can increase'; last updated 26 February 2026)
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf (grepped the whole Act for a capping provision; ss 45, 46, 47 are a complaint-and-assessment mechanism, not a cap)
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2025-03/25-006aa-authorised.pdf (ss 12 and 13 add 'any prescribed matters' to the excessive-rent tests in RTA ss 45(4)(b) and 47(3) - still not a cap)
    - searched: https://www.collings.com.au/rent-increases-in-victoria-rules-and-limits/ (the retelling)
- **[echoed-claim]** Cubbi states that the rent increase notice period in Victoria is 60 days.
  - as of: 2025-09-30
  - quote: "In Victoria, you can increase the rent once every 12 months. You must give written notice using the official form. Right now it is 60 days’ notice, but from November 2025 it will be 90 days. If the notice is not valid, the tenant does not need to pay the increase."
  - source: https://www.cubbi.com.au/blog/when-can-i-increase-the-rent-in-victoria
  - **BACKING FOUND** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - Backed twice over: the regulator states the date and the Act carries the amended text. CAV's operative instruction on the same page is now written in the new number - 'This form must be given to the renter at least 90 days before the increase is due to start' - and s 44(1) of the authorised Act (v113) reads 90 days. The purposes clause of the amending Act confirms the intent: it was enacted, among other things, 'to extend notice periods in notices of proposed rent increases and certain notices to vacate to 90 days'. The same Act made the parallel change for rooming houses (s 29 of the amending Act: in s 101(1) of the RTA, for '60' substitute '90'), which none of the retellings mention. Cubbi's forward-looking phrasing ('from November 2025 it will be 90 days') was correct at the time of writing; it is the pages still asserting 60 days in 2026 that are wrong.
    - source: "From 25 November 2025, the minimum notice period for rental providers to issue a rent increase notice will change from 60 to 90 days."
      Consumer Affairs Victoria https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/rent-bond-bills-and-condition-reports/rent/rent-increases
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/rent-bond-bills-and-condition-reports/rent/rent-increases (fetched 15 August 2026; 'Last updated: 26 February 2026')
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/new-changes-to-the-rental-laws (linked from the CAV banner announcing the change)
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf (s 44(1) now reads 90 days)
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2025-03/25-006aa-authorised.pdf (the amending Act, No. 6/2025, assented 18 March 2025)
    - searched: https://landlordwise.com.au/guides/rent-increase-vic/ (retelling that names the commencement date)

## rental minimum standards (7)

- **[echoed-claim]** RACV asserts that failure to meet the rental minimum standards in a Victorian rental property can incur penalties of more than $11,000 for an individual.
  - as of: 2025-06-13
  - quote: "Failure to meet the minimum standards in a rental property can incur penalties of more than $11,000 for an individual and over $57,000 for a company."
  - source: https://www.racv.com.au/royalauto/property/renting/what-are-the-minimum-rental-standards-victoria.html
  - **BACKING FOUND** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - Same provision, same quote - s 65A(1) sets 300 penalty units for a body corporate. Conversions: $57,693 at the 2023-24 penalty unit value of $192.31, $59,277 at the 2024-25 value of $197.59, $61,053 at 2025-26 and $62,730 at 2026-27. All are 'over $57,000'. Note where the threshold pins the vintage: $57,000 divided by 300 is $190, and the first year the penalty unit exceeded $190 was 2023-24. RACV's figure was therefore computed no earlier than 2023-24 and has not been refreshed since, even though the page carries a 13 June 2025 date - it now understates the exposure by about $5,700. The figure is correct as stated because RACV wrote 'over', but it is a frozen number, not a maintained one.
    - source: "Penalty: 60 penalty units in the case of a natural person; 300 penalty units in the case of a body corporate."
      Victorian Government, Office of the Chief Parliamentary Counsel (legislation.vic.gov.au) https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf
