- **[echoed-claim]** RACV surveyed Victorian rental providers.
  - as of: 2025-06-13
  - quote: "RACV data reveals that 81 per cent of Victorian rental providers surveyed use a property manager, citing reasons such as professional advice on their responsibilities as rental providers and assurance that the rental property is compliant with the standards."
  - source: https://www.racv.com.au/royalauto/property/renting/what-are-the-minimum-rental-standards-victoria.html
  - **SEARCHED, NOTHING FOUND** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - Genuinely unbacked. The named study cannot be found on the named organisation's own site - the classic tell. RACV is not a research publisher and the figure is attributed only to 'RACV data', a formulation that names no instrument. Nothing about the survey is checkable: not the population (all Victorian rental providers? RACV insurance customers? a self-selected panel?), not the sample size, not when it was run. That distinction matters a lot for this particular number, because RACV sells landlord insurance and the most plausible frame - its own policyholders - would not be representative of Victorian rental providers generally. Note also what the sentence is doing on the page: it sits directly under the heading 'Do rental providers using a property manager need to know the rental minimum standards?' and immediately after a recommendation to engage a compliance-report vendor, so the statistic is load-bearing for a commercial recommendation. No Victorian government series measures property manager use either, so there is no independent way to sanity-check 81 per cent. Page published and last modified 2025-06-13.
    - searched: https://www.racv.com.au/royalauto/property/renting/what-are-the-minimum-rental-standards-victoria.html - the retelling. The phrase is 'RACV data reveals that 81 per cent of Victorian rental providers surveyed use a property manager'. There is no link, no survey name, no fieldwork date, no sample size and no methodology note anywhere on the page.
    - searched: extracted every outbound link on the RACV page: the only non-RACV, non-adtech destinations are consumer.vic.gov.au pages and premier.vic.gov.au. None of them is a survey and none of them is RACV research.
    - searched: https://www.racv.com.au/about-racv/newsroom.html - RACV's newsroom; searched the page text for 'rental provider', 'property manager', '81', 'survey' and 'research'. No rental-provider survey, no 81 per cent figure, nothing on property managers.
    - searched: https://www.racv.com.au/about-racv/media-centre.html - 404
    - searched: https://www.racv.com.au/search.html?q=rental%20providers%20survey - 404; RACV exposes no working site search at that path
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/repairs-alterations-safety-and-pets/minimum-standards/minimum-standards-for-rental-properties - checked whether the regulator publishes any comparable figure on property manager use; it does not
    - searched: https://www.dffh.vic.gov.au/publications/rental-report - Homes Victoria Rental Report; publishes median rents and rent indices, nothing on management arrangements

- **[echoed-claim]** Safehaus asserts that the mould and damp requirement is a Victorian rental minimum standard.
  - as of: 2026-05-08
  - quote: "All rooms free from mould and damp caused by the building structure. This is the standard that catches the most landlords off guard."
  - source: https://www.safehaus.com.au/insights/victorian-rental-minimum-standards-a-practical-compliance-checklist-for-property-managers
  - **SEARCHED, NOTHING FOUND** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - Unbacked, and as phrased unfalsifiable - 'catches landlords off guard' measures surprise, which nobody collects. No Victorian regulator publishes compliance-failure rates by standard, so there is no source that could rank the standards even in principle. The nearest real evidence points the other way. In the CPRC and Tenants Victoria mystery shop of 100 Victorian rental properties (fieldwork February to April 2023), 9 properties had visible mould while 15 failed to meet the heating standard, and 28 had maintenance issues. Mould was not the most commonly failed standard in the only study that measured it. Tenants Victoria's summary groups the weak areas as 'mould, window coverings, and heating' without singling mould out. Worth noting that Safehaus sells compliance assessments and the sentence introduces the standard it is best placed to assess. The neighbouring assertion in the same passage is more defensible than this one; see the finding on painting over mould. Page published 2026-05-08.
    - searched: https://www.safehaus.com.au/insights/victorian-rental-minimum-standards-a-practical-compliance-checklist-for-property-managers - the retelling. The page cites CAV and the Regulations for the standards themselves but cites nothing for this ranking claim; it is a bare assertion appended to the standard's description.
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/repairs-alterations-safety-and-pets/minimum-standards/minimum-standards-for-rental-properties - CAV publishes the standards but no compliance-failure statistics and no ranking of them
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/repairs-alterations-safety-and-pets/minimum-standards/checklist-rental-properties-minimum-standards - CAV's own compliance checklist; no data on which standard is most often missed
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/repairs-alterations-safety-and-pets/pests-infestations-and-mould - no frequency data
    - searched: https://tenantsvic.org.au/news/minimum-standards-in-rentals/ - the only actual measurement located: the Consumer Policy Research Centre and Tenants Victoria mystery shop of 100 Victorian rental properties, reported as 'Is it Liveable? A mystery shop of private rental properties' (2024)
    - searched: https://www.dffh.vic.gov.au/publications/rental-report - checked for any compliance series; the Rental Report covers rents only

- **[echoed-claim]** Safehaus asserts that painting over mould is not compliance with the Victorian minimum standard.
  - as of: 2026-05-08
  - quote: "Painting over mould isn't compliance."
  - source: https://www.safehaus.com.au/insights/victorian-rental-minimum-standards-a-practical-compliance-checklist-for-property-managers
  - **BACKING FOUND** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - Backed by the wording of the standard rather than by any Victorian source addressing painting specifically - no such source exists, and that gap is worth recording. Schedule 4 clause 8 states an outcome the room must be in ('must be free from mould and damp'), not a treatment the provider must perform, so a cosmetic covering that leaves the mould and its structural cause in place does not meet it. CAV's mould page supports the causal half: 'if the problem happens because of the building structure a renter can ask for an urgent repair', and it gives 'a broken or faulty window frame has let rainwater inside and caused mould or damp' as an example of provider responsibility. One overreach in the Safehaus sentence should be flagged: it lists 'poor ventilation' alongside a leaking roof and rising damp as causes the provider must fix. The standard is confined to mould 'caused by or related to the building structure', and CAV expressly gives mould that 'formed because they didn't use a ventilation fan in the bathroom' as an example where the renter may be responsible. Ventilation-caused mould is therefore not automatically the provider's to remedy under this standard. Page published 2026-05-08.
    - source: "Each room in the rented premises must be free from mould and damp caused by or related to the building structure."
      Victorian Government, Office of the Chief Parliamentary Counsel (legislation.vic.gov.au) https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-06/21-3sra009-authorised.pdf
    - searched: https://www.safehaus.com.au/insights/victorian-rental-minimum-standards-a-practical-compliance-checklist-for-property-managers - the retelling: 'Mould treatment without addressing the cause. Painting over mould isn't compliance. The standard requires the property to be free of structural-related mould'
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-06/21-3sra009-authorised.pdf - Residential Tenancies Regulations 2021, Schedule 4 clause 8; read the operative wording, which states a result rather than a treatment
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/repairs-alterations-safety-and-pets/pests-infestations-and-mould - CAV's guidance on who is responsible for mould and when it is an urgent repair; searched it for any statement about remediation method or painting
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/repairs-alterations-safety-and-pets/minimum-standards/minimum-standards-for-rental-properties - CAV's statement of the mould and damp standard; no guidance on how it must be remedied
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/repairs-alterations-safety-and-pets/minimum-standards/checklist-rental-properties-minimum-standards - CAV's checklist; no treatment guidance
    - searched: searched the Regulations for any prescribed remediation method for mould; there is none - the only mould provisions are Schedule 4 clause 8, its Schedule 6 community housing equivalent, and a disclosure duty about mould notices in the last 3 years

## break lease (6)

- **[echoed-claim]** Landlord Wise states that a renter who broke the agreement may be required to cover reasonable costs the rental provider paid to advertise the property to find a replacement renter.
  - as of: 2026-04-06
  - quote: "If you paid to advertise the property to find a replacement renter, the renter who broke the agreement may be required to cover those costs — but only reasonable costs."
  - source: https://landlordwise.com.au/guides/break-lease-vic/
  - **A REAL SOURCE SAYS SOMETHING NARROWER** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - The regulator and the tenants' advocate read the same section in opposite directions, and the section is genuinely capable of both readings, so no verdict is honest here. CAV (7 December 2025) says what the retelling says - the quoted sentence, plus 'Reasonable costs means costs that most people would think are fair. The law does not define exactly what reasonable costs are'. Tenants Victoria (June 2026) says the opposite in terms: 'Lease-break costs are meant to compensate the landlord for expenses they paid in renting the property to you, not the costs to find or advertise to a new renter or to prepare a new lease', and adds that past the first fixed term 'the landlord cannot claim advertising costs or a re-letting fee to find a new renter'. The text both are construing is s 211A(3)(a), which requires the Tribunal to 'determine advertising costs and reletting fees (if any) incurred by the residential rental provider or the site owner (as the case requires) on a basis that is proportionate to the actual cost of securing the renter or the site tenant' - and 'the renter' is exactly the ambiguity: outgoing (Tenants Victoria) or incoming (CAV). The statutory Example under that paragraph leans CAV's way: it has the property manager advertise 'immediately' after notice, calls those $250 the advertising costs, and totals the claim as one week's rent ($500) plus the reletting fee pro-rated over the unexpired term ($250) plus advertising costs ($250) - the re-advertising cost included in full, only the reletting fee pro-rated. Tenants Victoria's second limb - nothing claimable at all once you are past your first fixed term - has no textual home in the Act or the Regulations that I could find; it is an inference from the same paragraph. Both sides agree on the boundaries: costs must have been actually incurred, the provider must mitigate by re-letting promptly (s 211A(3)(b)), and if the parties cannot agree what is reasonable it is for the Tribunal (now via Rental Dispute Resolution Victoria first) to decide.
    - source: "The renter may have to cover any reasonable costs that the rental provider or agent has paid to re-advertise the property."
      Consumer Affairs Victoria https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/moving-out-giving-notice-and-evictions/breaking-a-rental-agreement
    - searched: web search (allowed_domains consumer.vic.gov.au): 'consumer.vic.gov.au lease breaking renter ends rental agreement early costs advertising re-letting fee'
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/moving-out-giving-notice-and-evictions/breaking-a-rental-agreement (CAV, section 'Costs for advertising'; last updated 7 December 2025)
    - searched: https://tenantsvic.org.au/?s=breaking+a+lease (site search to locate the advocacy page)
    - searched: https://tenantsvic.org.au/explore-topics/ending-your-tenancy/ending-or-breaking-your-lease/private-rental/ (Tenants Victoria, sections 'Leaving early and paying costs' and 'Steps to calculate and minimise lease-break costs'; last updated June 2026)
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf (ss 210, 211, 211A of the authorised Act v113, including the worked Example under s 211A(3)(a))
    - searched: grep of the authorised Act for 'advertising costs and reletting fees' and 'proportionate to the actual cost of securing the renter'
    - searched: https://landlordwise.com.au/guides/break-lease-vic/ (the retelling)
- **[echoed-claim]** Landlord Wise states that clause 16.1 of the standard form rental agreement limits claimable rent foregone to no more than one month's rent for each year remaining on the agreement.
  - as of: 2026-04-06
  - quote: "The standard form rental agreement (clause 16.1) limits the amount of rent foregone you can claim to no more than one month’s rent for each year remaining on the agreement."
  - source: https://landlordwise.com.au/guides/break-lease-vic/
  - **A REAL SOURCE SAYS SOMETHING NARROWER** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - The clause exists and says this, but it is in the wrong form for the audience the guide is written for. Clause 16 ('Ending the agreement early') appears only in Form 2 of Schedule 1 - the prescribed form for an agreement of MORE THAN 5 years. Regulation 10 prescribes Form 1 for fixed terms of not more than 5 years and (reg 10(3)) for periodic agreements, and Form 1 contains no early-ending clause and no cap at all. On the ordinary 6- or 12-month lease that most break-lease disputes concern, there is no clause 16.1. Landlord Wise's companion sentence, that the cap 'applies to all standard-form agreements - not just long-term ones', is contradicted outright by s 211A(4) of the Act, which limits the Tribunal's one-month-per-12-months ceiling to 'a residential rental agreement for a fixed term of more than 5 years', and by CAV, which lists the cap as one of the 'different laws for long-term agreements'. Two parts of clause 16 the retelling does keep faith with: the amount 'must reflect the actual losses suffered by the rental provider', and the note that on a VCAT compensation application the provider may have to show how the losses were calculated.
    - source: "That amount must be no more than one month's rent for each year left in the agreement."
      Victorian Government - Chief Parliamentary Counsel (authorised version, Residential Tenancies Regulations 2021) https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-06/21-3sra009-authorised.pdf
    - searched: https://www.legislation.vic.gov.au/in-force/statutory-rules/residential-tenancies-regulations-2021 (version list is JS-rendered; probed version pages 001-011 for PDFs, 009 is the latest with a PDF)
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-06/21-3sra009-authorised.pdf (authorised Residential Tenancies Regulations 2021 v009, as at 30 June 2026; Schedule 1 Form 1 and Form 2 read in full, plus reg 10)
    - searched: grep of the authorised Regulations for "one month's rent for each year" - one hit in the whole instrument, inside Form 2
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf (ss 26(1), 26(1A), 211A(4))
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/starting-and-changing-rental-agreements/different-rental-agreements/residential-rental-agreements (CAV lists the cap under 'Long fixed-term agreements'; last updated 24 November 2025)
    - searched: https://landlordwise.com.au/guides/break-lease-vic/ (the retelling)
- **[echoed-claim]** Landlord Wise states that a self-managing Victorian rental provider who does not use an agent has no re-letting fee to recover.
  - as of: 2026-04-06
  - quote: "If you self-manage your property and do not use an agent, there is no re-letting fee to recover."
  - source: https://landlordwise.com.au/guides/break-lease-vic/
  - **BACKING FOUND** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - Backed cleanly: the quoted line is an item in CAV's list of 'Costs a renter doesn't have to pay', and CAV states the converse in the same section - 'A renter who has broken the agreement may be asked to pay the re-letting fee if the rental provider uses an agent', pro-rated by the proportion of the term remaining (CAV's worked example: a $500 fee with 6 months left of a 12-month agreement gives $250). The Act reaches the same place through s 211A(3)(a), which lets the Tribunal determine only 'advertising costs and reletting fees (if any) incurred' - a self-manager incurs none. This is the one break-lease claim where the regulator, the advocate and the Act all say the same thing.
    - source: "re-letting fees if the property is rented directly with the owner and not through a real estate agent."
      Consumer Affairs Victoria https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/moving-out-giving-notice-and-evictions/breaking-a-rental-agreement
    - searched: web search (allowed_domains consumer.vic.gov.au): 'consumer.vic.gov.au lease breaking renter ends rental agreement early costs advertising re-letting fee'
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/moving-out-giving-notice-and-evictions/breaking-a-rental-agreement (sections 'Costs for re-letting fees' and 'Costs a renter doesn't have to pay'; last updated 7 December 2025)
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf (s 211A(3)(a) - 'incurred by the residential rental provider')
    - searched: https://tenantsvic.org.au/explore-topics/ending-your-tenancy/ending-or-breaking-your-lease/private-rental/ (the advocacy side agrees, and adds that the renter can ask for the agent's invoice)
    - searched: https://landlordwise.com.au/guides/break-lease-vic/ (the retelling)
- **[echoed-claim]** Landlord Wise states that an unexplained flat early termination fee in a Victorian rental agreement is unenforceable.
  - as of: 2026-04-06
  - quote: "An unexplained flat fee is unenforceable."
  - source: https://landlordwise.com.au/guides/break-lease-vic/
  - **A REAL SOURCE SAYS SOMETHING NARROWER** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - The conclusion holds; the stated test is not the law. No primary source makes explanation the dividing line. Section 27B(1)(c) makes it a prohibited term to provide that on contravening the agreement the renter is liable to pay all or part of the remaining rent, increased rent, 'a penalty' or 'liquidated damages' - and s 26A(2) provides that 'If a residential rental agreement contains a prohibited term, that term is void and unenforceable', with s 26A(1) attaching a 25 penalty unit offence to preparing an agreement containing one. A flat break fee is void whether or not its derivation is set out, so a provider who reads Landlord Wise and writes the arithmetic into the lease has not fixed anything. The closest primary support for the 'unexplained' framing is CAV's quoted sentence and s 28, under which the Tribunal may declare a term invalid as harsh or unconscionable. What is enforceable is not a fee at all but a compensation order under s 210, assessed on actual loss, mitigated by prompt re-letting and pro-rated under s 211A(3).
    - source: "A rental agreement may include fees for breaking the agreement. If there are fees listed, the agreement must indicate the reason for the fees."
      Consumer Affairs Victoria https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/moving-out-giving-notice-and-evictions/breaking-a-rental-agreement
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf (ss 26A(1)-(2), 27(1), 27B(1)(c), 28 of the authorised Act v113)
    - searched: grep of the authorised Act for 'unenforceable', 'a penalty', 'liquidated damages', 'harsh or unconscionable'
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-06/21-3sra009-authorised.pdf (reg 11, the prescribed prohibited terms made under s 27B(1)(g) - no 'unexplained fee' category)
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/moving-out-giving-notice-and-evictions/breaking-a-rental-agreement (CAV on fees listed in an agreement; last updated 7 December 2025)
    - searched: https://landlordwise.com.au/guides/break-lease-vic/ (the retelling, in the guide body and again in the FAQ)
- **[echoed-claim]** realestate.com.au states that in the traditional rental market standard leases are typically fixed at six or twelve months
  - as of: 2026-01-06
  - quote: "This blend of short and mid-range commitments differs from the traditional rental market, where standard leases are typically fixed at six or twelve months, and where moving or breaking a lease can be costly."
  - source: https://www.realestate.com.au/insights/why-this-short-lease-rental-model-is-about-to-become-much-more-available/
  - **BACKING FOUND** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - A vague claim, but the primary sources do carry it, and they name the components CAV calls 'lease-break fees': remaining rent until the property is re-let, advertising fees, and re-letting fees. The Act's own worked Example under s 211A(3)(a) puts a number on a typical case - a 12-month lease at $500 a week broken 6 months early, re-let after one week, produces $1,000 of advertising and reletting costs (one week's rent $500, reletting fee pro-rated $250, advertising $250). Tenants Victoria, from the other side, says lease-break costs 'can be expensive' and advises renters to try to avoid the path. The countervailing limits, which realestate.com.au's throwaway line does not mention, are real: no penalty may be charged (CAV; RTA s 27B(1)(c)), the provider must mitigate by re-letting promptly (s 211A(3)(b)), liability for rent stops when a new renter moves in, and there are listed reasons - severe hardship, family violence, premises unfit, provider breach - that allow a renter to leave early paying nothing.
    - source: "If the rental provider (landlord) has to cover costs because the renter broke the rental agreement, the renter will usually have to pay them back."
      Consumer Affairs Victoria https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/moving-out-giving-notice-and-evictions/breaking-a-rental-agreement
    - searched: https://www.realestate.com.au/insights/why-this-short-lease-rental-model-is-about-to-become-much-more-available/ (the retelling, first published 7 January 2026)
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/moving-out-giving-notice-and-evictions/breaking-a-rental-agreement (CAV, opening section and 'Costs for breaking an agreement (lease)'; last updated 7 December 2025)
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf (ss 210, 211A - the compensation machinery and its worked example: one week's rent plus a pro-rated $250 reletting fee plus $250 advertising on a 12-month lease broken 6 months early)
    - searched: https://tenantsvic.org.au/explore-topics/ending-your-tenancy/ending-or-breaking-your-lease/private-rental/ ('This means you will probably have to pay lease-break costs, which can be expensive'; last updated June 2026)
    - searched: https://landlordwise.com.au/guides/break-lease-vic/ (a provider-side retelling of the same cost list)

## pets in rentals (6)

- **[echoed-claim]** Little Real Estate states that tenants have always needed to request permission from their landlord to keep pets.
  - as of: 2026-03-02
  - quote: "Tenants have always needed to request permission from their landlord to keep pets in their properties, however now the new laws are in place so that landlords cannot unreasonably refuse pets, unless there is a practical reason."
  - source: https://www.littlerealestate.com.au/news/new-pet-laws-in-victoria-for-tenants
  - **A REAL SOURCE SAYS SOMETHING NARROWER** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - RE-STATUSED contradicted -> ambiguous on audit (2026-08-17). The statutory history is exactly as the tracer found - Division 5B was inserted by the Consumer Legislation Amendment Act 2019 and commenced 2 March 2020, with the s 533 transitional proving it did not reach existing agreements - but that establishes only that the requirement was not statutory before then, not that renters were free to keep pets. A duty to ask arose from the lease's own terms for most renters, and had always been statutory for rooming house residents under s 117 (an original 1997 provision). Branding the source wrong implies pre-2020 renters needed no permission, which was untrue for anyone whose agreement contained a consent clause. The defect to name: the general statutory pets regime dates from 2 March 2020; before that any duty to ask was contractual. [right-error-wrong-scope] Evidence: "A resident must not keep an animal on the premises of the rooming house without the rooming house operator's consent." https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf ORIGINAL TRACE NOTE, kept because it usually found something real: Contradicted. The obligation to ask was created, not inherited. Before 2 March 2020 the Residential Tenancies Act 1997 said nothing at all about pets - Division 5B did not exist - and Parliament wrote an express transitional (s 533, quoted above) saying the new Division does not reach into tenancies already on foot unless the tenant wants a pet from then on. Parliament does not write a transitional carve-out for a rule that was already universal. VCAT states the same thing from the other side: 'Tenants who already have a pet are not affected, whether they have their landlord's consent or not.' The charitable reading of Little Real Estate's sentence is 'your lease usually had a no-pets clause' - which was often true as a matter of contract - but that is not what it says, and it is not a legal requirement that 'always' existed. The sentence also fights the rest of Little's own article, which correctly reports that 'The new pet laws don't apply to pets that have already been accepted and present in the rental property before the new laws commenced'. Dates, established from primary sources so the other findings can lean on them: the Victorian pets provisions (Residential Tenancies Act 1997 Part 2 Division 5B, ss 71A-71E) were inserted by s 47 of the Consumer Legislation Amendment Act 2019 and commenced on 2 MARCH 2020 - the Act's Table of Amendments records 'ss 46-49 on 2.3.20: Special Gazette (No. 91) 25.2.20 p. 1'. The broader Residential Tenancies Amendment Act 2018 reforms commenced on 29 MARCH 2021 (Special Gazette (No. 42) 27.1.21), and on that date s 61 of the 2018 Act substituted the whole of Division 5B, replacing 'landlord'/'tenant' with 'residential rental provider'/'renter' but leaving the mechanism unchanged. VCAT's own pets page, published July 2020, says 'Most of the reforms start on 1 July 2020' - that date was overtaken by COVID-era deferral and never happened; it is stale on the page to this day.
    - source: "Division 5B of Part 2 as inserted by the Consumer Legislation Amendment Act 2019 does not apply to a tenancy agreement in existence before the commencement of that Division, unless the tenant, on or after that commencement, introduces or wishes to keep a pet at the rented premises."
      Victorian Government (Chief Parliamentary Counsel), Consumer Legislation Amendment Act 2019 No. 47/2019, s 49 (inserting s 533 of the Residential Tenancies Act 1997) https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/efd17577-07a3-3ba6-935f-eacf6e9d39b7_19-047aa%20authorised.pdf
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf - Residential Tenancies Act 1997 v113: Part 2 Division 5B, and its provenance note 'Pt 2 Div. 5B (Heading and ss 71A-71E) inserted by No. 47/2019 s. 47, substituted by No. 45/2018 s. 61'; then the Table of Amendments entry for No. 47/2019, which gives 'ss 46-49 on 2.3.20: Special Gazette (No. 91) 25.2.20 p. 1'; then s 533, the transitional provision
    - searched: https://www.legislation.vic.gov.au/as-made/acts/consumer-legislation-amendment-act-2019 then https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/efd17577-07a3-3ba6-935f-eacf6e9d39b7_19-047aa%20authorised.pdf - Consumer Legislation Amendment Act 2019 as made; s 47 inserts Division 5B and s 49 inserts the s 533 transitional
    - searched: https://www.vcat.vic.gov.au/news/new-laws-about-pets-rental-properties - VCAT: 'From Monday 2 March 2020: a tenant must ask the landlord before they keep a pet on the rented property, using Consumer Affairs Victoria's approved form' and 'Tenants who already have a pet are not affected, whether they have their landlord's consent or not.'
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/repairs-alterations-safety-and-pets/pets - CAV's current pets page, checked for any statement that the requirement pre-dates 2020. There is none.
    - searched: https://www.littlerealestate.com.au/news/new-pet-laws-in-victoria-for-tenants - the retelling, dated 13 March 2020, eleven days after commencement
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf (audit re-check 2026-08-17)

- **[echoed-claim]** Turnbull Cook states that a landlord has 14 days commencing the day after receipt of the form to decide whether the pet may be kept.
  - as of: 2025-09-07
  - quote: "A landlord has 14 days commencing the day after receipt of the form to make a decision if the pet may be kept. Refusal to allow the pet may only be made on reasonable grounds."
  - source: https://www.turnbullcook.com.au/pets-in-strata-info-for-tenants-and-landlords-on-pets-in-rentals/
  - **BACKING FOUND** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - Backed. Section 71C(1) states the duty ('A residential rental provider must not unreasonably refuse to consent to the renter keeping a pet on the rented premises') and s 71E(1)(b), quoted above, is the gate: VCAT may make an order that refusal is reasonable only 'if satisfied that it is reasonable to refuse consent'. Section 71E(2) lists the matters it may weigh - the type of pet, the character and nature of the premises, the character and nature of the appliances, fixtures and fittings, whether refusal is permitted under any other Act, prescribed matters, and anything else it considers relevant. So Turnbull Cook's sentence is right. What Turnbull Cook gets wrong on the same page, though outside the traced claim, is who goes to VCAT: it writes 'a tenant can apply to VCAT for an order permitting the tenant to keep the pet'. There is no such application in the Act. Section 71D confers the right to apply on the residential rental provider only, and the s 71E(1)(a) order permitting the renter to keep the pet is something VCAT may make ON THE PROVIDER'S APPLICATION. A renter who does nothing after 14 days already has consent under s 71C(2); a renter who applies to VCAT is filing something the Division does not provide for.
    - source: "if satisfied that it is reasonable to refuse consent to the keeping of the pet on the rented premises"
      Victorian Government (Chief Parliamentary Counsel), Residential Tenancies Act 1997 version 113, s 71E(1)(b) https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf - Residential Tenancies Act 1997 v113 s 71C(1) (heading: 'Residential rental provider must not unreasonably refuse to consent to keep a pet on rented premises'), s 71D(1) and s 71E(1)(b) and (2)
    - searched: https://www.vcat.vic.gov.au/news/new-laws-about-pets-rental-properties - 'the landlord cannot unreasonably refuse consent'
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/repairs-alterations-safety-and-pets/pets - CAV's 'VCAT's role' section, listing the matters VCAT may consider
    - searched: https://www.turnbullcook.com.au/pets-in-strata-info-for-tenants-and-landlords-on-pets-in-rentals/ - the retelling

    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf - Residential Tenancies Act 1997 v113 s 71C(1) (heading: 'Residential rental provider must not unreasonably refuse to consent to keep a pet on rented premises'), s 71D(1) and s 71E(1)(b) and (2)
    - searched: https://www.vcat.vic.gov.au/news/new-laws-about-pets-rental-properties - 'the landlord cannot unreasonably refuse consent'
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/repairs-alterations-safety-and-pets/pets - CAV's 'VCAT's role' section, listing the matters VCAT may consider

- **[echoed-claim]** RentBuzz states that Victoria was the first Australian state to introduce comprehensive protections for pets in rentals.
  - as of: 2026-03-26
  - quote: "Victoria was the first Australian state to introduce such comprehensive protections for pet-owning renters."
  - source: https://rentbuzz.com.au/guides/pets-rental-victoria
  - **SEARCHED, NOTHING FOUND** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - Not found, and the nearest check goes the other way. No Victorian primary source - CAV, VCAT, or the legislation itself - claims Victoria was first at anything on pets, and RentBuzz cites nothing for it. Meanwhile the Australian Capital Territory got there first: the Residential Tenancies Amendment Act 2019 (ACT) (A2019-5) commenced on 1 NOVEMBER 2019, four months before Victoria's 2 March 2020, and it built the same machine - a tenant seeks consent (s 71AE), the lessor must apply to the tribunal to refuse (s 71AF), and the tribunal decides on enumerated grounds. The sentence survives only on the technicality that the ACT is a territory rather than a state, and RentBuzz shows no sign of having relied on that distinction: its own 'How This Compares to Other States' paragraph names Queensland (2022), New South Wales and Western Australia and does not mention the ACT at all. This has the classic profile of an unbacked field claim - a superlative with no citation, in a bibliography-styled article whose sources are listed without links and include an organisation renamed years earlier. Treat 'Victoria was first' as unsupported; 'Victoria was early, and its framework is the most tested through VCAT decisions' is the defensible version, and even that would need decision counts nobody has published.
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/repairs-alterations-safety-and-pets/pets - read end to end. CAV's pets page makes no comparative or 'first in Australia' claim.
    - searched: https://www.vcat.vic.gov.au/news/new-laws-about-pets-rental-properties - read end to end. VCAT frames the change as 'part of a reform package' under the Residential Tenancies Amendment Act 2018 and makes no interstate comparison.
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/new-changes-to-the-rental-laws - CAV's reform timetable page; no primacy claim anywhere on it
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/search?keywords=pets+first+in+Australia - CAV's site search; the URL 404s and the on-site search is JavaScript-driven, so no server-side result set to read
    - searched: https://rentbuzz.com.au/guides/pets-rental-victoria - the retelling itself, read in full including its 'Sources' block. The block is a bare bibliography with no URLs and no pinpoints: 'Residential Tenancies Act 1997 (Vic) ... legislation.vic.gov.au', 'Consumer Affairs Victoria - Pets and Renting. consumer.vic.gov.au', 'Tenants Union of Victoria - Pets in Rented Properties Factsheet. tuv.org.au'. Nothing in it addresses primacy, and the Tenants Union of Victoria was renamed Tenants Victoria in 2019, so that citation points at a body that has not existed under that name since before the law being described.
    - searched: https://www.legislation.act.gov.au/a/1997-84/ - ACT Residential Tenancies Act 1997 register page; its amendment table lists 'A2019-5 Residential Tenancies Amendment Act 2019' with commencement 1 November 2019 affecting pt 2
    - searched: https://www.legislation.act.gov.au/a/2019-5/ then https://www.legislation.act.gov.au/DownloadFile/a/2019-5/20191101-70714/PDF/2019-5.PDF - read the ACT amending Act. It inserts a new part 5AA into the ACT Act including s 71AE 'Process for tenant seeking consent - animals' and s 71AF 'Lessor to apply to ACAT for refusal - animals', with grounds including that 'the premises are unsuitable to keep the animal'. Same architecture as Victoria's, four months earlier.
- **[echoed-claim]** For pets in rentals, RentBuzz states that the burden falls on the landlord to demonstrate that their refusal of a pet request is reasonable.
  - as of: 2026-03-26
  - quote: "It is important to understand that the burden falls on the landlord to demonstrate that their refusal is reasonable."
  - source: https://rentbuzz.com.au/guides/pets-rental-victoria
  - **BACKING FOUND** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - Backed in substance, though the Act never uses the phrase. Division 5B places the onus on the provider structurally rather than by naming a burden of proof, and it does so twice over. First, the default outcome is consent: under s 71C(2), quoted above, silence for 14 days IS consent, so the renter who does nothing wins and the provider who does nothing loses. Second, the provider is the moving party: s 71D(1) lets a provider 'apply to the Tribunal for an order that it is reasonable for the residential rental provider to refuse consent to keep the pet on the rented premises', and s 71E(1)(b) lets VCAT grant that order only 'if satisfied that it is reasonable to refuse consent'. The party who must satisfy the Tribunal is the applicant, i.e. the provider. RentBuzz's second sentence, 'The tenant does not need to prove their pet will be perfectly behaved', follows from that structure and is fair; its third, that the landlord 'must show concrete, evidence-based reasons', overstates a statute whose s 71E(2) list is discretionary ('the Tribunal MAY have regard to') and open-ended ('any other matter the Tribunal considers relevant'). Worth recording that RentBuzz gets this one right while getting the primacy claim wrong on the same page - accuracy is not uniform across the article.
    - source: "The residential rental provider is taken to have consented to a request to the keeping of a pet on the rented premises unless, within 14 days after being given the request, the residential rental provider applies to the Tribunal under section 71D."
      Victorian Government (Chief Parliamentary Counsel), Residential Tenancies Act 1997 version 113, s 71C(2) https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf - Residential Tenancies Act 1997 v113 ss 71A, 71C(2), 71D(1), 71E(1). Searched Division 5B for 'burden', 'onus' and 'proof': none appear.
    - searched: https://www.vcat.vic.gov.au/news/new-laws-about-pets-rental-properties - 'the landlord has 14 days from receiving the request to apply to VCAT for permission to refuse, or consent is taken to have been given.'
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/repairs-alterations-safety-and-pets/pets - 'Rental providers can apply to VCAT for an order to refuse permission' and 'If the rental provider does not respond at all within the 14 days the renter can keep the pet.'
    - searched: https://rentbuzz.com.au/guides/pets-rental-victoria - the retelling, section 'The Burden of Proof'; it cites nothing for the proposition

## rental application (6)

- **[echoed-claim]** Urban Renters asserts that most property management agents will ask a rental applicant to provide identification that adds up to around 100 points.
  - as of: 2026-01-20
  - quote: "Before submitting your rental application, most property management agents will ask you to provide identification that adds up to around 100 points."
  - source: https://urbanrenters.com.au/blog/rental-application-tips/
  - **A PRIMARY SOURCE SAYS THE OPPOSITE** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - The regulator caps identity documents at two, not at a points total. The same words appear in the prescribed form itself - Schedule 1 Form 3A item 7 of the Residential Tenancies Regulations 2021 reads 'A rental provider or their agent can request no more than two identity documents from the following list—' - and the form is compulsory: s 30AC of the Act says a provider or agent 'must not prepare or authorise the preparation of an application form used to apply to enter into a residential rental agreement in a form that is not in the prescribed form', with a penalty of 60 penalty units (300 for a body corporate). Regulation 15(2) then makes 'any information other than the information set out in Form 3A in Schedule 1' prohibited information under s 30C, and CAV lists 'any questions that are not on the new rental application form' among questions renters cannot be asked. The regime took effect 31 March 2026. A 100-point request is therefore not merely unnecessary in Victoria, it is a request the agent is forbidden to make. The 100-point concept is the Commonwealth financial-sector customer identification procedure (the FTR Act 100 point check used for opening bank accounts) imported wholesale into rental advice; it has never had a basis in Victorian tenancy law, and neither the Act nor the Regulations contains the phrase. Worth noting how this one fails: the Urban Renters page carries a modified date of 8 July 2026 - more than three months after the two-document cap commenced - never mentions Victoria or any jurisdiction anywhere in its text, and cites no source at all. It is generic national-market advice being read as Victorian rules.
    - source: "A rental provider or their agent can request no more than two identity documents from this list:"
      Consumer Affairs Victoria https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/starting-and-changing-rental-agreements/applying-signing-and-moving-in/applying-for-a-property
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/starting-and-changing-rental-agreements/applying-signing-and-moving-in/applying-for-a-property (CAV's own page on what may be asked for)
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-06/21-3sra009-authorised.pdf (Residential Tenancies Regulations 2021, regs 14A/14B/15 and Schedule 1 Form 3A item 7 'Verification of identity')
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf (Residential Tenancies Act 1997 ss 30AC, 30B, 30BA, 30C)
    - searched: full-text search of the Act and the Regulations for '100 point' / '100 points': zero occurrences in either instrument
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/search-results?query=100+points+of+identification (CAV site search)
    - searched: https://urbanrenters.com.au/blog/rental-application-tips/ (the retelling itself: audited for outbound citations - it links to no legislation, no regulator page, nothing)
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/starting-and-changing-rental-agreements/applying-signing-and-moving-in
- **[echoed-claim]** Urban Renters advises rental applicants to have a short pet resume ready before the inspection if they are a pet owner.
  - as of: 2026-01-20
  - quote: "Have your documents ready before the inspection. This includes identification, proof of income (such as recent pay slips), references, and any pet information, pet references, or a short pet resume if you’re a pet owner looking for pet-friendly rentals."
  - source: https://urbanrenters.com.au/blog/rental-application-tips/
  - **SEARCHED, NOTHING FOUND** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - No primary source exists for the pet resume, because it is not a legal artefact - it is an industry convention. Victoria's statutory pet pathway runs the other way and starts after the agreement, not during the application: under s 71B a renter who requests consent to keep a pet 'must do so in the form approved by the Director', and under s 71C the provider must not unreasonably refuse and has 14 days to apply to VCAT or be taken to have consented. The prescribed application form (Form 3A) has no pet item at all, and from 31 March 2026 an agent must not require information outside that form (s 30C with reg 15(2)). So a pet resume is something an applicant may volunteer, and nothing forbids volunteering it, but the advice implies a step in the Victorian process that does not exist - and following it hands over information the agent is not permitted to demand. Not contradicted, because the claim is framed as tactics rather than as law.
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-06/21-3sra009-authorised.pdf (Schedule 1 Form 3A, all 11 items of Part A - there is no pet item; the form's only capped-document categories are financial (item 6) and identity (item 7))
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf (Act ss 71A, 71B, 71C - the statutory pet pathway)
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/starting-and-changing-rental-agreements/applying-signing-and-moving-in/applying-for-a-property (searched for 'pet': the page's list of what may be requested contains no pet category)
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/repairs-alterations-safety-and-pets/pets (CAV's pets page - zero occurrences of 'resume')
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/search-results?query=pet+resume+rental+application (CAV site search)
    - searched: https://urbanrenters.com.au/blog/rental-application-tips/ (cites nothing for the claim)
- **[echoed-claim]** Urban Renters asserts that a cover letter gives a rental applicant the chance to introduce themselves beyond the standard completed application form.
  - as of: 2026-01-20
  - quote: "Your well-written cover letter gives you the chance to introduce yourself beyond the standard completed application form."
  - source: https://urbanrenters.com.au/blog/rental-application-tips/
  - **SEARCHED, NOTHING FOUND** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - Nothing primary supports or measures this. It is a claim about what improves an applicant's odds, and there is no CAV guidance, VCAT material, regulation or study behind it; CAV's own application checklist is 'Read through and complete the rental application form', be able to pay bond and first month's rent, and 'Sign your completed application form, include all required attachments and send it back'. There is a live tension worth recording: CAV states that 'A rental provider or agent can only ask for the information set out in the prescribed rental application form', and among the questions renters cannot be asked are 'any questions that are not on the new rental application form'. A volunteered cover letter is not prohibited - the restriction binds the agent's requests, not the applicant's offers - but it routes personal information (household composition, circumstances, protected attributes) around a form deliberately narrowed in March 2026 to stop exactly that being collected. The advice is not backed, and its unstated cost is not disclosed by the reteller.
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/starting-and-changing-rental-agreements/applying-signing-and-moving-in/applying-for-a-property (CAV 'Application checklist' section - three steps, none of them a cover letter)
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-06/21-3sra009-authorised.pdf (Form 3A - no free-text or personal-statement item)
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/search-results?query=cover+letter+rental+application (CAV site search)
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf (ss 29C-30E, the application and discrimination provisions)
    - searched: https://urbanrenters.com.au/blog/rental-application-tips/ (no citation offered)
- **[echoed-claim]** Urban Renters advises rental applicants to treat a property inspection like a relaxed job interview.
  - as of: 2026-01-20
  - quote: "Treat the inspection like a relaxed job interview for your future home. Dress neatly in smart casual clothing to make a positive first impression."
  - source: https://urbanrenters.com.au/blog/rental-application-tips/
  - **SEARCHED, NOTHING FOUND** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - Pure presentation folklore. There is no primary source - no CAV guidance, no VCAT material, no study - saying that dress or demeanour at an inspection affects application outcomes, and no dataset anyone could point at to test it. What the primary sources do say cuts against the framing: CAV states 'A rental provider has the right to choose the person they think is the most suitable applicant for their property', but immediately qualifies that it is unlawful to refuse on protected characteristics, and the Act carries express anti-discrimination provisions for rental applications at ss 29C-30A. The advice quietly recasts a legally constrained assessment of suitability (identity, capacity to pay, rental history - the only things Form 3A collects) as an impression-management exercise, which is the part that has no source behind it.
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/starting-and-changing-rental-agreements/applying-signing-and-moving-in/applying-for-a-property (CAV on how a provider chooses a renter)
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf (ss 29C-30A, discrimination in relation to residential rental agreements)
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/search-results?query=100+points+of+identification and related CAV site searches for inspection presentation guidance
    - searched: https://urbanrenters.com.au/blog/rental-application-tips/ (asserts it with no source; the surrounding text adds 'Avoid overly casual items like shorts or thongs')

- **[echoed-claim]** Urban Renters asserts that agents tend to prioritise complete rental applications submitted first, especially in areas with low vacancy rates.
  - as of: 2026-01-20
  - quote: "Agents tend to prioritise complete applications submitted first, especially in areas with low vacancy rates."
  - source: https://urbanrenters.com.au/blog/rental-application-tips/
  - **SEARCHED, NOTHING FOUND** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - This is an empirical claim about agent behaviour and there is no primary source for it anywhere: no CAV guidance, no VCAT material, no REIV or government dataset breaking application outcomes down by submission order or vacancy rate. The Victorian legal position is only that the choice is the provider's - CAV: 'A rental provider has the right to choose the person they think is the most suitable applicant for their property' - constrained by the discrimination rules. Two things make the claim self-serving rather than merely unbacked: it is published by a letting agency, and it converts an unmeasured belief into urgency for the applicant ('lodge your application as soon as you leave the viewing'), which pushes people to submit documents before they have read the pricing and information rules CAV publishes on the same subject. The 'especially in areas with low vacancy rates' qualifier is the giveaway - it names a real, published statistic to lend weight to a proposition that statistic does not measure.
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/starting-and-changing-rental-agreements/applying-signing-and-moving-in/applying-for-a-property (CAV on the provider's decision to choose a renter - no ordering or first-in rule)
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf (ss 30AC-30E; nothing imposes or describes an order-of-receipt process)
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-06/21-3sra009-authorised.pdf (Form 3A and regs 14A/14B/15 - the form has no timestamp or queue concept)
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/search-results?query=cover+letter+rental+application and CAV site searches for application-processing order
    - searched: https://urbanrenters.com.au/blog/rental-application-tips/ (the source page; no survey, no agency data, no citation - the adjacent tip is headed 'Submit Your Application Immediately After Viewing')

## bond (5)

- **[echoed-claim]** Landlord Wise states that a Victorian rental provider must pay the bond to the RTBA within 10 business days of receiving it.
  - as of: 2026-04-06
  - quote: "You must pay the bond to the RTBA within 10 business days of receiving it."
  - quote: "Rental Bond VIC — Complete Guide for Self-Managing Landlords"
  - source: https://landlordwise.com.au/guides/rental-bond-vic/
  - **BACKING FOUND** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - DUPLICATE, our error not the field's. The corpus already carries this from the primary source: the 'bond' subject in /tmp/vic- records claim 5, 'Section 406 of the Residential Tenancies Act 1997 requires a Victorian residential rental provider to give the bond to the Authority within 10 business days after it is received, with a penalty of 150 penalty units', citing the same authorised PDF. The three retellings (Landlord Wise, Aussie Rent Laws, PropertyNow) were recorded alongside it and flagged as unsourced repetition. They are accurate. Section 406 is headed 'Duty to pay bond to Authority' and carries a penalty of 150 penalty units; the duty is unchanged across authorised versions 110 (6 Aug 2025), 111 (25 Nov 2025) and 113 (1 July 2026). Worth recording: the odd one out is Consumer Affairs Victoria's own bond page, which states 'If the renter paid the rental provider, the rental provider must lodge the bond with the RTBA within 14 days (not including public holidays) of receiving the bond amount.' That is a different and looser period than the Act's 10 business days, and the corpus already notes the conflict (claims 4, 6 and 7). The blog retellings track the statute; CAV's guidance page does not.
    - source: "provider must, within 10 business days after the amount of bond is received, give the amount of bond to the Authority together with the completed bond lodgment form."
      Victorian Government, Office of the Chief Parliamentary Counsel (legislation.vic.gov.au) https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf
    - searched: https://landlordwise.com.au/guides/rental-bond-vic/ - the retelling; states the 10-business-day duty without naming a section or citing the Act
    - searched: https://aussierentlaws.com/victoria/how-to-lodge-bond-rtba-victoria - the second retelling; same period, no section cited
    - searched: https://help.propertynow.com.au/en/article/how-to-lodge-the-bond-in-vic-1rq8iyd/ - the third retelling; same period, no section cited
    - searched: https://www5.austlii.edu.au/cgi-bin/viewdoc/au/legis/vic/consol_act/rta1997207/s406.html - AustLII copy of s 406, blocked by Cloudflare ('Sorry, you have been blocked'), so the authorised PDF was used instead
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/rent-bond-bills-and-condition-reports/bond/lodging-the-bond-with-the-rtba - CAV's lodging page; describes who lodges and how, but states no statutory period
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/rent-bond-bills-and-condition-reports/bond/bond-amounts-and-paying-a-bond - CAV's bond page; gives the period as '14 days (not including public holidays)', not 10 business days
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/rent-bond-bills-and-condition-reports/bond/rental-provider-administration-and-managing-bond-transactions - checked for a stated lodgement period; none
    - searched: https://www.legislation.vic.gov.au/in-force/acts/residential-tenancies-act-1997/167 - version index for the Act
    - searched: probed content.legislation.vic.gov.au for the current authorised consolidation: 97-109aa110 (6 Aug 2025), 97-109aa111 (25 Nov 2025) and 97-109aa113 (1 July 2026) all resolve; aa113 is the latest, aa114+ 404
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf - read s 406 in the current authorised consolidation, verified contiguous under both pdftotext and pdftotext -layout
- **[echoed-claim]** Landlord Wise states that Victoria does not allow a separate pet bond.
  - as of: 2026-04-06
  - quote: "No Pet Bond: Victoria does not allow a separate pet bond — the maximum bond is one month's rent regardless of whether pets are permitted"
  - quote: "Rental Bond VIC — Complete Guide for Self-Managing Landlords"
  - source: https://landlordwise.com.au/guides/rental-bond-vic/
  - **BACKING FOUND** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - The quote is deliberately cut before the closing words because the page renders the term inside curly single quotes (HTML &lsquo;/&rsquo;); the full sentence on the page is: "Rental providers and owners cannot ask for an additional bond as a 'pet bond'." It sits under the page's 'Pet bonds' heading, and the page was last updated 23 April 2025. The statute agrees by construction rather than by naming pets: s 31(1) of the Residential Tenancies Act 1997 makes it an offence (60 penalty units) to demand or accept a bond exceeding one month's rent, and the Act's pets division (Division 5B) confers no power to take a further bond. So the Landlord Wise assertion is correct and CAV states it directly.
    - source: "Rental providers and owners cannot ask for an additional bond as a"
      Consumer Affairs Victoria https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/repairs-alterations-safety-and-pets/pets

    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/repairs-alterations-safety-and-pets/pets - CAV's pets page, which has a dedicated 'Pet bonds' heading; this is the primary statement
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/rent-bond-bills-and-condition-reports/bond/bond-amounts-and-paying-a-bond - CAV's bond page; sets out the maximum bond rules and mentions no pet bond at all
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf - grepped the whole Act for 'pet'; Division 5B (Pets, ss 71A-71E) governs consent to keep a pet and VCAT orders about pets, and creates no bond entitlement of any kind
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf - read s 31 ('What is the maximum bond?'), s 32 and s 33; the only routes above the cap are a VCAT order under s 33 and the s 31(3) prescribed-rent exception, neither of which mentions pets
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-06/21-3sra009-authorised.pdf - Residential Tenancies Regulations 2021; no pet-bond regulation exists

- **[echoed-claim]** A Victorian landlord who collected the bond themselves must lodge the bond within 10 business days of receiving the bond.
  - as of: 2026-03-02
  - quote: "If you collected the bond yourself, you must lodge within 10 business days of receiving it."
  - quote: "How to lodge the bond in VIC"
  - source: https://help.propertynow.com.au/en/article/how-to-lodge-the-bond-in-vic-1rq8iyd/
  - **BACKING FOUND** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - DUPLICATE, our error not the field's. The corpus already carries this from the primary source: the 'bond' subject in /tmp/vic- records claim 5, 'Section 406 of the Residential Tenancies Act 1997 requires a Victorian residential rental provider to give the bond to the Authority within 10 business days after it is received, with a penalty of 150 penalty units', citing the same authorised PDF. The three retellings (Landlord Wise, Aussie Rent Laws, PropertyNow) were recorded alongside it and flagged as unsourced repetition. They are accurate. Section 406 is headed 'Duty to pay bond to Authority' and carries a penalty of 150 penalty units; the duty is unchanged across authorised versions 110 (6 Aug 2025), 111 (25 Nov 2025) and 113 (1 July 2026). Worth recording: the odd one out is Consumer Affairs Victoria's own bond page, which states 'If the renter paid the rental provider, the rental provider must lodge the bond with the RTBA within 14 days (not including public holidays) of receiving the bond amount.' That is a different and looser period than the Act's 10 business days, and the corpus already notes the conflict (claims 4, 6 and 7). The blog retellings track the statute; CAV's guidance page does not.
    - source: "provider must, within 10 business days after the amount of bond is received, give the amount of bond to the Authority together with the completed bond lodgment form."

## notice of intention to vacate (5)

- **[echoed-claim]** Aussie Rent Laws states that the standard notice of intention to vacate for most tenants in Victoria is 28 days for periodic tenancies.
  - as of: 2026-03-09
  - quote: "The standard notice for most tenants in Victoria is 28 days for periodic tenancies, but fixed-term agreements and special situations (for example, ending due to family violence or breach) may alter the timeframe."
  - source: https://aussierentlaws.com/victoria/renter-notice-intention-to-vacate
  - **BACKING FOUND** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - Backed twice over. The statute is s 91Z(2) of the Residential Tenancies Act 1997: a renter's notice of intention to vacate 'must specify a termination date that is not less than 28 days after the date on which the notice is given'. The 28 days is not limited to periodic agreements - it is the general renter's notice period - and s 91ZA makes such a notice of no effect if it names a date before the end of a fixed term. The retelling's caveat about shorter timeframes is also right: s 91ZB drops the period to 14 days in listed circumstances, and s 91V allows a family-violence application to VCAT instead of a notice.
    - source: "Renters must give 28 days' notice if they want to leave at the end of a rental agreement (lease)."
      Consumer Affairs Victoria https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/moving-out-giving-notice-and-evictions/giving-notice-as-a-renter/renter-giving-notice
    - searched: https://aussierentlaws.com/victoria/renter-notice-intention-to-vacate (the retelling; it links out to consumer.vic.gov.au, legislation.vic.gov.au and vcat.vic.gov.au but does not cite a section)
    - searched: web search (site:consumer.vic.gov.au): consumer.vic.gov.au notice of intention to vacate renter ending rental agreement 28 days
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/moving-out-giving-notice-and-evictions/giving-notice-as-a-renter/renter-giving-notice
    - searched: https://www.legislation.vic.gov.au/in-force/acts/residential-tenancies-act-1997/106 and /113 (version history - current in-force version is 113, as at 1 July 2026)
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf - read s 91Z (Notice of intention to vacate), s 91ZA (notice of no effect during a fixed term) and s 91ZB (reduced 14-day periods)
- **[echoed-claim]** Aussie Rent Laws states there is no single prescribed government form for a tenant's Notice of Intention to Vacate in Victoria.
  - as of: 2026-03-09
  - quote: "There is no single prescribed government form for a tenant’s Notice of Intention to Vacate in Victoria. A clear written notice including your details, the property address and the vacate date is sufficient; templates and guidance are available from Consumer Affairs Victoria."
  - source: https://aussierentlaws.com/victoria/renter-notice-intention-to-vacate
  - **BACKING FOUND** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - Backed, and the statute is cleaner than the regulator's phrasing. Section 91ZZN of the Residential Tenancies Act 1997 says a notice of intention to vacate 'is not valid unless- (a) it is in writing; and (b) it is signed by the person giving the notice or by that person's agent' - no prescribed form. The contrast with s 91ZZO, which does require a notice TO vacate to be 'in the relevant prescribed form' (Form 6, prescribed by reg 37 of the Residential Tenancies Regulations 2021), is deliberate. I also checked the Regulations end to end for a renter's notice-of-intention-to-vacate form and there is none. The only correction to the retelling: the notice must be signed, which its 'clear written notice' phrasing leaves out.
    - source: "Although it is not compulsory to use this form"
      Consumer Affairs Victoria https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/moving-out-giving-notice-and-evictions/giving-notice-as-a-renter/renter-giving-notice
    - searched: https://aussierentlaws.com/victoria/renter-notice-intention-to-vacate (the retelling)
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/moving-out-giving-notice-and-evictions/giving-notice-as-a-renter/renter-giving-notice
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/library/forms/housing-and-accommodation/renting/notice-of-intention-to-vacate-rented-premises.docx (the CAV template itself)
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf - s 91ZZN 'Form of notice of intention to vacate' (writing + signature only) versus s 91ZZO 'Form of notice to vacate' (prescribed form required)
    - searched: web search (site:legislation.vic.gov.au): Residential Tenancies Regulations 2021 Victoria authorised version pdf prescribed forms schedule
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2021-04/21-003sra%20authorised.PDF - searched the Regulations for any prescribed notice of intention to vacate form; regs 37, 52, 72 and 89 prescribe notice TO vacate forms (Forms 6, 16, 22) and there is no prescribed renter's notice of INTENTION to vacate form
- **[echoed-claim]** Before the November 2025 reforms, rental providers could issue a 'no reason' notice to vacate at the end of a fixed-term agreement under sections 91ZZD and 91ZZDA of the Act.
  - as of: 2026-04-06
  - quote: "Before the November 2025 reforms, rental providers could issue a “no reason” notice to vacate at the end of a fixed-term agreement under sections 91ZZD and 91ZZDA of the Act."
  - source: https://landlordwise.com.au/guides/notice-to-vacate-vic/
  - **BACKING FOUND** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - Backed, with the section numbers exactly right - unusual for a property blog. In the last pre-reform authorised version (No. 110, as at 6 August 2025) s 91ZZD covered fixed terms of not more than 5 years and s 91ZZDA fixed terms of more than 5 years; neither required the provider to state a ground, which is what 'no reason' means. Both were repealed by s 25 of the Consumer and Planning Legislation Amendment (Housing Statement Reform) Act 2025 (No. 6/2025), which the RTA 1997 endnote records as commencing 25 November 2025, and s 534 preserves notices already issued. Two precisions the retelling drops: the notice could only end the INITIAL fixed term (s 91ZZD(1) 'at the end of the initial fixed term'), not any renewal, and it still carried minimum lead times of 90 days (or 60 days for terms under 6 months). Consumer Affairs Victoria states the post-reform position as 'Rental providers cannot issue a renter with a notice to vacate without a valid reason, even at the end of a fixed-term agreement.'
    - source: "91ZZD End of fixed term residential rental agreement of"
      Victorian Government (Chief Parliamentary Counsel), legislation.vic.gov.au https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2025-08/97-109aa110-authorised.pdf
    - searched: https://landlordwise.com.au/guides/notice-to-vacate-vic/ (the retelling; its only outbound citation is one consumer.vic.gov.au link)
    - searched: https://www.legislation.vic.gov.au/in-force/acts/residential-tenancies-act-1997/110 (pre-reform authorised version, as at 6 August 2025)
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2025-08/97-109aa110-authorised.pdf - ss 91ZZD and 91ZZDA in force, neither requiring a reason
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf - both sections now shown as 'repealed by No. 6/2025 s. 25', plus transitional s 534 and the endnote 'ss 3-51 on 25.11.25: s. 2(2)'
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/new-changes-to-the-rental-laws (CAV's 25 November 2025 changes page)
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/moving-out-giving-notice-and-evictions/notice-to-vacate/notice-to-vacate-in-rental-properties
- **[echoed-claim]** Landlord Wise states the minimum notice period for a mortgagee notice to vacate is 60 days.
  - as of: 2026-04-06
  - quote: "The minimum notice period for a mortgagee notice is 60 days."
  - source: https://landlordwise.com.au/guides/notice-to-vacate-vic/
  - **BACKING FOUND** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - Backed. Section 91ZZK(2) of the Residential Tenancies Act 1997 (authorised version 113, p. 218): 'The notice must specify a termination date that is not less than 60 days after the date on which the notice is given.' This is worth stating precisely because it is a survivor: the Consumer and Planning Legislation Amendment (Housing Statement Reform) Act 2025 lifted most no-fault notice periods from 60 to 90 days (see s 91ZZC(2), amended by No. 6/2025 s. 24), and CAV's notice-to-vacate table now reads '90 days (previously 60 days)' down the column, but s 91ZZK was left at 60. Also unmentioned by the retelling: s 91ZZK(4) bars the notice entirely where the mortgagee consented to the letting.
    - source: "not less than 60 days after the date on which the"
      Victorian Government (Chief Parliamentary Counsel), legislation.vic.gov.au https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf

For more information, refer to the Residential Tenancies Act or contact us.'
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf - s 91ZZK 'Notice by mortgagee', subsection (2), at p. 218 of the current authorised version

- **[echoed-claim]** Landlord Wise states that a letter, email or text message is not a valid notice of intention to vacate in Victoria even if it contains all the required information.
  - as of: 2026-04-06
  - quote: "A letter, email, or text message is not a valid notice to vacate, even if it contains all the required information."
  - source: https://landlordwise.com.au/guides/notice-to-vacate-vic/
  - **BACKING FOUND** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - Backed on the substance, but the retelling picks the wrong axis and could mislead a reader. Section 91ZZO says 'A notice to vacate given under this Division is not valid unless- (a) it is in the relevant prescribed form', and reg 37 of the Residential Tenancies Regulations 2021 prescribes Form 6. So an ad-hoc letter, email or text is indeed invalid however complete its contents - the defect is the form, not the medium. What is wrong is the implication that email cannot be used: Consumer Affairs Victoria's notice-to-vacate page lists 'electronic communication (such as email), if the renter has given consent to receive notices and other documents this way' as a permitted delivery method, so emailing the completed prescribed form is valid service. Note also that s 91ZZO(a) is only one of six cumulative requirements - (b) addressed to the renter, (c) signed, (d) reasons stated, (e) supporting evidence for the listed grounds, (f) termination date - so form compliance alone does not save a notice either.
    - source: "(a) it is in the relevant prescribed form; and"
      Victorian Government (Chief Parliamentary Counsel), legislation.vic.gov.au https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf
    - searched: https://landlordwise.com.au/guides/notice-to-vacate-vic/ (the retelling)
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf - s 91ZZO 'Form of notice to vacate', paragraphs (a) to (f)
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2021-04/21-003sra%20authorised.PDF - reg 37: 'For the purposes of section 91ZZO(a) of the Act, the prescribed form is Form 6 in Schedule 1.'
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/moving-out-giving-notice-and-evictions/notice-to-vacate/notice-to-vacate-in-rental-properties - CAV's delivery rules, including electronic communication
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/library/forms/housing-and-accommodation/renting/notice-to-vacate-to-renter-of-rented-premises.docx (the prescribed form as published by CAV)

## periodic agreement (5)

- **[echoed-claim]** Compton Green states that the legislation will abolish the End of Fixed Term Notice to Vacate for both agreements of less than five years and agreements of more than five years.
  - as of: 2025-10-16
  - quote: "The legislation will abolish the End of Fixed Term Notice to Vacate for both agreements of less than five years and agreements of more than five years. If the Notice to Vacate is issued before the 25th November 2025, it will remain in force after the new legislation is in effect."
  - source: https://www.comptongreen.com.au/post?post_id=17219
  - **BACKING FOUND** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - Backed on every limb, and unusually precisely. The two repealed sections are exactly the two Compton Green describes: s 91ZZD was headed 'End of fixed term residential rental agreement of not more than 5 years' and s 91ZZDA 'End of fixed term residential rental agreement of more than 5 years'. Both were repealed by s 25 of the Consumer and Planning Legislation Amendment (Housing Statement Reform) Act 2025 ('Sections 91ZZD and 91ZZDA of the Residential Tenancies Act 1997 are repealed.'), and the Act's Table of Amendments dates the commencement of ss 3-51 of that Act to 25.11.25 - Compton Green's date is right. The saving of notices already issued is s 534, quoted above; it saves them 'until the termination date specified in the notice as if those sections had not been repealed', which is slightly more generous than Compton Green's 'it will remain in force' but the same in substance. On the brief's separate question - whether 'the tenancy rolls over automatically' has a primary source: it does, and it is not merely a CAV gloss. Section 91Q of the Act is headed 'Creation of periodic residential rental agreement' and deems the renter to occupy under a periodic agreement where 'the term of a fixed term residential rental agreement ends' and 'the renter under that agreement continues in occupation of the rented premises' otherwise than under a new fixed term. Section 91Q(3)(a) carries the old terms across: 'in the case of a residential rental agreement for a fixed term not exceeding 5 years, is on the same terms, so far as applicable, as the terms of the fixed term residential rental agreement'. That deeming provision predates the 2025 reform - what changed on 25 November 2025 is that the provider lost the no-reason exit that used to sit alongside it.
    - source: "Despite the repeal of sections 91ZZD and 91ZZDA by Part 2 of the Consumer and Planning Legislation Amendment (Housing Statement Reform) Act 2025, a notice to vacate issued under either of those sections as in force immediately before the repeal of those sections continues in force"
      Victorian Government (Chief Parliamentary Counsel), Residential Tenancies Act 1997 version 113, s 534 https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/new-changes-to-the-rental-laws - CAV's reform timetable; the 25 November 2025 row 'Ban on no fault evictions' says 'Rental providers cannot issue a renter with a notice to vacate without a valid reason, even at the end of a fixed-term agreement.' CAV never names the repealed sections, so I followed it into the statute book.
    - searched: https://www.legislation.vic.gov.au/in-force/acts/residential-tenancies-act-1997/113 - confirmed version 113 is the in-force version, effective 1 July 2026
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf - Residential Tenancies Act 1997 v113. Part 2 Division 10 now jumps from s 91ZZC straight to s 91ZZE, with a starred gap and the endnote 'Ss 91ZZD, 91ZZDA inserted by No. 45/2018 s. 236 ..., repealed by No. 6/2025 s. 25.' Section 534 is the saving provision. The Table of Amendments entry for No. 6/2025 gives the commencement: 'ss 3-51 on 25.11.25: s. 2(2)'.
    - searched: https://www.legislation.vic.gov.au/as-made/acts/consumer-and-planning-legislation-amendment-housing-statement-reform-act-2025 then https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2025-03/25-006aa-authorised.pdf - the repealing Act itself; s 25 reads 'Sections 91ZZD and 91ZZDA of the Residential Tenancies Act 1997 are repealed.'
    - searched: https://www.legislation.vic.gov.au/as-made/acts/residential-tenancies-amendment-act-2018 then https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/bd1ef6a2-1113-36c3-9bbc-caed05c175cc_18-045aa%20authorised.pdf - to recover the heading of the repealed s 91ZZD: 'End of fixed term residential rental agreement of not more than 5 years'
    - searched: https://www.legislation.vic.gov.au/as-made/acts/consumer-legislation-amendment-act-2019 then https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/efd17577-07a3-3ba6-935f-eacf6e9d39b7_19-047aa%20authorised.pdf - s 91ZZDA was added later, by this Act amending s 236 of the 2018 Act; its heading is 'End of fixed term residential rental agreement of more than 5 years'
    - searched: https://www.comptongreen.com.au/post?post_id=17219 - the retelling; it cites no source at all, it simply asserts the change
- **[echoed-claim]** Compton Green states that the current Notice to Vacate period is 60 days.
  - as of: 2025-10-16
  - quote: "The current Notice to Vacate period is 60 days; this will be increased to 90 days’ notice on the 25th of November 2025."
