  - **NOT YET CHECKED.** Nobody has gone looking for a source for this one, so it says nothing either way.
- **[echoed-claim]** Collings Real Estate states that the 12-month limit on rent increases applies regardless of whether the property is on a fixed-term or periodic month-to-month agreement.
  - as of: 2026-06-26
  - quote: "This 12-month limit applies regardless of whether the property is on a fixed-term or periodic (month-to-month) agreement."
  - source: https://www.collings.com.au/rent-increases-in-victoria-rules-and-limits/
  - **BACKING FOUND** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - Backed, with one omission worth flagging. The operative provision is s 44(4A) of the Residential Tenancies Act 1997: 'A residential rental provider must not increase the rent payable under a residential rental agreement at intervals of less than 12 months.' It is drafted without reference to agreement type, which is precisely Collings' point, so the 'regardless of whether the property is on a fixed-term or periodic agreement' framing is sound. (I could not quote s 44(4A) as the backing string because in the authorised PDF the marginal amendment notes interleave through those three lines, so the sentence is not contiguous under a layout-preserving extraction; CAV's own statement of the same rule is quoted instead.) The omission: Collings says 'regardless', but CAV says 'in most cases' and then lists the carve-out - 'for periodic rental agreements that started before 19 June 2019, the rent can't be increased more than once every 6 months', with the identical carve-out for fixed-term agreements that started before that date. Those legacy agreements are on a 6-month interval, not 12. A second point Collings gets wrong on the same page, though it is outside the traced claim: it states the notice period as 'a minimum 60 days written notice' in a guide dated 2026. Section 44(1) was amended by No. 6/2025 s 11 with effect from 25 November 2025 and now requires 'at least 90 days notice'.
    - source: "In most cases, rent cannot be increased more than once every 12 months, even if the rental agreement specifies a rent increase before that time."
      Consumer Affairs Victoria https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/rent-bond-bills-and-condition-reports/rent/rent-increases
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/rent-bond-bills-and-condition-reports/rent/rent-increases - CAV's rent increases page, sections 'How often rent can be increased', 'Fixed-term agreements' and 'Periodic (month by month) agreements'
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf - Residential Tenancies Act 1997 v113 s 44, read in full. s 44(4A): 'A residential rental provider must not increase the rent payable under a residential rental agreement at intervals of less than 12 months.' No agreement type appears in the provision.
    - searched: https://www.collings.com.au/rent-increases-in-victoria-rules-and-limits/ - the retelling itself; it attributes the rule to 'the sweeping reforms introduced through the Residential Tenancies Amendment Act 2018' but gives no section and no link, so I followed it to the Act
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/bd1ef6a2-1113-36c3-9bbc-caed05c175cc_18-045aa%20authorised.pdf - Residential Tenancies Amendment Act 2018 as made, s 34, to confirm the 12-month interval is that Act's doing
    - searched: RTA v113 Table of Amendments, entry for No. 45/2018 - 'ss 34(5)(c), 53(4), 368 on 19.6.19', which is where CAV's 19 June 2019 cutover comes from
- **[echoed-claim]** Forge Property states that landlords in Victoria can increase rent once every 12 months for periodic leases.
  - as of: 2026-02-20
  - quote: "In Victoria, landlords can increase rent once every 12 months for periodic leases or at the end of a fixed-term lease if specified in the agreement"
  - source: https://www.forgeproperty.com.au/en-au/media/what-are-the-rules-for-rent-increases-and-form-3a-in-victoria-in-2026
  - **BACKING FOUND** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - Backed, and already in the corpus from the same regulator page - a duplicate rather than a new idea. Statutory source is s 44(4A) of the Act. The only qualification is the legacy one CAV states immediately above the quoted line: 'for periodic rental agreements that started before 19 June 2019, the rent can't be increased more than once every 6 months'. Forge does acknowledge that exception elsewhere on its page.
    - source: "for rental agreements that became periodic on or after 19 June 2019, the rent can’t be increased more than once every 12 months"
      Consumer Affairs Victoria https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/rent-bond-bills-and-condition-reports/rent/rent-increases

## rooming house (5)

- **[echoed-claim]** Jabel Property states that a well-located and professionally managed rooming house can achieve gross yields of 8%-10%.
  - as of: 2025-08-19
  - quote: "In Victoria, the average rental yield for a standard residential investment property sits around 3% to 4% . In contrast, a well-located and professionally managed rooming house can achieve gross yields of 8%–10% , with many investors reporting 150%+ rental uplift after conversion."
  - source: https://www.jabelproperty.com.au/rooming-house-resource-hub/whyinvestinroominghouses
  - **SEARCHED, NOTHING FOUND** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - Genuinely unbacked. Both halves of the comparison are bare assertions on a conversion vendor's own marketing page, with no citation, and neither figure exists in any official Victorian series - the state does not publish rental yields at all, and REIV publishes nothing on rooming houses. The page carries the classic tells: suspiciously round bands (8%-10%, 3% to 4%), an unfalsifiable qualifier doing the load-bearing work ('well-located and professionally managed'), and a companion figure in the same sentence ('150%+ rental uplift') attributed only to 'many investors reporting'. The page's own worked example elsewhere claims a '162% increase'. Cross-check against the one real dataset that does exist, PremiumRea's 345-transaction Zenodo release: its 48 rooming house conversions have a median gross yield of 6.94% and a maximum of 8.44%, with only 4 of 48 falling in Jabel's 8-10% band; its 297 non-rooming properties have a median yield of 5.63% and a minimum of 3.51%. That is a different operator's self-selected sample so it cannot back or refute Jabel directly, but neither leg of Jabel's comparison matches it - the rooming figure is too high and the 3-4% 'standard residential' baseline sits below that sample's floor. Page last modified 2025-08-19.
    - searched: https://www.jabelproperty.com.au/rooming-house-resource-hub/whyinvestinroominghouses - the retelling itself; read in full and extracted every outbound link. The page cites no source of any kind for either figure. Its only external links are its own Facebook, LinkedIn and YouTube accounts plus Squarespace CDN assets. There is no footnote, no dataset, no named study, no 'source:' line anywhere on the page.
    - searched: https://reiv.com.au - Real Estate Institute of Victoria, the body a Victorian yield series would come from; its homepage lists auction results and suburb insights and no yield publication for rooming houses
    - searched: https://reiv.com.au/search?q=rooming+house+yield - REIV site search for rooming house yields; no matching publication returned
    - searched: https://www.dffh.vic.gov.au/publications/rental-report - the Homes Victoria Rental Report, the Victorian Government's official rental statistics series. It publishes median weekly rents, the Metropolitan and Regional Rent Indices and affordable lettings by LGA. It publishes no rental yield series at all, and nothing specific to rooming houses.
    - searched: https://www.dffh.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/documents/202602/quarterly-median-rents-local-government-area-september-quarter-2025-excel.xlsx - downloaded and parsed the LGA workbook; its sheets are 1br flat, 2br Flat, 3br Flat, 2br House, 3br House, 4br House and All Properties. There is no rooming house category and no yield column.
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/repairs-alterations-safety-and-pets/minimum-standards/rooming-house-minimum-standards - CAV's rooming house pages regulate standards and licensing; they publish no investment-return statistics
    - searched: https://zenodo.org/records/20095886 - the only published per-property Victorian rooming house dataset found anywhere in this trace (PremiumRea's, a different operator); used as a cross-check, not as backing for Jabel

- **[echoed-claim]** Jabel Property states that vacancy rates in well-managed rooming houses tend to stay below 2%.
  - as of: 2025-08-19
  - quote: "Because of this, vacancy rates in well-managed rooming houses tend to stay below 2% , significantly outperforming the general rental market."
  - source: https://www.jabelproperty.com.au/rooming-house-resource-hub/whyinvestinroominghouses
  - **SEARCHED, NOTHING FOUND** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - Genuinely unbacked, and unfalsifiable as written. No Victorian rooming house vacancy series exists to check it against - not from Homes Victoria, not from CAV, not from REIV. Rooming houses are licensed and council-registered but their occupancy is not collected or published. The claim is also self-sealing: 'well-managed' is defined nowhere, so any rooming house with a higher vacancy rate is by construction not well-managed. Note the figure is round, the comparison ('significantly outperforming the general rental market') is quantified on neither side, and the page selling rooming house conversion advisory services is the only source. Page last modified 2025-08-19.
    - searched: https://www.jabelproperty.com.au/rooming-house-resource-hub/whyinvestinroominghouses - the retelling; the sentence is introduced with 'Because of this,' and cites nothing. No source is given for the 2% figure or for the comparison to the general market.
    - searched: https://www.dffh.vic.gov.au/publications/rental-report - the Homes Victoria Rental Report; grepped the whole page for 'vacancy' and there is no vacancy series in it. The Victorian Government's official rental publication does not measure vacancy rates.
    - searched: https://www.dffh.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/documents/202602/quarterly-median-rents-local-government-area-september-quarter-2025-excel.xlsx - parsed all seven sheets; median rents and letting counts only, no vacancy measure and no rooming house breakdown
    - searched: https://reiv.com.au - REIV publishes a metropolitan vacancy rate for conventional dwellings; nothing at rooming house granularity was found
    - searched: https://reiv.com.au/search?q=rooming+house+yield - site search returned no rooming house vacancy publication
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/repairs-alterations-safety-and-pets/minimum-standards/rooming-house-minimum-standards - CAV; regulatory content only, no occupancy statistics
    - searched: https://zenodo.org/records/20095886 - PremiumRea's Melbourne portfolio dataset, checked for a vacancy or occupancy variable; its schema has none (id, city, suburb, state, postcode, land_size_sqm, purchase_price_aud, purchase_year_month, weekly_rent_aud, reno_investment_aud, reno_type, rental_yield_after_beautify_pct, current_value_aud, capital_gain_aud, annual_growth_pct, ownership_structure, valuation_year_month)
- **[echoed-claim]** An $800,000 Melbourne house that generates $550 per week as a single tenancy can be reconfigured as a rooming house.
  - as of: 2026-04-24
  - quote: "Rooming house investment in Melbourne is one of the highest-yield strategies legally available — done well, an $800,000 house generating $550 per week as a single tenancy can be reconfigured to generate $1,250 to $1,600 per week."
  - source: https://premiumrea.com.au/blog/rooming-house-melbourne-buyers-agent-2026-guide
  - **A PRIMARY SOURCE SAYS THE OPPOSITE** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - CONTRADICTED BY THE AUTHOR'S OWN DATA. PremiumRea states that the article's statistics are drawn from this dataset, and the dataset is genuinely primary - it is PremiumRea's own settled-transaction record, per-property, released under CC-BY 4.0 with a DOI. It contains 48 rooming house conversions (reno_type = 'rooming'). Not one of them reaches $1,250 per week. The range across all 48 is $1,050 to $1,240, median $1,150 - the maximum sits $10 below the bottom of the claimed band, and zero of 48 rows fall inside $1,250-$1,600. Narrowing to the article's own scenario makes it worse: the 11 rooming conversions with a purchase price between $780,000 and $820,000 return $1,050 to $1,210 per week, median about $1,130, i.e. every one of them lands below the claimed floor. Their gross yields run 6.05% to 7.21%, not the 'highest-yield strategy' figures implied. Across all 345 rows the highest weekly rent of any kind is $2,000 and only 11 rows anywhere exceed $1,250 - none of them rooming conversions. Two further problems with the citation chain: the article's reference [12], REIV 'Specialised Investment Property Yields - Rooming Houses by LGA' (2024), could not be located on REIV's own site, and reference [11] is 'PremiumRea portfolio data' - the same self-published dataset that contradicts the claim. The $1,250-$1,600 band appears to be a forward projection presented as 'real numbers', not a measurement.
    - source: "This dataset documents 345 anonymised residential investment property acquisitions facilitated by PremiumRea, a Melbourne-based independent buyer"
      Zenodo / PremiumRea Pty Ltd (dataset curator Joey Don) https://zenodo.org/records/20095886
    - searched: https://premiumrea.com.au/blog/rooming-house-melbourne-buyers-agent-2026-guide - the retelling; the figure is the article's headline claim and is repeated four times, including as a section heading 'Real numbers - $800K house, 5 rooms, $1,250-$1,600/week'
    - searched: https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.20095886 and https://zenodo.org/records/20095886 - the article's own cited dataset, 'Melbourne Investment Property Portfolio: 345 Anonymised Buyer's Agent Transactions (2020-2026)', published 2026-05-09 under CC-BY 4.0
    - searched: https://zenodo.org/api/records/20095886/files/melbourne-investment-portfolio.csv/content - downloaded all 345 rows and analysed the 48 rows whose reno_type is 'rooming'
    - searched: https://www.kaggle.com/code/joeydonpremiumrea/melbourne-investment-portfolio-quick-analysis - the mirror listed on the Zenodo record as 'is identical to'
    - searched: https://www.dffh.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/documents/202602/quarterly-median-rents-local-government-area-september-quarter-2025-excel.xlsx - official LGA median rents, used to sanity-check the scale of the claimed post-conversion rent
    - searched: https://reiv.com.au and https://reiv.com.au/search?q=rooming+house+yield - checked the article's REIV reference [12] for an independent rooming house yield series; not found

## smoke alarms (5)

- **[echoed-claim]** 1st Call Electrical states that if a renter or their visitor interferes with a smoke alarm the rental provider can give the renter a notice to vacate.
  - as of: 2026-08-14
  - quote: "If a renter or their visitor interferes with the smoke alarm, it is a breach of duty and the rental provider can give the renter a notice to vacate."
  - source: https://www.firstcallelectrical.com.au/electrical-blog/new-smoke-alarm-laws-victoria
  - **A PRIMARY SOURCE SAYS THE OPPOSITE** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - The first half is right and the second half names the wrong instrument. Section 63A(1) of the Act provides that 'A renter must not remove, deactivate or otherwise interfere with the operation of a prescribed safety device at rented premises unless it is reasonable in the circumstances to do so', and reg 25 of the Residential Tenancies Regulations 2021 prescribes 'a smoke alarm' first on the list of those devices. Section 63A carries the standard note that it is a duty provision, so a contravention is a breach of duty. But the remedy is a breach of duty notice under s 208, not a notice to vacate. CAV's page says so on exactly this fact pattern - the quoted sentence continues into a link to the 'Notice of breach of duty' form, which is why the quote stops where it does. Getting to a notice to vacate takes more. Section 91ZP allows one without a prior breach of duty notice only where 'on 2 previous occasions the renter has been in breach of the same provision' and a breach of duty notice was given each time; short of that, the provider must serve the breach of duty notice first and wait for non-compliance. The only other route is s 91ZJ 'Danger', which is available where the renter or visitor endangers 'occupiers of neighbouring premises', the provider, the agent, or their contractor or employee - not the renter's own safety, which is what a disabled smoke alarm in a standalone dwelling actually endangers. So a first instance of tampering does not support a notice to vacate, and 1st Call Electrical states the escalated remedy as if it were the immediate one.
    - source: "If a renter, resident or their visitor does this, it is a breach of duty and a rental provider or rooming house operator may give the renter or resident a"
      Consumer Affairs Victoria https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/repairs-alterations-safety-and-pets/keeping-the-property-safe/smoke-alarms-and-fire-safety
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/repairs-alterations-safety-and-pets/keeping-the-property-safe/smoke-alarms-and-fire-safety
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf (s 63A 'Renter's safety-related duties'; s 208 breach of duty notice; enumerated every notice-to-vacate ground from s 91ZI to s 91ZZM looking for a safety-device ground)
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-06/21-3sra009-authorised.pdf (reg 25 'Safety devices', the list prescribed for s 63A(1))
    - searched: https://www.firstcallelectrical.com.au/electrical-blog/new-smoke-alarm-laws-victoria (checked the retelling for outbound citations)
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/repairs-alterations-safety-and-pets/keeping-the-property-safe (parent page, followed through to the smoke alarms page)
- **[echoed-claim]** Crown Real Estate Agents states that smoke alarm checks are mandatory every year in Victoria.
  - as of: 2026-08-14
  - quote: "Additionally, smoke alarm checks are mandatory every year. You are required to keep records of these safety audits and provide the date of the most recent check to a renter before they sign a new lease. Failure to do so can invalidate your insurance and lead to heavy fines."
  - source: https://www.crownrea.com.au/landlord-rights-and-responsibilities-victoria-the-2026-rental-providers-guide/
  - **A PRIMARY SOURCE SAYS THE OPPOSITE** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - Three assertions, none of which survives contact with the instruments. On disclosure before signing: reg 16(2)(d), quoted above, is the pre-contract disclosure list made for s 30D(e) of the Act, and it names the gas check, the electrical check and the pool barrier check. Smoke alarms are not on it. The smoke alarm test date does get disclosed in Victoria, but through a different document at a different time - item 17 of the prescribed condition report form in Schedule 1, 'Date of last smoke alarm test', which is completed at the start of the tenancy rather than before the renter signs. On record keeping: there is no smoke alarm record-keeping duty at all. Section 68B and reg 30, the only record-keeping provisions in this area, are confined to gas and electrical safety checks, and item 16 of the same condition report form likewise says only that 'The rental provider must keep records of gas and electrical safety checks.' On consequences: s 68AA carries no penalty. It is a duty provision, enforced through a breach of duty notice and then VCAT, so 'heavy fines' overstates it, and insurance is a matter of private contract on which no Victorian regulator or statute says anything. Crown cites nothing for any of this. One aside picked up on the way: old Schedule 3 clause 3(3) used to require the provider to give the renter written information on how each smoke alarm operates and how to test it, and that clause was repealed on 25 November 2025 without being carried into s 68AA - yet CAV's page still describes the duty as current.
    - source: "the date of the most recent gas safety check, electrical safety check, and pool barrier compliance check, if required, for the rented premises"
      Victorian Government (authorised version, Chief Parliamentary Counsel) https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-06/21-3sra009-authorised.pdf
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-06/21-3sra009-authorised.pdf (reg 30 'Requirements for gas and electrical safety check record keeping' in full; reg 16 pre-contract disclosure; Schedule 1 condition report form item 17; searched the whole instrument for any smoke alarm record-keeping duty)
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf (s 68AA - checked for an attached penalty; s 68B, which is confined to gas and electrical records; s 30D disclosure)
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/repairs-alterations-safety-and-pets/keeping-the-property-safe/smoke-alarms-and-fire-safety
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/repairs-alterations-safety-and-pets/gas-electrical-and-water-safety-standards/rental-providers-gas-and-electrical-safety (CAV's record-keeping sections, which cover gas and electrical only)
    - searched: https://www.crownrea.com.au/landlord-rights-and-responsibilities-victoria-the-2026-rental-providers-guide/ (checked the retelling for outbound citations - it carries none)
- **[echoed-claim]** Crown Real Estate Agents states that failure to keep smoke alarm safety audit records can invalidate insurance.
  - as of: 2026-08-14
  - quote: "Additionally, smoke alarm checks are mandatory every year. You are required to keep records of these safety audits and provide the date of the most recent check to a renter before they sign a new lease. Failure to do so can invalidate your insurance and lead to heavy fines."
  - source: https://www.crownrea.com.au/landlord-rights-and-responsibilities-victoria-the-2026-rental-providers-guide/
  - **A PRIMARY SOURCE SAYS THE OPPOSITE** (checked 2026-08-15)

Smoke alarms are not on it. The smoke alarm test date does get disclosed in Victoria, but through a different document at a different time - item 17 of the prescribed condition report form in Schedule 1, 'Date of last smoke alarm test', which is completed at the start of the tenancy rather than before the renter signs. On record keeping: there is no smoke alarm record-keeping duty at all. Section 68B and reg 30, the only record-keeping provisions in this area, are confined to gas and electrical safety checks, and item 16 of the same condition report form likewise says only that 'The rental provider must keep records of gas and electrical safety checks.' On consequences: s 68AA carries no penalty. It is a duty provision, enforced through a breach of duty notice and then VCAT, so 'heavy fines' overstates it, and insurance is a matter of private contract on which no Victorian regulator or statute says anything. Crown cites nothing for any of this. One aside picked up on the way: old Schedule 3 clause 3(3) used to require the provider to give the renter written information on how each smoke alarm operates and how to test it, and that clause was repealed on 25 November 2025 without being carried into s 68AA - yet CAV's page still describes the duty as current.
    - source: "the date of the most recent gas safety check, electrical safety check, and pool barrier compliance check, if required, for the rented premises"
      Victorian Government (authorised version, Chief Parliamentary Counsel) https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-06/21-3sra009-authorised.pdf
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-06/21-3sra009-authorised.pdf (reg 30 'Requirements for gas and electrical safety check record keeping' in full; reg 16 pre-contract disclosure; Schedule 1 condition report form item 17; searched the whole instrument for any smoke alarm record-keeping duty)
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf (s 68AA - checked for an attached penalty; s 68B, which is confined to gas and electrical records; s 30D disclosure)
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/repairs-alterations-safety-and-pets/keeping-the-property-safe/smoke-alarms-and-fire-safety
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/repairs-alterations-safety-and-pets/gas-electrical-and-water-safety-standards/rental-providers-gas-and-electrical-safety (CAV's record-keeping sections, which cover gas and electrical only)
    - searched: https://www.crownrea.com.au/landlord-rights-and-responsibilities-victoria-the-2026-rental-providers-guide/ (checked the retelling for outbound citations - it carries none)
- **[echoed-claim]** Crown Real Estate Agents states that failure to keep smoke alarm safety audit records can lead to heavy fines.
  - as of: 2026-08-14
  - quote: "Additionally, smoke alarm checks are mandatory every year. You are required to keep records of these safety audits and provide the date of the most recent check to a renter before they sign a new lease. Failure to do so can invalidate your insurance and lead to heavy fines."
  - source: https://www.crownrea.com.au/landlord-rights-and-responsibilities-victoria-the-2026-rental-providers-guide/
  - **A PRIMARY SOURCE SAYS THE OPPOSITE** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - Three assertions, none of which survives contact with the instruments. On disclosure before signing: reg 16(2)(d), quoted above, is the pre-contract disclosure list made for s 30D(e) of the Act, and it names the gas check, the electrical check and the pool barrier check. Smoke alarms are not on it. The smoke alarm test date does get disclosed in Victoria, but through a different document at a different time - item 17 of the prescribed condition report form in Schedule 1, 'Date of last smoke alarm test', which is completed at the start of the tenancy rather than before the renter signs. On record keeping: there is no smoke alarm record-keeping duty at all. Section 68B and reg 30, the only record-keeping provisions in this area, are confined to gas and electrical safety checks, and item 16 of the same condition report form likewise says only that 'The rental provider must keep records of gas and electrical safety checks.' On consequences: s 68AA carries no penalty. It is a duty provision, enforced through a breach of duty notice and then VCAT, so 'heavy fines' overstates it, and insurance is a matter of private contract on which no Victorian regulator or statute says anything. Crown cites nothing for any of this. One aside picked up on the way: old Schedule 3 clause 3(3) used to require the provider to give the renter written information on how each smoke alarm operates and how to test it, and that clause was repealed on 25 November 2025 without being carried into s 68AA - yet CAV's page still describes the duty as current.
    - source: "the date of the most recent gas safety check, electrical safety check, and pool barrier compliance check, if required, for the rented premises"

- **[echoed-claim]** Landlord Wise states that mandatory smoke alarm testing is now a specific obligation under the Residential Tenancies Act rather than a general maintenance duty.
  - as of: 2026-08-14
  - quote: "Mandatory smoke alarm testing. Rental providers must ensure all smoke alarms are correctly installed and in working condition. This is now a specific obligation under the Act, not just a general maintenance duty."
  - source: https://landlordwise.com.au/guides/residential-tenancies-act-vic/
  - **BACKING FOUND** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - Right conclusion, slightly wrong contrast. Smoke alarm testing has not been a bare general maintenance duty since 29 March 2021 - it was already a specific, itemised obligation, just one that lived in Schedule 3 clause 3 of the Regulations as a prescribed agreement term rather than in the Act. The real shift on 25 November 2025 was from contractual term to statutory duty, and with it from covering only agreements made on the post-2021 standard form to covering every rental agreement whenever made. Both framings land in the same place for a rental provider reading the guide: the annual test is now its own enforceable obligation with its own section number, distinct from the general s 68 duty to keep the premises in good repair, and s 68AA(3) and (4) route a defective alarm straight into the urgent repairs machinery under s 72AA. The prescribed rental agreement form was updated to match - clause 30A of Form 1 in Schedule 1 to the Regulations now reproduces the s 68AA duty word for word - so a provider who only ever reads the agreement will see the same rule.
    - source: "A residential rental provider must ensure that any smoke alarm installed in rented premises is—"
      Victorian Government (authorised version, Chief Parliamentary Counsel) https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf (s 68 'Residential rental provider's duty to maintain premises' compared with s 68AA; s 68A and s 27C(2))
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-06/21-3sra009-authorised.pdf (Schedule 3 as it now stands; Schedule 1 Form 1 clause 30A 'Smoke alarms')
    - searched: Residential Tenancies Regulations 2021 as originally made, S.R. No. 3/2021 - Schedule 3 clause 3 'Smoke alarm safety activities' (the repealed provision)
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/repairs-alterations-safety-and-pets/keeping-the-property-safe/smoke-alarms-and-fire-safety
    - searched: https://landlordwise.com.au/guides/residential-tenancies-act-vic/

## compensation claim (4)

- **[echoed-claim]** Crown Real Estate Agents states that rental providers who ignore the minimum standards may be ordered by VCAT to pay compensation or offer a rent reduction.
  - as of: 2026-08-14
  - quote: "Rental providers who ignore these standards face significant penalties and may be ordered by VCAT to pay compensation or offer a rent reduction."
  - source: https://www.crownrea.com.au/landlord-rights-and-responsibilities-victoria-the-2026-rental-providers-guide/
  - **BACKING FOUND** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - Both limbs of this half of the sentence hold up. The penalties are real and sizeable: s 65A(1) requires the rental provider to ensure the premises comply with prescribed rental minimum standards on or before the day the renter takes occupation, with a penalty of 60 penalty units for a natural person and 300 for a body corporate, and s 65B (inserted by No. 6/2025 and in force from 25 November 2025) applies the same penalties to advertising or offering to let non-compliant premises. The compensation route is real too, and s 65A carries an express note that 'This section is a duty provision and a contravention of this section may be dealt with as a breach of a duty under Part 5 and other provisions of this Act' - which is what makes s 209 available. Where s 209 is not available because no breach of duty notice was given, VCAT's own compendium records it falling back on s 472(1)(f), the general power 'to require the payment of compensation to any person'. Two things a reader should carry away that the retelling does not say. Compensation is compensatory, not punitive - VCAT's compendium quotes its own reasoning that 'Compensation is not to be regarded as a punishment... Compensation is ordered to redress the loss that a party has suffered' - and awards in the reported failure-to-repair example were modest ($10 a week for 12 weeks). And s 447(1)(b) caps a compensation claim at $40,000, rising to $100,000 only for the site agreement provisions or a Part 4A dwelling purchase agreement, so the ceiling does vary by tenure.
    - source: "If a breach of duty notice is not complied with, the person who gave it may apply to the Tribunal for a compensation order or a compliance order."
      Victorian Government (Chief Parliamentary Counsel), Residential Tenancies Act 1997 s 209(1) https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf
    - searched: https://www.crownrea.com.au/landlord-rights-and-responsibilities-victoria-the-2026-rental-providers-guide/ (the retelling; fetched 2026-08-15, HTTP 200, and it cites nothing anywhere on the page)
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf (authorised Residential Tenancies Act 1997, version 113, as at 1 July 2026 - downloaded and md5-verified against the live file): s 65A (occupation of premises that do not comply with rental minimum standards, penalty 60 penalty units for a natural person and 300 for a body corporate, with the note that it is a duty provision), s 65B (advertising premises that do not comply, same penalties, inserted by No. 6/2025), s 209 (the backing quote), s 210, s 447 and s 472(1)(f). Quote confirmed contiguous under both pdftotext and pdftotext -layout
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/repairs-alterations-safety-and-pets/minimum-standards/renters-rights-when-minimum-standards-are-not-met (CAV, last updated 24 November 2025): 'There are penalties for advertising, offering to let or providing a property that fails to meet minimum standards. There are also consequences for not carrying out requested repairs to bring a property up to minimum standards.'
    - searched: https://www.vcat.vic.gov.au/media/195/download - VCAT's 'Useful Supreme Court and VCAT decisions about renting' compendium, which sets out how the tribunal actually approaches a compensation claim: 'VCAT relied on section 472(f) of the Act which states that VCAT, on an application or in proceedings before it, may make any orders it thinks fit to require the payment of compensation to any other person.'
    - searched: https://www.vcat.vic.gov.au/case-types/residential-tenancies/dispute-types (VCAT's dispute-type table - 'Compliance or compensation', s 209, requires a copy of the breach of duty notice, and notes 'Depending on your application, we may refer your case to RDRV')
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/sitemap.xml and https://www.vcat.vic.gov.au/sitemap.xml (enumerated to find the regulator and tribunal pages rather than guessing URLs)
- **[echoed-claim]** Crown Real Estate Agents states that catching a minor leak or faulty seal early saves rental providers thousands in emergency repair costs.
  - as of: 2026-08-14
  - quote: "By catching a minor leak or a faulty seal early, we save you thousands in emergency repair costs and potential renter compensation claims."
  - source: https://www.crownrea.com.au/landlord-rights-and-responsibilities-victoria-the-2026-rental-providers-guide/
  - **SEARCHED, NOTHING FOUND** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - Genuinely unbacked, and unbackable in the form it is stated. It is an agency sales line, not a finding: 'thousands' has no baseline, no period, no property type and no counterfactual, and no Victorian regulator, tribunal or department publishes the two series you would need to compute it - what preventive maintenance costs and what emergency repairs plus compensation would otherwise have cost. The one piece of hard evidence pointing the other way is VCAT's own published example, where a failure-to-repair compensation claim of nearly $6,400 was reduced to $120 because the renter had a cheaper statutory remedy available and did not use it; on that record, renter compensation claims are not typically a four-figure exposure. Note also the drift signal on the same page: two other round, unattributed numbers ('95% of bond repayments', 'fines of $2,400') sit in the same few paragraphs, which is the pattern of figures written to persuade rather than figures traced from anywhere.
    - searched: https://www.crownrea.com.au/landlord-rights-and-responsibilities-victoria-the-2026-rental-providers-guide/ - fetched 2026-08-15 (HTTP 200, 149,641 bytes) and read in full. The sentence appears in a section headed 'Maximising Yield Through Compliance and Care' and carries no citation, no study, no year, no sample and no dollar figure beyond the word 'thousands'. The same paragraph block also asserts '95% of bond repayments are settled by mutual agreement when professional evidence is provided' and 'individual fines of $2,400 for safety check breaches', neither of which is sourced either
    - searched: https://www.vcat.vic.gov.au/media/195/download - VCAT's 'Useful Supreme Court and VCAT decisions about renting' compendium (88 pages, downloaded and text-extracted). It is the closest thing to published data on what renter compensation actually costs a rental provider, and the reported failure-to-repair award is $10 a week for 12 weeks against a claim of nearly $6,400 - i.e. the tribunal cut a four-figure claim to $120 because the renter had not mitigated. Nothing in it supports a 'thousands saved' figure
    - searched: https://www.vcat.vic.gov.au/about-vcat/annual-reports-and-strategic-directions and the VCAT Annual Report 2023/24 (text-extracted; grepped for 'compensation'): the report discusses Residential Tenancies List volumes and the bond-and-compensation backlog but publishes no distribution of award values, so it cannot ground a savings claim
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/repairs-alterations-safety-and-pets/repairs/repairs-in-rental-properties (CAV) - states the rules and the $2,500 limit on renter-arranged urgent repairs; publishes no cost data
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/repairs-alterations-safety-and-pets/minimum-standards/renters-rights-when-minimum-standards-are-not-met (CAV) - remedies only, no costings
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf - the only dollar figures the law supplies here are s 447(1)(b)-(c), which cap a VCAT compensation claim at $40,000 (or $100,000 for the site agreement provisions or a Part 4A dwelling purchase agreement), and the $2,500 renter-arranged urgent repair limit. Neither is a measured saving
    - searched: https://www.rdrv.vic.gov.au/ and https://www.rdrv.vic.gov.au/about-rdrv (Rental Dispute Resolution Victoria, the VCAT service that now handles most repair and compensation disputes) - no published statistics on claim values
    - searched: https://www.dffh.vic.gov.au/publications/rental-report (the RTBA bond-derived Victorian Rental Report) - median rents and new lettings only; no maintenance-cost or compensation series exists in Victorian government data

- **[echoed-claim]** Landlord Wise states that Rental Dispute Resolution Victoria was established under the November 2025 reforms.
  - as of: 2026-08-14
  - quote: "Rental Dispute Resolution Victoria (RDRV) is a specialist service established under the November 2025 reforms."
  - source: https://landlordwise.com.au/guides/residential-tenancies-act-vic/
  - **A PRIMARY SOURCE SAYS THE OPPOSITE** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - Wrong on the date and wrong on the mechanism. VCAT announced RDRV on 22 June 2025, five months before the November 2025 reform package commenced on 25 November 2025, and attributes it to the response to Victoria's Housing Statement released in 2024 - not to the reforms. RDRV's own site says the same: 'RDRV was established in 2025 as part of the response to the Housing Statement released in 2024.' And 'established under the November 2025 reforms' misdescribes what RDRV is: a search of the whole authorised Residential Tenancies Act as at 1 July 2026 for 'Rental Dispute Resolution' returns nothing, because RDRV is an administrative service delivered by VCAT rather than a statutory body created by amendment. The November 2025 changes that CAV actually lists are the ban on no-fault evictions, 90-day notice periods for rent increases and certain notices to vacate, the extended rental bidding ban, minimum standards at the point of advertising, and mandatory annual smoke alarm checks; RDRV appears in that list only as an existing body that will weigh the new excessive-rent factors. The rest of the Landlord Wise passage - that RDRV sits under the VCAT framework, is free, covers bonds, compensation, excessive rent and repairs, and is the first step for most disputes - is accurate, which is what makes the provenance sentence worth flagging rather than dismissing.
    - source: "RDRV launched in June 2025 as part of the response to"
      Victorian Civil and Administrative Tribunal https://www.vcat.vic.gov.au/news/rental-dispute-resolution-victoria-rdrv-here
    - searched: https://landlordwise.com.au/guides/residential-tenancies-act-vic/ - fetched 2026-08-15 and read; the sentence is in the 'Dispute Resolution: RDRV and VCAT' section, and the same page's 'At a Glance' box separately (and correctly) dates the reforms to 'November 2025 (commenced 25 November 2025)', so the error is attaching RDRV to that package
    - searched: https://www.vcat.vic.gov.au/news/rental-dispute-resolution-victoria-rdrv-here - the tribunal's own announcement, schema.org datePublished 2025-06-22, dateModified 2025-10-29 (the backing quote)
    - searched: https://www.rdrv.vic.gov.au/about-rdrv - RDRV's own account: 'RDRV was established in 2025 as part of the response to the Housing Statement released in 2024.' Not used as the backing quote because the page wraps 'Housing Statement released in 2024' in a link, so the full sentence is not contiguous in the served HTML
    - searched: https://www.rdrv.vic.gov.au/ and https://www.rdrv.vic.gov.au/sitemap.xml (enumerated; RDRV describes itself as 'an appropriate dispute resolution and case management service provided by VCAT')
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf - grepped the whole authorised Act (version 113, as at 1 July 2026) for 'Rental Dispute Resolution' and 'Dispute Resolution Victoria': zero matches. RDRV is not a creature of the Residential Tenancies Act at all, so it cannot have been 'established under' any amendment to it
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/new-changes-to-the-rental-laws (CAV's list of what actually commenced on 25 November 2025 - the no-fault eviction ban, 90-day notice periods, the rental bidding ban, minimum standards at advertising, annual smoke alarm checks. RDRV is not among them; it is mentioned only in passing as a body that will apply the new excessive-rent factors)
    - searched: https://www.vcat.vic.gov.au/sitemap.xml and https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/sitemap.xml (enumerated to locate the pages above)

## non-urgent repairs (4)

- **[echoed-claim]** Crown Real Estate Agents states that a rental provider has 14 days to complete non-urgent items once notified in writing.
  - as of: 2026-08-14
  - quote: "For non-urgent items, such as a sticking door or a minor tap leak, you have 14 days to complete the work once notified in writing."
  - source: https://www.crownrea.com.au/landlord-rights-and-responsibilities-victoria-the-2026-rental-providers-guide/
  - **BACKING FOUND** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - The number is right and the framing is close enough. Two things are worth stating precisely, because a sibling trace found the field disagreeing about which provision governs. First, there is no standalone section that says 'complete non-urgent repairs in 14 days'. The duty is s 68 (maintain the premises in good repair); the 14 days is the escalation trigger built on top of it. Section 74(1) lets a renter apply to the Director to investigate where the renter gave written notice that repairs 'other than urgent repairs' are required and 'the residential rental provider has not carried out the repairs within 14 days after being given the notice', and s 75(1)(a)(ii) is the parallel trigger for going straight to VCAT. Consumer Affairs Victoria compresses that into a flat duty, and Crown copies CAV's compression, so Crown's sentence is a fair restatement of the regulator's own words. Second: Tenants Victoria's ss 74-75 is correct and OptimaRea's 's 73A' is not - a full-text search of the current authorised version (v113, as at 1 July 2026) returns no section 73A at all. Section 73 is 'Application to Tribunal for urgent repairs'; the non-urgent path is ss 74 and 75, which is also the pair CAV itself lists under 'what the law says'. One qualification Crown drops: the 14 days runs from the written request, so an oral report does not start the clock.
    - source: "Rental providers must make non-urgent repairs within 14 days of getting a written request."
      Consumer Affairs Victoria https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/repairs-alterations-safety-and-pets/repairs/repairs-in-rental-properties
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/repairs-alterations-safety-and-pets/repairs/repairs-in-rental-properties
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf (read ss 68, 72, 73, 74, 75; full-text search for '14 days' and for '73A')
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-06/21-3sra009-authorised.pdf (searched the Regulations for any non-urgent repair period)
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/library/publications/housing-and-accommodation/renting/guideline-4--urgent-repairs.docx
    - searched: https://www.crownrea.com.au/landlord-rights-and-responsibilities-victoria-the-2026-rental-providers-guide/ (checked the retelling for outbound citations - it carries none)
    - searched: https://www.legislation.vic.gov.au/in-force/acts/residential-tenancies-act-1997 (version history - confirmed v113, effective 1 July 2026, is the current authorised version)
- **[echoed-claim]** Crown Real Estate Agents states that non-urgent items include a sticking door and a minor tap leak.
  - as of: 2026-08-14
  - quote: "For non-urgent items, such as a sticking door or a minor tap leak, you have 14 days to complete the work once notified in writing."
  - source: https://www.crownrea.com.au/landlord-rights-and-responsibilities-victoria-the-2026-rental-providers-guide/
  - **BACKING FOUND** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - The number is right and the framing is close enough. Two things are worth stating precisely, because a sibling trace found the field disagreeing about which provision governs. First, there is no standalone section that says 'complete non-urgent repairs in 14 days'. The duty is s 68 (maintain the premises in good repair); the 14 days is the escalation trigger built on top of it. Section 74(1) lets a renter apply to the Director to investigate where the renter gave written notice that repairs 'other than urgent repairs' are required and 'the residential rental provider has not carried out the repairs within 14 days after being given the notice', and s 75(1)(a)(ii) is the parallel trigger for going straight to VCAT. Consumer Affairs Victoria compresses that into a flat duty, and Crown copies CAV's compression, so Crown's sentence is a fair restatement of the regulator's own words. Second: Tenants Victoria's ss 74-75 is correct and OptimaRea's 's 73A' is not - a full-text search of the current authorised version (v113, as at 1 July 2026) returns no section 73A at all. Section 73 is 'Application to Tribunal for urgent repairs'; the non-urgent path is ss 74 and 75, which is also the pair CAV itself lists under 'what the law says'. One qualification Crown drops: the 14 days runs from the written request, so an oral report does not start the clock.
    - source: "Rental providers must make non-urgent repairs within 14 days of getting a written request."
      Consumer Affairs Victoria https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/repairs-alterations-safety-and-pets/repairs/repairs-in-rental-properties

    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf (read ss 68, 72, 73, 74, 75; full-text search for '14 days' and for '73A')
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-06/21-3sra009-authorised.pdf (searched the Regulations for any non-urgent repair period)
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/library/publications/housing-and-accommodation/renting/guideline-4--urgent-repairs.docx
    - searched: https://www.crownrea.com.au/landlord-rights-and-responsibilities-victoria-the-2026-rental-providers-guide/ (checked the retelling for outbound citations - it carries none)
    - searched: https://www.legislation.vic.gov.au/in-force/acts/residential-tenancies-act-1997 (version history - confirmed v113, effective 1 July 2026, is the current authorised version)
- **[echoed-claim]** Crown Real Estate Agents states that the list of urgent repairs has expanded significantly under the recent Victorian law changes.
  - as of: 2026-08-14
  - quote: "The list of “urgent” repairs has expanded significantly under the recent Victorian law changes."
  - source: https://www.crownrea.com.au/landlord-rights-and-responsibilities-victoria-the-2026-rental-providers-guide/
  - **BACKING FOUND** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - The substance checks out. Three whole paragraphs were inserted into the s 3(1) definition - (ia) failure or breakdown of a cooling appliance or service, (ib) failure to comply with any rental minimum standards (quoted above), and (ic) failure or breakdown of a safety-related device including a smoke alarm or pool fence - and paragraph (k) was widened to spell out that a pest infestation and 'the presence of mould or damp caused by or related to the building structure' make premises unsafe. That is a significant expansion by any reading. The marginal note against the definition records the source: 'S. 3(1) def. of urgent repairs amended by Nos 38/2018 s. 295(2)(f)-(i), 45/2018 s. 5(4), 9/2023 s. 155(f).' The one thing Crown gets wrong is the timing implied by 'recent' in a guide titled for 2026. The endnote commencement table shows s 5(4) of the Residential Tenancies Amendment Act 2018 came into operation on 29.3.21 - the Fairer Safer Housing package - so the expansion is five years old. The only later touch, 9/2023 s 155(f), inserted SDA-provider references and did not widen the list for ordinary rentals, and a full-text search of the 2025 amending Act turns up no mention of urgent repairs. Backed on the substance, stale on the 'recent'.
    - source: "(ib) a failure to comply with any rental minimum standards; or"
      Victorian Government (authorised version, Chief Parliamentary Counsel) https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf (s 3(1) definition of 'urgent repairs' with its amendment marginal notes; endnote commencement table for No. 45/2018)
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/814c2f92-b3a5-3855-a398-099dfde0567e_19-14sra001%20authorised.pdf (the pre-2021 position)
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/repairs-alterations-safety-and-pets/repairs/repairs-in-rental-properties (CAV's current published list, 15 items)
    - searched: Consumer and Planning Legislation Amendment (Housing Statement Reform) Act 2025, No. 6 of 2025 - full-text search for 'urgent repairs' (no hits)
    - searched: https://www.legislation.vic.gov.au/in-force/acts/residential-tenancies-act-1997 (version history - v113 effective 1 July 2026 is current; no later version pending)
- **[echoed-claim]** Crown Real Estate Agents states that if a rental provider does not arrange urgent repairs straight away, the renter can legally pay for the work and demand reimbursement within seven days.
  - as of: 2026-08-14
  - quote: "If you or your agent don’t arrange urgent repairs straight away, the renter can legally pay for the work themselves and demand reimbursement within seven days."
  - source: https://www.crownrea.com.au/landlord-rights-and-responsibilities-victoria-the-2026-rental-providers-guide/
  - **BACKING FOUND** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - Also a duplicate of an already-backed corpus finding (researchId eeb67c07050d in the urgent-repairs trace). Section 72 carries two separate seven-day periods and Crown's 'demand reimbursement within seven days' blurs which one it means. Section 72(2)(a) puts a seven-day written-notice obligation on the renter, who must give the provider notice of the repairs carried out and their cost; s 72(2)(c) then requires the provider to reimburse the reasonable cost 'within 7 days after receiving written notice' of the repairs and the cost. So the deadline that bites the rental provider - the audience of Crown's guide - runs from receipt of the renter's notice, not from the date of the repair. CAV states the provider-facing limb the same way. Both figures are seven days, so the sentence is not wrong, merely imprecise about whose clock it is.
    - source: "The rental provider must pay them back within 7 days."
      Consumer Affairs Victoria https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/repairs-alterations-safety-and-pets/repairs/repairs-in-rental-properties

    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf (s 72(2)(a) and s 72(2)(c))
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/library/publications/housing-and-accommodation/renting/guideline-4--urgent-repairs.docx

## rent arrears (4)

- **[echoed-claim]** AuCore Elite asserts that Victorian landlords may issue a Notice to Vacate once rental arrears exceed 14 days.
  - as of: 2026-06-10
  - quote: "Landlords may issue a Notice to Vacate once rental arrears exceed 14 days."
  - source: https://www.aucoreelite.com/en/post/tenant-not-paying-rent-victoria
  - **BACKING FOUND** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - Backed by the regulator and by the Act. Residential Tenancies Act 1997 s 91ZM(7) defines the trigger: 'occasion where the renter owes at least' 14 days rent within a 12 month period (Authorised Version No. 113, https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf). Two precision points the retelling loses: (a) the statutory test is at least 14 days' rent owing, not arrears that 'exceed' 14 days, and it is measured as rent owed within a 12-month period, not elapsed days; (b) a separate 14-day figure applies to the notice itself - s 91ZM(5) requires the notice to specify a termination date not less than 14 days after it is given. The rooming-house/caravan-park variant (7 days) is absent from the retelling.
    - source: "If renters are more than 14 days late on rent (7 days for rooming house or caravan park tenants), the rental provider can give them a notice to vacate."
      Consumer Affairs Victoria https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/during-a-lease-or-residency/rent-and-other-payments/rent-arrears
    - searched: corpus scan: grep of all 29 trace-\*.json claim files for citations to consumer.vic.gov.au / legislation.vic.gov.au / vcat.vic.gov.au - the corpus contains no primary-source citation for any rent-arrears claim, so this is not a corpus duplicate
    - searched: https://www.aucoreelite.com/en/post/tenant-not-paying-rent-victoria (the retelling itself - it cites no source; the page says only 'Under Victorian tenancy legislation' with no section reference and no outbound link)
    - searched: WebSearch: Consumer Affairs Victoria notice to vacate non-payment of rent 14 days renter (site:consumer.vic.gov.au)
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/during-a-lease-or-residency/rent-and-other-payments/rent-arrears
    - searched: https://www.legislation.vic.gov.au/in-force/acts/residential-tenancies-act-1997 -> version 113 -> https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf (downloaded, pdftotext, read s 91ZM in full)
- **[echoed-claim]** AuCore Elite asserts that if a Victorian tenant pays all outstanding rent before the matter progresses the eviction process may be stopped.
  - as of: 2026-06-10
  - quote: "If the tenant pays all outstanding rent before the matter progresses, the eviction process may be stopped."
  - source: https://www.aucoreelite.com/en/post/tenant-not-paying-rent-victoria
  - **BACKING FOUND** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - Backed, and the hedged 'may be stopped' is the correct hedge. RTA 1997 s 91ZM(1)(b) says that on the first four occasions of non-payment, if the renter pays the unpaid rent on or before the termination date, 'paragraph (a), the notice is of no effect; and'. But s 91ZM(2)(b) carves out the fifth occasion in a 12-month period: the notice 'remains in effect despite the renter paying the unpaid rent on or before the termination date in the notice'. CAV states the same carve-out on the page cited. The retelling's 'before the matter progresses' is vaguer than the statutory test, which is the termination date stated on the notice, not the hearing date.
    - source: "When a renter is given a notice to vacate because they have not paid rent, they can pay the rent owing by the vacate date stated on the notice to avoid eviction."
      Consumer Affairs Victoria https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/during-a-lease-or-residency/rent-and-other-payments/rent-arrears
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/during-a-lease-or-residency/rent-and-other-payments/rent-arrears
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf - s 91ZM(1)(b), s 91ZM(2)(b), s 91ZM(3) read in full from the authorised PDF
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/moving-out-giving-notice-and-evictions/evictions-and-immediate-notice/evictions-and-possession-orders
    - searched: https://www.aucoreelite.com/en/post/tenant-not-paying-rent-victoria (retelling; no citation given)
- **[echoed-claim]** AuCore Elite asserts that rental arrears remain one of the most common challenges faced by Victorian landlords.
  - as of: 2026-06-10
  - quote: "Rental arrears remain one of the most common challenges faced by Victorian landlords."
  - source: https://www.aucoreelite.com/en/post/tenant-not-paying-rent-victoria
  - **SEARCHED, NOTHING FOUND** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - Genuinely unbacked marketing throat-clearing rather than a sourced finding. No number, no period, no population, no sample - 'one of the most common' is unfalsifiable as written, and the page attributes it to no one. The two Victorian bodies that would hold the underlying data publish nothing that ranks it: VCAT's annual report gives Residential Tenancies List caseload but no breakdown by ground, and the DFFH/RTBA Rental Report is a rents series. The RTBA does not publish arrears incidence at all. Note that arrears being a frequent VCAT application ground would still be a different proposition from arrears being a common problem for landlords generally.
    - searched: https://www.aucoreelite.com/en/post/tenant-not-paying-rent-victoria - the page asserts this in its opening section and cites nothing; there is no study, survey, dataset or year attached to it anywhere on the page
    - searched: WebSearch: VCAT annual report residential tenancies list applications possession non-payment of rent most common 2024-25 (site:vcat.vic.gov.au)
    - searched: https://www.vcat.vic.gov.au/media/517/download (VCAT Annual Report 2023-24, accessible version - downloaded and text-extracted; searched for 'possession', 'unpaid rent', 'arrears': zero matches. The report discusses Residential Tenancies List volumes and the backlog recovery program but publishes no breakdown of applications by ground, so it cannot rank arrears against other landlord problems)
    - searched: https://www.vcat.vic.gov.au/about-vcat/annual-reports-and-strategic-directions
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/during-a-lease-or-residency/rent-and-other-payments/rent-arrears (regulator page on arrears - states the rules, publishes no incidence data)
    - searched: https://www.dffh.vic.gov.au/publications/rental-report (RTBA bond-derived Victorian Rental Report - median rents and new lettings only; no arrears or landlord-problem series)
- **[echoed-claim]** AuCore Elite asserts that attempting to evict a Victorian tenant without following the correct legal process may result in significant penalties.
  - as of: 2026-06-10
  - quote: "Attempting to evict a tenant without following the correct legal process may result in significant penalties."
  - source: https://www.aucoreelite.com/en/post/tenant-not-paying-rent-victoria
  - **BACKING FOUND** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - Backed, and the primary source is more specific than the retelling. RTA 1997 s 91P(1) makes it an offence for a rental provider, except in accordance with the Act, to 'require or compel or attempt to compel' a renter to vacate; s 91P(2) does the same for obtaining possession by entry. The quoted penalty line is s 91P(1)'s: 150 penalty units for a natural person, and the following lines read '750 penalty units in the case of a body corporate'. On the Victorian penalty unit value that is tens of thousands of dollars, so 'significant' is fair. Consumer Affairs Victoria states the process side on its evictions page: 'Only the police can evict a renter and only when they have a warrant of possession from VCAT.' (https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/moving-out-giving-notice-and-evictions/evictions-and-immediate-notice/evictions-and-possession-orders, last updated 8 July 2026). Note the offence attaches to attempting the eviction, not only to succeeding.
    - source: "Penalty: 150 penalty units in the case"
      Victorian Government - Chief Parliamentary Counsel (Residential Tenancies Act 1997, Authorised Version No. 113, incorporating amendments as at 1 July 2026) https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf
    - searched: https://www.aucoreelite.com/en/post/tenant-not-paying-rent-victoria (retelling; says 'significant penalties' with no figure and no section reference)
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf - searched the authorised PDF for the offence provision; found s 91P 'Offence to obtain possession etc. of premises'
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/moving-out-giving-notice-and-evictions/evictions-and-immediate-notice/evictions-and-possession-orders
