## vcat application (4)

- **[echoed-claim]** Landlord Wise states renters can challenge no-fault notices at VCAT within 30 days and that breach-based notices can be challenged at the possession order hearing.
  - as of: 2026-04-06
  - quote: "Renters can challenge no-fault notices at VCAT within 30 days — breach-based notices can be challenged at the possession order hearing"
  - source: https://landlordwise.com.au/guides/notice-to-vacate-vic/
  - **BACKING FOUND** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - Right, with an edge the retelling rounds off. s 91ZZS(1) of the authorised Act gives the challenge right only for notices to vacate given under ss 91ZX (repairs), 91ZY (demolition), 91ZZ (premises to be used for business), 91ZZA (occupation by the rental provider or family), 91ZZB (sale) and 91ZZC (public purposes) - the six grounds CAV lists, all of which now require 90 days notice (s 91ZZC(2) was amended to 90 days by No. 6/2025). VCAT states the same limit on its own dispute-types page and spells it out: 'This application allows a renter to challenge the validity of 90 day notices only.' So 'no-fault' is slightly wider than the statutory route: notices under ss 91ZZE and 91ZZEA (renter no longer meets eligibility or NRAS/affordable-housing criteria) and s 91ZZF (transitional housing) are no-fault too but are not in the s 91ZZS list. Two further details worth having: s 91ZZS(1) also requires the application to be made 'on or before the hearing of an application for a possession order', so 30 days is a ceiling and not a floor; and under s 91ZZT(2), once VCAT has determined the notice is valid the renter cannot challenge it again absent exceptional circumstances. CAV also warns that an unsuccessful challenger 'may have to pay the rental provider's VCAT application fee'.
    - source: "An application under subsection (1) must be made within 30 days after the notice to vacate is given."
      Victorian Government (Chief Parliamentary Counsel), Residential Tenancies Act 1997 s 91ZZS(2) https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf
    - searched: https://landlordwise.com.au/guides/notice-to-vacate-vic/ (the retelling)
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf (authorised Residential Tenancies Act 1997, version 113, incorporating amendments as at 1 July 2026 - downloaded and md5-verified against the live file; ss 91ZZR, 91ZZS, 91ZZT, 91ZZU read in both pdftotext and pdftotext -layout extractions, and the backing quote confirmed contiguous in both)
    - searched: https://www.vcat.vic.gov.au/case-types/residential-tenancies/dispute-types - VCAT's own table of residential tenancies dispute types: row 'Challenge the validity of a notice to vacate', section 91ZZS, 'This application allows a renter to challenge the validity of 90 day notices only.', time limit 'Application must be made within 30 days after notice to vacate is given'
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/moving-out-giving-notice-and-evictions/notice-to-vacate/challenging-a-notice-to-vacate (CAV, last updated 8 February 2023): 'The renter or resident must apply to VCAT within 30 days of getting a notice to vacate if the property is:' followed by the six no-fault grounds - being repaired, being demolished, going to be used for business, going to be used by the rental provider or someone in their family, going to be sold, owned by a government authority who needs it for public use
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/moving-out-giving-notice-and-evictions/notice-to-vacate/notice-to-vacate-in-rental-properties (CAV, last updated 26 February 2026 - the notice-period tables, checked to confirm which grounds carry 90 days)
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/sitemap.xml and https://www.vcat.vic.gov.au/sitemap.xml (enumerated to find the regulator and tribunal pages above rather than guessing URLs)
- **[echoed-claim]** Landlord Wise states only Victoria Police can carry out a forcible eviction, acting on a VCAT warrant.
  - as of: 2026-04-06
  - quote: "Only Victoria Police can carry out a forcible eviction, acting on a VCAT warrant."
  - source: https://landlordwise.com.au/guides/notice-to-vacate-vic/
  - **BACKING FOUND** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - The regulator says it in exactly these words, so the claim is backed - but it is a restatement of CAV's sentence rather than of the Act, and the Act is slightly wider. s 355(1)(b) provides that a warrant of possession must be directed '(i) to a police officer; or (ii) to an authorised person', and s 333(2) requires the possession order to warn the renter that they 'may be forcibly vacated... by a police officer or an authorised person carrying out a warrant of possession'. Section 355(2)(a) is what supplies the force: the warrant authorises the person to whom it is directed 'to enter the rented premises, room and rooming house, building, site or caravan (as the case may be), by force if necessary'. There is also a separate regime in Division 5 of Part 7 under which the sheriff, not police, removes a caravan and the goods in it. In ordinary rented-premises practice warrants are directed to Victoria Police and CAV's simplification holds, but a writer relying on 'only Victoria Police' as a statement of law would be overstating it. The rest of the claim - that force requires a VCAT warrant, not merely a possession order - is exactly right and is the part that matters: CAV on the same page states that rental providers 'cannot lock out renters or personally carry out an eviction'.
    - source: "Only Victoria Police can carry out a forcible eviction and only when they are acting on a warrant of possession granted by VCAT."
      Consumer Affairs Victoria https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/moving-out-giving-notice-and-evictions/evictions-and-immediate-notice/evictions-and-possession-orders
    - searched: https://landlordwise.com.au/guides/notice-to-vacate-vic/ (the retelling - its sentence is a near-verbatim lift of the regulator's own wording)
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/moving-out-giving-notice-and-evictions/evictions-and-immediate-notice/evictions-and-possession-orders (CAV, last updated 8 July 2026 - the backing page; the same page also says 'Rental providers cannot lock out renters or personally carry out an eviction')
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf - ss 333(2), 351, 355 and 360 of the authorised Act read in both pdftotext and pdftotext -layout extractions
    - searched: https://www.vcat.vic.gov.au/sitemap.xml, and the tribunal's warrant forms at https://www.vcat.vic.gov.au/documents/request-rental-provider-warrant-possession and .../request-rental-provider-duplicate-warrant-possession (checked who a warrant is directed to in practice)
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/sitemap.xml (enumerated to locate the evictions page rather than guessing the URL)
- **[echoed-claim]** Aussie Rent Laws states most tenancy disputes are resolved before a tribunal hearing through negotiation or mediation.
  - as of: 2026-03-09
  - quote: "Most tenancy disputes are resolved before a tribunal hearing through negotiation or mediation."
  - source: https://aussierentlaws.com/victoria/renter-notice-intention-to-vacate
  - **NOT YET CHECKED.** Nobody has gone looking for a source for this one, so it says nothing either way.
- **[echoed-claim]** Collings Real Estate states a renter who pays an unlawfully imposed rent increase can apply to VCAT to recover the overpaid amount.
  - as of: 2026-06-26
  - quote: "If a renter pays a rent increase that was unlawfully imposed, they can apply to VCAT to recover the overpaid amount."
  - source: https://www.collings.com.au/rent-increases-in-victoria-rules-and-limits/
  - **BACKING FOUND** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - Backed, and s 210 is the precise mechanism the retelling gestures at without naming. s 44(5) makes a rent increase given in contravention of s 44 invalid, so the old rent remained the amount payable and anything paid on top is money 'paid to the other party more than the applicant is required to pay'. s 210(1) lets a party to a residential rental agreement apply to VCAT 'for an order for payment to the applicant by the other party... of compensation', and s 472(1)(f) confirms VCAT's power 'to require the payment of compensation to any person'. Three qualifications the retelling omits. s 210(2)(b) switches the section off if the rental provider has given the renter a notice to vacate under s 91ZM, which is exactly the situation a renter in dispute over rent may find themselves in. s 447(1)(b) caps a compensation claim at $40,000, rising to $100,000 for the site agreement provisions or a Part 4A dwelling purchase agreement. And since June 2025 the practical first step is not VCAT: CAV directs renters to Rental Dispute Resolution Victoria, a free VCAT service, and says 'If the case isn't resolved at RDRV, they can help you go to VCAT'. Note also that this is a different question from the excessive-rent route in ss 45-48, where the remedy is a maximum rent set by VCAT (with a refund available under s 48 only if an order is made under s 47) - the two are easy to conflate and the Collings page does not distinguish them.
    - source: "the applicant has paid to the other party more than the applicant is required to pay in accordance with this Act"
      Victorian Government (Chief Parliamentary Counsel), Residential Tenancies Act 1997 s 210(1)(b) https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf
    - searched: https://www.collings.com.au/rent-increases-in-victoria-rules-and-limits/ (the retelling)
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf (authorised Act version 113, as at 1 July 2026): s 44(1)-(5) on rent increase notice, fixed-term and 12-month rules, s 44(5) 'A rent increase in contravention of this section is invalid.', s 210(1)(b) the recovery ground quoted as backing, s 447(1)(b)-(c) the monetary limits, s 452(1) and s 472(1)(f). The backing quote was checked for contiguity under both pdftotext and pdftotext -layout - the full sentence of s 210(1)(b) is not contiguous under -layout because the marginal amendment note 'No. 45/2018 s. 230(1).' interleaves, so the quote is trimmed to the span that survives both extractions
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/rent-bond-bills-and-condition-reports/rent/rent-increases (CAV, checked: it says 'If the rent increase rules are not followed the increase may be invalid' and 'If you are a renter and you think the rent increase is invalid you can request a review', but it does not describe recovering money already paid)
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/rent-bond-bills-and-condition-reports/rent/challenging-rent-increases-or-high-rent (CAV, last updated 30 March 2026 - this page is about excessive rent under ss 45-48, a different question from an unlawfully imposed increase, and its remedy is a maximum rent set by VCAT rather than a refund)
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/legal-and-dispute-support/resolving-disputes (CAV - the RDRV-first pathway)
    - searched: https://www.vcat.vic.gov.au/case-types/residential-tenancies/dispute-types (VCAT's dispute-type table - no dedicated 'recover overpaid rent' row; the routes offered are 'Compliance or compensation' under s 209 and 'General dispute' under s 452(1))
    - searched: https://www.vcat.vic.gov.au/media/195/download (VCAT's 'Useful Supreme Court and VCAT decisions about renting' compendium - its rent-increase case summaries are all about excessive rent under s 45, none about recovering an invalid increase)

## domestic violence provisions (3)

- **[echoed-claim]** EBM RentCover states that renters are responsible for damage to a Victorian rental property caused by family and domestic violence.
  - as of: 2025-05-05
  - quote: "Renters are responsible for damage to the property caused by FDV. Your impacted tenant can apply to VCAT to determine who is responsible for paying for any damage to the property."
  - source: https://www.rentcover.com.au/insurance-insights/domestic-violence-and-tenancy-law/
  - **BACKING FOUND** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - Backed, but the retelling states the default rule without the statutory relief that sits next to it. The regulator's own page continues: 'If your name is on the rental agreement, you are legally responsible for damage that a renter or a visitor causes' (default liability, which flows from s 61 of the Residential Tenancies Act 1997 - renters and visitors must not intentionally or negligently damage the premises), and then 'VCAT may order that you are not liable for any repairs or damage due to family violence' and 'VCAT may decide who should pay for the repairs'. In the Act the machinery is s 420B(2) (Tribunal may order that a victim of family violence 'is not liable for any loss or damage suffered by the residential rental provider' where the loss was caused by the alleged perpetrator and an intervention order is in force), s 420A(3)(a) (Tribunal may apportion liability and make the renter who committed the violence liable for all of the provider's loss and damage) and s 91X (on a s 91W termination/new-agreement order the Tribunal may determine liability 'relating to damage caused to the rented premises'). So RentCover's second sentence is exactly right; its first sentence is the starting point, not the end point, and reads as harsher than the law is.
    - source: "If the property you rent is damaged because you experienced family violence, you may still be responsible for fixing the damage"
      Consumer Affairs Victoria https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/family-violence-when-renting/repairs
    - searched: https://www.rentcover.com.au/insurance-insights/domestic-violence-and-tenancy-law/ (opened the retelling; its only Victorian outbound citation is https://www.legislation.vic.gov.au/in-force/acts/residential-tenancies-act-1997/090 - it points at the Act but does not cite a section for the damage statement)
    - searched: web search: Residential Tenancies Act 1997 Victoria section 91V liability damage family violence VCAT
    - searched: web search (site:consumer.vic.gov.au): Consumer Affairs Victoria family violence renting rental agreement VCAT damage liability
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/family-violence-when-renting/repairs (primary - CAV's own page on who pays for FV damage)
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/family-violence-when-renting/changing-or-ending-the-rental-agreement
    - searched: https://www.legislation.vic.gov.au/in-force/acts/residential-tenancies-act-1997/113 (current in-force version listing)
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf - read s 61 (renter and visitor must not damage premises), s 91V-91X (FV termination and Tribunal determination of liability for 'damage caused to the rented premises'), s 420A (apportionment of liability between renters where one is a victim of family violence) and s 420B (Tribunal may order the victim is not liable for loss or damage caused by the alleged perpetrator)
    - searched: https://www.vcat.vic.gov.au/case-types/residential-tenancies/apply-residential-tenancies/house-family-violence (VCAT's own application page, found via search)
- **[echoed-claim]** EBM RentCover states that the Victorian Parliament passed the Residential Tenancies Amendment Act 2018 in September 2018.
  - as of: 2025-05-05
  - quote: "The Victorian Parliament passed the Residential Tenancies Amendment Act 2018 in September 2018 which included reforms concerning family and domestic violence (FDV)."
  - source: https://www.rentcover.com.au/insurance-insights/domestic-violence-and-tenancy-law/
  - **BACKING FOUND** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - Backed from the Act itself. The as-made authorised PDF of the Residential Tenancies Amendment Act 2018 No. 45 of 2018 carries '[Assented to 18 September 2018]' on its cover, so Parliament had passed it by September 2018. The same PDF's purposes clause includes '(xii) to provide further protections in relation to family violence; and', and s 236 of that Act inserted the family-violence Subdivision into the Residential Tenancies Act 1997 (ss 91V-91Y, 'Termination and new residential rental agreements because of family violence or personal violence'); s 292 inserted the bond provisions ss 420A-420B. The marginal notes throughout the current authorised RTA 1997 confirm this ('S. 91V inserted by No. 45/2018 s. 236'). Worth flagging that the FV provisions did not commence in 2018 - the RTA 1997 endnote records ss 236-322 of No. 45/2018 commencing on 29 March 2021 - so 'passed in September 2018' is right but the reforms did not bite for another two and a half years.
    - source: "[Assented to 18 September 2018]"
      Victorian Government (Chief Parliamentary Counsel), legislation.vic.gov.au https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/bd1ef6a2-1113-36c3-9bbc-caed05c175cc_18-045aa%20authorised.pdf
    - searched: https://www.rentcover.com.au/insurance-insights/domestic-violence-and-tenancy-law/ (the retelling)
    - searched: https://www.legislation.vic.gov.au/as-made/acts/residential-tenancies-amendment-act-2018 (as-made landing page; HTML carries no assent date, only the authorised PDF link)
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/bd1ef6a2-1113-36c3-9bbc-caed05c175cc_18-045aa%20authorised.pdf (as-made authorised Act - cover page assent line, purposes clause, and Part 9 amendments)
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf endnotes - 'Residential Tenancies Amendment Act 2018, No. 45/2018 ... Assent Date: 18.9.18'
    - searched: web search: legislation.vic.gov.au Residential Tenancies Act 1997 authorised version PDF in force

## rental provider duties (2)

- **[echoed-claim]** Compton Green states that from 1 December 2025 all Victorian residential rental properties must comply with new blind and curtain cord safety standards.
  - as of: 2025-10-16
  - quote: "From 1 December 2025, all Victorian residential rental properties must comply with new blind and curtain cord safety standards."
  - source: https://www.comptongreen.com.au/post?post_id=17219
  - **BACKING FOUND** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - Backed, and the corpus already had it - this is our duplicate, not the field's invention. The law is Residential Tenancies Regulations 2021 Schedule 4 clause 15 'Corded internal window coverings', which opens 'On and from 1 December 2025, the standard for corded internal window coverings installed at the rented premises is that - (a) a loose cord that is part of the window covering cannot form a loop of 220 mm or longer that is less than 1600 mm above floor level, and is secured by either - (i) a cord guide... or (ii) a cleat that is installed against a wall or structure at least 1600 mm above floor level'. Regulation 29 makes Schedule 4 the 'rental minimum standards' for the purposes of s 65A of the Act. Two details Compton Green (and CAV's own banner) smooth over. First, the clause was inserted on 1 December 2024, by the Residential Tenancies and Residential Tenancies (Rooming House Standards) Amendment (Minimum Energy Efficiency and Safety Standards) Regulations 2024 (S.R. No. 130/2024) reg 6, with a deliberate 12-month lead-in written into the clause itself - so 1 December 2025 is the date the standard bites, not the date the rule was made, and it is not part of the 25 November 2025 Housing Statement Reform package that Compton Green files it under. Second, 'all Victorian residential rental properties' overstates it slightly: CAV's own applicability section says the Schedule 4 minimum standards 'don't apply if your rental agreement became a periodic agreement (month to month) before 29 March 2021'. CAV writes 'all rental properties' in its headline too, so the overstatement originates with the regulator rather than with the agency. Also worth recording: CAV's page sends readers to the wrong statutory rule for the detail (see searched).
    - source: "On and from 1 December 2025, the standard for"
      Victorian Government (Chief Parliamentary Counsel), Residential Tenancies Regulations 2021 version 009, Schedule 4 clause 15 https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-06/21-3sra009-authorised.pdf
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/repairs-alterations-safety-and-pets/minimum-standards/minimum-standards-for-rental-properties - CAV's own page; banner reads 'From 1 December 2025, all rental properties must have secured cords on internal window coverings to prevent them forming loops.' and the 'Window covering anchors' standard entry reads 'From 1 December 2025, all corded internal window coverings, such as blinds and curtains, must have an anchor installed to secure the cords and prevent them forming loops.'
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/new-changes-to-the-rental-laws - CAV's reform timetable, 1 December 2025 row: 'New rental minimum standard - blind cord safety anchors / All rental properties must have secured cords on internal window coverings to prevent them forming loops.'
    - searched: https://www.legislation.vic.gov.au/in-force/statutory-rules/residential-tenancies-regulations-2021 - version list; the current in-force version is 009, effective 30 June 2026
    - searched: https://www.legislation.vic.gov.au/in-force/statutory-rules/residential-tenancies-regulations-2021/009 - located the authorised PDF
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-06/21-3sra009-authorised.pdf - Residential Tenancies Regulations 2021 version 009; read Schedule 4 (Rental minimum standards) clause 15 'Corded internal window coverings', and regulation 29 which prescribes Schedule 4 as the rental minimum standards for the purposes of section 65A of the Act; also read the endnote table, which dates clause 15 to S.R. No. 130/2024 reg. 6
    - searched: https://www.legislation.vic.gov.au/as-made/statutory-rules/residential-tenancies-amendment-housing-statement-reform-and-part-4a-site-agreements-regulations then https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2025-11/25-123sra-authorised.pdf - this is the statutory rule CAV's own page points readers to 'for further detail on the window covering anchors requirements'. I read it end to end: it contains no reference to cords, blinds or window coverings at all. CAV's pointer is misdirected; the anchor standard actually sits in S.R. No. 130/2024.
    - searched: https://www.comptongreen.com.au/post?post_id=17219 - opened the retelling itself to see what it pointed at; it cites nothing, it just asserts the rule and then sells a blind-cord audit service
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf - Residential Tenancies Act 1997 version 113, ss 65A and 65B, to check who the standard binds and when
- **[echoed-claim]** Compton Green tells renters that safety upgrades are considered an urgent repair for its property management team to action.
  - as of: 2025-10-16
  - quote: "If you notice loose or hanging cords, let your property manager know immediately. Safety upgrades are considered an urgent repair for our property management team to action."
  - source: https://www.comptongreen.com.au/post?post_id=17219
  - **BACKING FOUND** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - Backed by the Act's own definition. Section 3(1) defines 'urgent repairs' as 'any work necessary to repair or remedy' a list of things, and paragraph (ib) of that list is 'a failure to comply with any rental minimum standards'. Paragraph (ic) independently covers 'a failure or breakdown of any safety-related devices'. Because Schedule 4 clause 15 of the Residential Tenancies Regulations 2021 is a rental minimum standard (prescribed by reg 29 for the purposes of s 65A), work to secure a loose blind or curtain cord falls inside paragraph (ib) as a matter of definition - it is not a discretionary agency policy. Section 65A(2) reinforces it: 'If rented premises do not comply with the rental minimum standards on or immediately after the day on which the renter enters into occupation of the premises, the renter may issue a request to the residential rental provider for urgent repairs to be carried out to the premises to ensure that the premises comply with the standards.' One honest caveat: the sentence traced is partly a first-party statement about Compton Green's own internal workflow ('for our property management team to action'), and no external source can confirm or deny what one agency's team does. The checkable half - that a minimum-standards failure is legally an urgent repair - is what the Act settles, and it settles it in Compton Green's favour. Note also that the urgent-repair classification carries the s 72 self-help route and the s 73 two-business-day VCAT hearing, which is a stronger consequence than 'let your property manager know' implies.
    - source: "a failure to comply with any rental minimum standards; or"
      Victorian Government (Chief Parliamentary Counsel), Residential Tenancies Act 1997 version 113, s 3(1) definition of 'urgent repairs' https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf - Residential Tenancies Act 1997 version 113; read the s 3(1) definition of 'urgent repairs' in full (paragraphs (a) to (m)), then ss 65A, 68, 72, 73
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-06/21-3sra009-authorised.pdf - Residential Tenancies Regulations 2021 v009, reg 29 and Schedule 4 cl 15, to confirm the corded window covering rule is a 'rental minimum standard' and not some free-standing safety rule
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/repairs-alterations-safety-and-pets/minimum-standards/minimum-standards-for-rental-properties - CAV's 'Sections of the Act' box lists 'Section 65A - Occupation of rented premises that do not comply with rental minimum standards' and 'Section 72 - Urgent repairs' as the operative provisions for minimum standards, which is the same link Compton Green is making
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/repairs-alterations-safety-and-pets/repairs/repairs-in-rental-properties - CAV's repairs page, to see whether CAV itself categorises minimum-standards work as urgent
    - searched: https://www.comptongreen.com.au/post?post_id=17219 - the retelling; it cites no source for the urgent-repair characterisation

## right of entry (2)

- **[echoed-claim]** aussierentlaws.com states that the right of entry normally requires at least 24 hours written notice for a routine inspection in Victoria.
  - as of: 2026-03-09
  - quote: "At least 24 hours written notice is normally required for a routine inspection."
  - source: https://aussierentlaws.com/victoria/routine-inspections-how-often-landlords-enter-vic
  - **A PRIMARY SOURCE SAYS THE OPPOSITE** (checked 2026-08-15)
    - The primary source says the opposite number. The routine inspection ground is s 86(1)(f) - 'entry is required to enable inspection of the premises and entry for that purpose has not been made within the last 6 months'. The notice period attached to that ground is set by s 85(b)(iv), quoted above: SEVEN days, not 24 hours. Twenty-four hours is the s 85(b)(iii) period, and it attaches to different grounds entirely - s 86(1)(c) (carrying out a duty under the Act or the agreement), (e) (reasonable grounds to believe the renter has breached their duties) and (g) (inspection for family violence proceedings under s 91V(1)). Consumer Affairs Victoria's own notice table says the same thing: 'General inspection (routine inspection)' = 7 days. Tenants Victoria says the same. So does the corpus. On the chain: aussierentlaws.com carries no pinpoint citation at all. Its only references are two bare links, '[1] Residential Tenancies Act 1997 (Vic) - Victorian legislation' and '[2] Consumer Affairs Victoria - renting and inspections', and it tells the reader to 'check the Residential Tenancies Act and Consumer Affairs Victoria guidance for details' - while stating a figure that both of those sources contradict. The most likely explanation is staleness rather than invention: 24 hours WAS the general-inspection notice period under the pre-reform Act, and the 7-day period was introduced by the Residential Tenancies Amendment Act 2018 (No. 45/2018) s 74(c), commencing 29 March 2021. The page is repeating a rule that expired five years ago. The hedge word 'normally' does no work - there is no circumstance in which 24 hours suffices for a routine inspection.
    - source: "for a purpose set out in section 86(1)(d) or (f), if at least 7 days notice has been given to the renter in accordance with section 88."
      Victorian Government (Chief Parliamentary Counsel), Residential Tenancies Act 1997 version 113 https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf
    - searched: WebSearch: Consumer Affairs Victoria renter entry to the property 24 hours notice inspection (site:consumer.vic.gov.au)
    - searched: WebSearch: Residential Tenancies Act 1997 Vic section 86 notice of entry 24 hours
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/housing/renting/rental-providers-inspecting-or-entering-a-property/when-a-rental-provider-can-enter-a-property - CAV's notice table gives 'General inspection (routine inspection)' = 7 days, and 24 hours only for repairs/legal duties, suspected breach of renter duties, and family violence proceedings
    - searched: https://www.legislation.vic.gov.au/in-force/acts/residential-tenancies-act-1997 - then /113 for the current authorised version
    - searched: https://content.legislation.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/2026-07/97-109aa113-authorised.pdf - read Division 8 Rights of entry, ss 85, 86, 88
    - searched: https://www.consumer.vic.gov.au/library/publications/housing-and-accommodation/renting/renters-guide.pdf - CAV Renters Guide; its entry section gives 8am-6pm and the 7-day agreed-time rule but does not state the general-inspection notice period, so not usable as backing
    - searched: https://www5.austlii.edu.au/au/legis/vic/consol_act/rta1997207/s86.html - HTTP 403
    - searched: https://aussierentlaws.com/victoria/routine-inspections-how-often-landlords-enter-vic - opened the retelling itself to trace its chain

  - as of: 2026-03-09
